\begin{abstract}
This document describes the mathematical foundations of {\bf Owl} - Optimal wealth lab, a Python application that optimizes US retirement planning using mixed-integer linear programming. Owl models federal tax laws, Medicare and ACA marketplace premiums, Social Security rules, pension income, Roth conversions, Health Savings Accounts, and account dynamics jointly within a single optimization framework. The self-consistent iteration loop and MIP reformulations handle nonlinear tax interactions. Chapters~1--3 present the conceptual model for a general audience. Chapters~4--8 provide the formal constraint structure, the objective functions, the rate models, and the two outer searches --- stochastic spending and Social Security claiming ages. Five appendices hold the implementation reference: the notation, the variable mapping, the construction of the constraint matrices, the solver options, and a guide to the source files.
\end{abstract}

\chapter{Introduction}
\label{Chap:Intro}
This document describes the mathematical model underlying
the optimization algorithms implemented in
Owl, which is a Python application optimizing retirement
planning using mixed-integer linear programming.
Owl is designed for US retirees as it considers US federal tax laws, Medicare premiums,
rules for 401k including required minimum distributions, maturation rules
for Roth accounts and conversions, Social Security rules, etc.
The goal of
these calculations is to optimize the financial aspects
of retirement planning, considering the types of savings accounts,
federal income tax, contributions, return rates, Medicare premiums, Roth conversions,
and desired income among many other things.

The approach is described here mathematically and the Python implementation
follows the structure and notation presented in this document.
The intent of this document is to provide a guide to the source code
for any individual desiring to extend the model to other cases.
The mathematical description is not tied to any specific mathematical
programming language so that it remains as generic as possible and
can be solved using different linear solvers.
The source code, installation instructions, and usage examples are
available on the project's GitHub repository~\cite{owlgithub}.

This document is intended for two audiences.
Chapters~\ref{Chap:Intro} through~\ref{Chap:Architecture} require only basic
financial literacy: familiarity with retirement account types, tax-deferred
vs.\ tax-free savings, Social Security, and Medicare is sufficient to follow the
discussion.
Chapters~\ref{Chap:Formulation} through~\ref{Chap:SSAges} require mathematical
maturity: comfort with linear algebra, index notation, and the basics of linear
and mixed-integer programming is assumed, and
Chapter~\ref{Chap:RateModels} additionally assumes familiarity with probability
distributions and time-series models.
Readers interested only in the conceptual model may stop after
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Architecture}; readers seeking to extend or verify the
implementation will need the later chapters and the appendices.

The remainder of this document is organized as follows.
Chapter~\ref{Chap:TaxRules} surveys the US retirement tax rules that Owl
models---Social Security benefits and the earnings test, pension income, Roth
account rules, Health Savings Accounts, ACA marketplace premiums, Medicare
premiums, capital gains tax, the Net Investment Income Tax, and federal and state
ordinary income tax---with notes on how each is implemented and where known
limitations apply.
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Architecture} describes the overall model architecture: how
account balances evolve over time, how asset allocation glide paths are
specified, how spending objectives are formulated, and how the self-consistent
iteration loop and the mixed-integer reformulations resolve the nonlinear tax
interactions that arise within a linear programming framework.
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Formulation} presents the formal constraint structure, and
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Objectives} defines the objective functions.
Chapter~\ref{Chap:RateModels} describes the rate models available for generating
annual return sequences, from simple fixed-rate assumptions to fully stochastic
simulations.
The last three chapters cover the outer searches that solve the plan repeatedly
rather than once: Chapter~\ref{chap:stochastic-spending} commits to a spending
level across many return scenarios and samples longevity,
Chapter~\ref{Chap:SpendingBequest} traces how much spending each dollar of bequest
costs, and Chapter~\ref{Chap:SSAges} chooses Social Security claiming ages.

Five appendices hold the implementation reference.
Appendix~\ref{Chap:Notation} defines every index, variable, and parameter used in
the formulation, and is the notation key for
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Formulation} onwards.
Appendix~\ref{Chap:VarMap} gives the mapping of decision variables into the
solver's one-dimensional array, and Appendix~\ref{Chap:Matrices} the construction
of the constraint matrices, including the constraint tags that drive the
explanation engine.
Appendix~\ref{Chap:Options} documents every solver option, with its default and
the options that have been retired or removed, and
Appendix~\ref{Chap:SourceRef} is a reference guide to the Python source files.

\chapter{US Retirement Tax Rules and Their Implementation in Owl}
\label{Chap:TaxRules}

\section{Overview}

Retirement income in the United States comes from four broad account types:
taxable brokerage accounts, tax-deferred accounts such as traditional 401(k)s
and IRAs, tax-free Roth accounts, and Health Savings Accounts (HSAs).
Withdrawals from these accounts interact with multiple simultaneously computed taxes
and premiums: ordinary income tax (with bracket stacking), long-term capital gains
(LTCG) tax, the Net Investment Income Tax (NIIT), ACA Premium Tax Credit, Medicare Part~B premium surcharges,
and the taxability fraction of Social Security (SS) benefits.
Because all these quantities depend on the same underlying income variables,
optimizing any one strategy in isolation---such as Roth conversion timing or
Social Security claiming age---can inadvertently worsen another.

Owl models all of these rules \emph{jointly} within a single linear program
(LP) or mixed-integer program (MIP) so that strategies such as Roth
conversions, SS claiming age, and withdrawal order are optimized together
rather than sequentially.
Many rules are nonlinear (piecewise, min/max); Owl handles them within a linear
framework using a self-consistent (SC) iteration loop for quantities that can
be lagged by one iteration, and MIP reformulations for quantities that require
exact simultaneous computation.

\section{Savings Accounts}

US retirement savings are held in four fundamentally different account types,
each with its own tax treatment, contribution rules, and withdrawal constraints.
Owl models all four types and optimizes across them jointly.
For married couples, each spouse holds their own independent set of accounts;
balances, contribution histories, claiming ages, and Required Minimum Distribution
(RMD) schedules are tracked
separately for each individual while the optimizer considers the combined
household portfolio.

\subsection*{Taxable brokerage accounts}

Taxable accounts hold securities purchased with after-tax dollars.
Dividends and interest are taxed as ordinary income (or at preferential
qualified-dividend rates) in the year received, regardless of whether the
cash is withdrawn.
Capital gains are recognized and taxed only when securities are sold.
There is no tax deduction for contributions, no contribution limit from a
federal tax standpoint, and no RMD requirement.

Owl models the taxable account as generating a dividend and interest yield
each year proportional to the balance.
Capital gains arise when the optimizer elects to sell holdings; all realized
gains are treated as long-term (see the limitation noted in
Section~\ref{sec:ltcg}).
When an initial cost basis is provided via \code{setCostBasis()}, the
unrealized gain fraction is computed from the actual basis and tracked
throughout the plan via a pro-rata update rule
(see Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:Qx3})--(\ref{Eq:BasisUpdate}));
this reduces the taxable gain recognized on withdrawals in the early plan years.

\subsection*{Tax-deferred accounts (traditional IRA, 401(k), and 403(b))}

Tax-deferred accounts accept pre-tax contributions that reduce current taxable
income, subject to annual IRS limits.
Growth inside the account is not taxed until withdrawal.
Every dollar withdrawn is taxed as ordinary income in the year taken.
Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs) apply starting at age 70--75 depending
on the account owner's birth year (see Section~\ref{sec:ordincome}).
A 10\% early withdrawal penalty applies to withdrawals taken before
age~59\textonehalf, subject to IRS exceptions not modeled here.

Owl supports contributions to traditional 401(k) and IRA accounts as entered
in the Wages and Contributions table described in Section~\ref{sec:cfhfp}.
The optimizer determines the optimal annual withdrawal amount from these
accounts subject to RMD floors and other constraints.
The pro-rata rule (IRS Form~8606) is not modeled: Owl treats the entire
tax-deferred balance as pre-tax, so every dollar converted or withdrawn is
fully taxable (see the limitation in Section~\ref{sec:roth}).

\subsection*{Tax-free Roth accounts (Roth IRA and Roth~401(k))}

Roth accounts accept after-tax contributions.
Qualified withdrawals of both principal and earnings are entirely tax- and
penalty-free.
Roth accounts are not subject to RMDs during the original owner's lifetime.
Roth conversions---transfers from a tax-deferred account to a Roth
account---are fully taxable as ordinary income in the year of conversion but
produce tax-free growth thereafter.
The detailed five-year seasoning and age-59\textonehalf{} rules governing
Roth withdrawals and conversions are described in Section~\ref{sec:roth}.

Owl supports contributions to Roth~401(k) and Roth~IRA accounts, and
optimizes Roth conversion amounts annually subject to user-specified
constraints and tax-bracket considerations.

\subsection*{Health Savings Accounts (HSA)}

HSAs are the only triple tax-advantaged savings vehicle in the US tax code:
contributions are pre-tax (reducing ordinary income and MAGI), growth is
tax-free, and qualified medical withdrawals are entirely tax-free at any age.
After age~65 (Medicare enrollment), non-qualified withdrawals are taxed as
ordinary income, effectively making the HSA a second traditional IRA without
Required Minimum Distributions.
A surviving spouse inherits the account intact, preserving its full
tax-advantaged status.
Medicare Parts~B and~D premiums, IRMAA surcharges, Medigap premiums,
and out-of-pocket medical costs all qualify as HSA withdrawals, so these
expenses can be funded entirely tax-free from the account.
Annual contribution limits are set by the IRS (approximately \$4,400 for
self-only and \$8,750 for family coverage in 2026) and must cease at Medicare
enrollment.
See Section~\ref{sec:hsa} for the detailed rules and implementation.

\section{Household Financial Profile and Cash Flow}
\label{sec:cfhfp}

Owl takes two primary inputs.
The first is the \textbf{case configuration file} (a \texttt{.toml} file),
which specifies all plan parameters: account balances, Social Security
claiming ages, spending objectives, rate assumptions, solver options, and so on.
The second is the \textbf{Household Financial Profile (HFP)}, an Excel
workbook that captures the year-by-year financial data for each individual
and the household as a whole.
The HFP consists of three tables: the \emph{Wages and Contributions} table
(one tab per individual), the \emph{Fixed Assets} table, and the
\emph{Debts} table.

\subsection*{Wages and Contributions table}

Each individual has their own sheet with the following year-indexed columns:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textit{year} --- calendar year;
  \item \textit{anticipated wages} --- earned income expected for that year,
    as it enters AGI: pay net of the pre-tax contributions (traditional 401(k)
    deferrals, deductible IRA and payroll HSA contributions; employer portions
    included in the \textit{ctrb} columns are not subtracted, as they were never
    part of wages). This single amount serves both as cash available to the plan
    and as taxable employment income, and the employee's payroll tax is charged on it
    (plus the year's tax-deferred contributions) as a cash outflow. An after-tax contribution paid from pay
    (Roth 401(k), Roth IRA, taxable) therefore stays in the wages, where it is
    taxed, and is paid for by a negative \textit{big-ticket items} entry of the
    same amount;
  \item \textit{months worked} --- months of work at the start of the year,
    from 0 to 12 (12 when absent in a year with wages), read by the earnings test
    (Section~\ref{sec:ss});
  \item \textit{other inc} --- other ordinary income not captured elsewhere
    (enter 0 if not applicable); a negative amount is an above-the-line deduction
    paid from cash, such as an HSA contribution made without wages;
  \item \textit{net inv} --- rent and trust distributions that count as net
    investment income for NIIT (enter 0 if not applicable);
  \item \textit{taxable ctrb} --- contributions to the taxable brokerage
    account;
  \item \textit{401k ctrb} --- traditional 401(k) contributions;
  \item \textit{Roth 401k ctrb} --- Roth 401(k) contributions;
  \item \textit{IRA ctrb} --- traditional IRA contributions;
  \item \textit{Roth IRA ctrb} --- Roth IRA contributions;
  \item \textit{HSA ctrb} --- Health Savings Account contributions (enter 0 if
    not applicable); the year of Medicare enrollment keeps the months before it,
    and later entries are ignored;
  \item \textit{Roth conv} --- planned or historical Roth conversion amounts;
    the five years preceding the plan start date are used to implement the
    five-year lookback rule (Section~\ref{sec:roth});
  \item \textit{Roth conv fixed} --- a checkbox, not an amount, deciding whether
    the \textit{Roth conv} figure beside it binds.  Unticked, the default, the
    amount is documentation only and the year's conversion is optimized as usual;
    ticked, the conversion is held at exactly that amount, bypassing the maximum
    annual conversion cap and the start and stop years, so a ticked~0 means no
    conversion that year.  Keeping the flag separate from the amount lets a year's
    figure stay in place while it is un-ticked.  The flag has no effect on the five
    lead-in rows, where past conversions always count;
  \item \textit{QCD} --- qualified charitable distributions paid directly from
    the tax-deferred account to a charity (Section~\ref{sec:qcd});
  \item \textit{big-ticket items} --- large planned expenses such as a home
    purchase or college tuition that must be funded from the portfolio in a
    specific year (negative), or inflows such as an inheritance (positive); a
    negative entry also pays for an after-tax contribution made from pay.
\end{itemize}

Only \textit{year} is required. Any other column may be omitted from a person
sheet, in which case it is read as zero for every year.

\subsection*{Fixed Assets table}

The Fixed Assets table describes illiquid assets held outside the investment
accounts: a primary residence, other real estate, collectibles, precious metals,
individual stocks, or a lump-sum fixed annuity.
Assets may be already held or acquired in a future year.
Each row has the following columns:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textit{active} --- a boolean turning the asset on or off without deleting
    the row, which is useful for comparing cases; absent or true, the asset is
    included;
  \item \textit{name} --- a unique identifier for the asset, which is also what a
    loan in the Debts table names to be paid off by its sale;
  \item \textit{type} --- one of \textit{residence}, \textit{real estate},
    \textit{collectibles}, \textit{precious metals}, \textit{stocks}, or
    \textit{fixed annuity}.  The type governs both how the growth \textit{rate}
    is read and how the proceeds are taxed at disposition, as described below;
  \item \textit{year} --- the reference year, which is the first January in which
    the asset is held: an asset already owned carries the current year, while one
    bought during a year is entered as the year after, since it was not there in
    January.  A year already past is reset to the current year on read;
  \item \textit{basis} --- the cost basis, in the nominal dollars of the year it
    was or will be paid, against which the gain at disposition is measured;
  \item \textit{value} --- the value at the beginning of the reference year, in
    reference-year dollars, from which the asset grows to its disposition year;
  \item \textit{rate} --- the annual growth rate, in percent, read as \emph{real}
    for a \textit{residence}, \textit{real estate}, \textit{collectibles}, and
    \textit{precious metals}, and as \emph{nominal} for \textit{stocks} and a
    \textit{fixed annuity}.  The default of zero therefore means two different
    things: for a physical asset it tracks inflation and preserves purchasing
    power, which long-run US data make a defensible starting point, while for a
    nominal-rate asset it means no growth at all and a steady loss of real value;
  \item \textit{yod} --- the year of disposition, at the start of which the asset
    is sold.  A non-positive value counts back from the end of the plan, so $-1$
    is the final plan year and $-2$ the year before it, while $0$ is one year
    past the end.  A \textit{yod} landing beyond the plan---the $0$ case
    included---leaves the asset unsold, to be liquidated into the bequest at the
    end of the plan, untaxed, since property passing to heirs takes a stepped-up
    basis (IRC~\S1014);
  \item \textit{commission} --- the sale commission, in percent of the grown
    value, which reduces both the proceeds and, through them, the taxable gain.
\end{itemize}

When an asset \emph{is} sold within the plan, the proceeds are the grown value
net of commission, the gain is measured against the cost basis from those net
proceeds, and the proceeds are split into tax-free, ordinary-income, and
capital-gain portions by type:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textit{fixed annuity}: any gain over basis is ordinary income, and the
    basis itself returns tax-free;
  \item \textit{residence}: the gain is reduced by the principal-residence
    exclusion of \$250,000, or \$500,000 for a couple filing jointly
    (IRC~\S121(b)(1)--(2)); only the excess is a capital gain, while the basis
    plus the excluded part of the gain is tax-free.
    A surviving spouse keeps the \$500,000 limit for a sale no later than two
    years after the death and takes \$250,000 after that (IRC~\S121(b)(4)); with
    Owl's convention of placing the death at the end of year $n_d - 1$ and a sale
    at the start of its year, the joint limit applies for $n \le n_d + 1$;
  \item \textit{real estate}, \textit{collectibles}, \textit{precious metals}, and
    \textit{stocks}: the whole gain over basis is a capital gain and the basis
    returns tax-free.
\end{itemize}
A sale at a loss makes the entire proceeds tax-free; losses are not carried
against other gains.
The 28\% collectibles rate is not modeled: a collectibles gain is taxed at the
long-term rates of Section~\ref{sec:ltcg}.

\subsection*{Debts table}

The Debts table lists outstanding loan and mortgage obligations, whose payments
are not part of the net spending amount.
Each row has the following columns:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textit{active} --- a boolean turning the debt on or off without deleting
    the row; absent or true, the debt is included;
  \item \textit{name} --- a unique identifier for the debt, such as
    ``Primary mortgage'' or ``Car loan'';
  \item \textit{type} --- \textit{loan} or \textit{mortgage}.  The two are
    classified separately for the reader's benefit but are treated identically;
  \item \textit{year} --- the start year, in which the loan was taken out or
    payments begin;
  \item \textit{term} --- the term in years, so that regular payments run from
    the start year through the year before \textit{year}~$+$~\textit{term};
  \item \textit{amount} --- the principal outstanding at the start year, which is
    the balance amortized over the term;
  \item \textit{rate} --- the annual interest rate, in percent, from which the
    constant payment is computed by standard monthly amortization;
  \item \textit{property} --- optionally, the \textit{residence} or
    \textit{real estate} row of the Fixed Assets table that the loan finances.
    Naming one ties the loan to it: the loan is paid off in the year that
    property is sold, out of the sale proceeds, and nothing is paid afterwards.
    Left empty, the loan runs to its term.
\end{itemize}

Annual debt-service payments enter the cash-flow constraint as fixed outflows.
No optimization takes place on a debt: whether to pay a mortgage down or stay
invested turns on risk tolerance rather than on a comparison of rates, which is
outside what a linear program over returns can express.
A debt left outstanding at the end of the plan is settled from the savings
accounts, so a bequest of zero still leaves enough to discharge it---which also
means that selling a property late in a plan with a zero bequest can leave the
problem infeasible.
Mortgage interest is not deducted, since Owl takes the standard deduction.

\subsection*{Cash-flow balance}

In every plan year, Owl enforces an annual cash-flow balance.
Spending plus all taxes (the employee's payroll tax on wages included), Medicare premiums,
and debt payments must equal income from wages, Social Security benefits,
account withdrawals, fixed-asset sale proceeds, and other income.
Account contributions do not appear in this balance: anticipated wages are
entered net of pre-tax contributions, an after-tax contribution paid from pay
is offset by a negative big-ticket item (see the Wages and Contributions table
above), and the contributed amounts are deposited directly into their accounts
through the account-evolution equations.
Big-ticket expenses shift additional spending into specific years.
The optimizer determines the combination of account withdrawals and Roth
conversions---across all account types and both spouses---that satisfies
this constraint while maximizing the chosen objective.

\section{Social Security Benefits}
\label{sec:ss}

\subsection*{Rules}

Monthly Social Security retirement benefits equal the Primary Insurance Amount
(PIA) multiplied by an age-based adjustment factor.
The Full Retirement Age (FRA) depends on birth year: 65 for those born before
1938; 66 for those born between 1943 and 1954; and 67 for those born in 1960
or later, with two-month-per-year interpolation for the transition cohorts
(born 1938--1942 and 1955--1959).
A special Social Security Administration (SSA) rule treats individuals born on
January~1st as attaining age on the last day of the prior month, shifting their
effective FRA by one month.
Reference: SSA POMS RS~00615.003 \cite{ssa-poms}.

Benefits claimed before FRA are permanently reduced: by 5/9 of~1\% per month
for the first 36~months before FRA, and by 5/12 of~1\% per month for any
additional months beyond that, yielding a minimum of approximately 70\% of PIA
at age~62 when FRA is~67.
Benefits deferred beyond FRA earn delayed retirement credits of 8\% per year
up to age~70.
Reference: SSA POMS RS~00615.015 \cite{ssa-poms}.

The spousal benefit is up to 50\% of the higher-earning spouse's PIA, reduced
by the claiming spouse's own PIA (floored at zero), with an additional reduction
of 25/36 of~1\% per month for claiming before the spousal FRA (which mirrors
the own FRA schedule) and no bonus for deferral beyond FRA.
Delayed credits in the claiming spouse's own benefit do not add to the spousal
excess: the combined payment is computed without them and the own benefit with
them is subtracted from it, so the credits come out of the spousal part, down to
zero (POMS RS~00615.694; 42~U.S.C.~402(k)(3)(A)).

The survivor benefit starts from the deceased spouse's PIA plus the delayed
retirement credits earned up to death, fixed at death.  It is reduced if the
survivor claims before their own survivor FRA (minimum 71.5\% at age~60,
interpolated linearly in the claiming month).  If the deceased claimed before
their own FRA, the reduced amount is then capped at the greater of the deceased's
reduced benefit and 82.5\% of their PIA (Social Security Act §~202(e)(2)(D)); the
cap applies after the survivor's age reduction and is not itself reduced.
Unlike the retirement benefit, it earns no delayed retirement credits past the
survivor FRA, so deferring beyond that date is never advantageous.
The survivor benefit and the survivor's own retirement benefit are distinct
entitlements with independent claiming dates, and only the larger of the two is
paid in any given month; claiming one does not forfeit the other. A survivor may
therefore claim the survivor benefit at~60 and let their own benefit accrue
delayed credits to age~70, or the reverse.
Reference: CFR~§~404.338 \cite{ssa-poms}.

Benefits claimed before FRA are also subject to the \emph{retirement earnings
test}, which withholds benefits while the beneficiary works.
Below the year of FRA attainment, \$1 of benefit is withheld for every \$2 of
earnings above an annual exempt amount; in the FRA year the rate softens to \$1
per \$3 above a much higher exempt amount, and only earnings in the months
\emph{before} the FRA month count.
From the FRA month on the test no longer applies, whatever the earnings.
For 2026 the exempt amounts are \$24,480 below the FRA year and \$65,160 in it.
Earnings here are wages before pre-tax deferrals (W-2 box~5) plus net
self-employment earnings, not AGI.
The excess is charged against the family benefit on the worker's record,
month by month from the first month of entitlement in the year, and an
auxiliary's own excess is then charged against whatever remains of their
benefit.
A beneficiary's first year of entitlement also gets a \emph{grace year}: a
month in which no work is done, or in which wages do not exceed one twelfth of
the annual exempt amount, is paid in full regardless of the annual total.
References: SSA POMS RS~02501.021, .025, .030, .080, .095;
20~CFR~404.430--404.439 \cite{cfr404earnings}.

Benefits withheld by the test are \emph{not} forfeited.
At FRA the SSA performs an \emph{adjustment of the reduction factor} (ARF):
the months in which any benefit was withheld, the \emph{crediting months}, are
subtracted from the original reduction factor, which is equivalent to
recomputing the factor as though the claim had been made that many months
later.
Writing $c_i$ for the crediting months accumulated by individual $i$, the
factor applied from the FRA month on is
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:ARF}
    \Phi^{\mathrm{arf}}_i \;=\;
    \min\!\Bigl(1,\; \Phi\bigl(\min(\alpha_i + c_i/12,\, \mathrm{FRA}_i),\,
        \mathrm{FRA}_i\bigr)\Bigr),
\end{equation}
with $\alpha_i$ the claiming age and $\Phi$ the adjustment factor of
Eq.~(\ref{Eq:SelfFactor}).
The cap at FRA is what makes the ARF a partial, not a full, restitution: a
withheld month raises the benefit for the rest of the beneficiary's life, but
never above the unreduced PIA, so an individual who claims at FRA or later and
works earns no credit back.
For someone born on the first of a month, whose SSA age runs a month ahead, the
cap falls one conventional month before $\mathrm{FRA}_i$.
A spouse accumulates crediting months from deductions caused by their own work
and by the worker's, counted separately on each record.
References: SSA POMS RS~00615.480, .482, .490.

The fraction of SS benefits subject to federal income tax ranges from 0\% to
85\% based on ``provisional income'' (PI), defined as adjusted gross income
(excluding Social Security) plus tax-exempt interest plus half of total SS benefits.
Thresholds are \$25,000/\$34,000 (single) and \$32,000/\$44,000 (MFJ), frozen
since 1983 and 1994 respectively.
Reference: IRS Publication~915 \cite{irs915}.

Owl also accepts a \code{trim_pct} and \code{trim_year} to model a
projected reduction in SS benefits, for example to reflect a potential trust
fund depletion scenario.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

SS benefit amounts, FRA schedules, spousal benefits, and survivor benefits
are computed from the PIA inputs and each individual's date of birth.
Own, spousal, and survivor entitlements are built as three separate time series
and then combined as SSA pays them: while both spouses are alive, each receives
their own benefit plus any excess spousal amount; from the year of the first
death~$n_d$ the spousal add-on ends and the survivor receives
$\max(\zeta^{\text{own}}_{i_s n},\,\zeta^{\text{surv}}_{n})$.
The survivor claiming date is set by the parameter \code{survivor_claim_age},
which accepts \code{"immediate"} (as soon as eligible), \code{"FRA"}, or an
explicit age; it is never earlier than age~60 or~$n_d$, and is capped at the
survivor FRA.
When claiming ages are searched (\code{withSSAges}), each candidate age is a
complete, ordinary solve, so all three entitlements---own, spousal, and
survivor---are recomputed exactly for that age rather than approximated; see
Chapter~\ref{Chap:SSAges}.
SS taxability is computed iteratively using the provisional income formula,
updated at each pass until it converges; a more exact method is also available
via the option \code{withSSTaxability="optimize"} \cite{irs915}.

Owl always applies the earnings test.
The earnings it counts in year $n$ are the \textit{anticipated wages} plus that
year's \textit{401k ctrb} and \textit{IRA ctrb}, which those wages are entered
net of, deflated to today's dollars to match the exempt amounts, and taken as
spread evenly over the \textit{months worked} at the start of the year.
That month count is what decides whether a month qualifies as a non-service
month for the grace-year rule, and, in the FRA year, what share of the year's
earnings falls before the FRA month.
The excess is floored to the whole dollar and charged month by month against the
family's monthly entitlements, splitting a partial last month in proportion to
the original entitlement shares; the number of months carrying any deduction is
accumulated as the crediting months $c_i$ and applied from the FRA month through
Eq.~(\ref{Eq:ARF}).
Everything the test does is, for a given claiming month, a fixed function of
that month: the withheld amounts and the raised post-FRA benefit are numbers
computed before the LP is built, so the test adds no variable and no constraint,
and a claiming-age search sees its effect on every candidate.
Exempt amounts come from the POMS chart for 2024--2026 and are held at the 2026
level in today's dollars thereafter.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

The SSA family maximum benefit cap (CFR~§~404.403) is not modeled; this may
overstate benefits when multiple beneficiaries claim on one worker's record.
The earnings test is not applied to checks paid from the year of the first death
on: survivor benefits under the test, and the survivor ARF of
RS~00615.580, are not modeled.
Its exempt amounts are indexed to the national average wage with a two-year lag,
which Owl does not track, so holding them flat in real terms after 2026 slightly
overstates the withholding of later years.
Employer contributions included in \textit{401k ctrb} were never wages, so
counting them makes the earnings slightly high; a payroll HSA contribution is
correctly left out, as it is not in W-2 box~5.
Self-employment earnings count only where they are entered in
\textit{anticipated wages}: the test does not read \textit{other inc}.
The monthly test is implemented only as the initial grace year; the
substantial-services test for self-employment is out of scope.
The survivor claiming date is a user setting rather than an optimized decision:
the model evaluates whichever date is specified, but does not search for the one
that maximizes the objective.
The Windfall Elimination Provision (WEP) and Government Pension Offset (GPO)
were repealed by the Social Security Fairness Act (signed January~5, 2025) and therefore
require no modeling.

\section{Pension Income}

\subsection*{Overview}

A pension is a fixed periodic payment from an employer-sponsored defined-benefit
plan, paid for the life of the retiree.
Unlike Social Security, pension amounts are set by the plan sponsor and are
not subject to federal phase-in rules; they begin in full at the elected
commencement age.
Some plans offer cost-of-living adjustments (COLAs) that partially or fully
index payments to inflation; many do not.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

Each individual may have a monthly pension amount and a commencement age.
Owl converts the monthly amount to an annual figure and determines the
first plan year in which payments begin, using the individual's birth year
and birth month to compute the age reached in each calendar year.
In the first year of receipt, the annual amount is prorated by the fraction
of the year remaining after commencement.
For subsequent years the full annual amount (twelve times the monthly benefit)
is credited.

When indexed, the annual pension payment grows with inflation each year so
that its purchasing power remains constant in real terms.
When not indexed, the nominal dollar amount is fixed for the entire
duration of the pension.
In both cases no pension income is credited before the commencement year.
Pension income enters the cash-flow constraint as ordinary income
alongside wages, other income, and Social Security benefits.

\subsection*{Joint-and-survivor option}

When a pension holder elects a joint-and-survivor (J\&S) option, the surviving
spouse receives a fraction (e.g., 50\%, 75\%, or 100\%) of the primary's
pension after the primary's death.
The user specifies the \emph{actual} monthly amount received (already
actuarially reduced if J\&S was elected) and the survivor fraction as a number
between 0 and 1.
Starting in the year after the first spouse passes away, the surviving spouse
receives that fraction of the deceased's pension each year for the remainder of
their horizon.
If the primary's pension is inflation-indexed, the survivor benefit follows the
same cost-of-living adjustment, so both the primary's pension (while alive) and
the survivor benefit grow at the same rate over time.
The resulting survivor benefit is added to the surviving spouse's pension income
and enters the cash-flow as ordinary income.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

Early-commencement reductions and late-commencement credits are not modeled.

\section{Single Premium Immediate Annuity (SPIA)}
\label{sec:spia}

\subsection*{Overview}

A Single Premium Immediate Annuity (SPIA) converts a one-time lump-sum premium
into a guaranteed income stream that begins immediately and continues for the
annuitant's lifetime.
Unlike a pension, the income rate (premium-to-income ratio) is market-determined
and must be supplied by the user; Owl does not contain an actuarial pricing model.
The premium is funded from the individual's tax-deferred account via an IRA
rollover---a non-taxable transfer---so no ordinary income is recognized at the
time of purchase.
Annual payments begin in the year of purchase and are fully taxable as ordinary
income for the remainder of the plan horizon.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

Each individual may hold one or more SPIAs; each is added independently via
\code{addSPIA()}.
In the purchase year, the premium is deducted from the annuitant's tax-deferred
balance as a non-taxable IRA rollover; no ordinary income is generated at that
time.
If the purchase year precedes the plan's start year (an already-purchased SPIA),
the premium deduction is skipped and income begins at plan year zero.

Annual SPIA income equals twelve times the specified monthly benefit.
When the \emph{indexed} flag is set, the nominal benefit grows with the inflation
series each year so that its real purchasing power remains constant; otherwise
the nominal dollar amount is fixed for the life of the annuity.

An optional \emph{survivor fraction} ($0 \le f_s \le 1$) directs a specified
share of the annuitant's benefit to continue to the other individual after the
annuitant's death.

SPIA income enters the cash-flow constraint as ordinary income alongside wages,
pension payments, and Social Security benefits.
It therefore raises Modified Adjusted Gross Income (MAGI) and affects Medicare
IRMAA surcharges and the Social Security taxability fraction.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item The premium is assumed drawn entirely from a tax-deferred account (IRA
    rollover); funding from taxable or Roth accounts is not modeled.
  \item Full annual income is credited from the purchase year; partial-year
    proration in the purchase year is not modeled.
  \item Annuity pricing (premium-to-income ratio) is user-supplied; Owl
    contains no actuarial pricing model.
  \item Payments cease at the annuitant's plan horizon; term-certain and
    period-certain riders are not modeled.
\end{itemize}

\section{Roth Account Rules}
\label{sec:roth}

\subsection*{Rules}

Roth IRA contributions are made with after-tax dollars; qualified withdrawals
are entirely tax- and penalty-free.
Reference: IRC~§~408A \cite{irc408a}.
Contribution \emph{principal} may be withdrawn at any time without tax or
penalty.
Roth \emph{earnings} on contributions require both (a) the account must be at
least five years old and (b) the owner must be at least age~59\textonehalf{}
(or meet another IRS exception) for a penalty-free withdrawal.

Each Roth \emph{conversion} carries its own independent five-year clock.
Conversion principal withdrawn before age~59\textonehalf{} is subject to a
10\% early withdrawal penalty if taken within five years of that conversion.
Roth conversions are fully taxable as ordinary income in the year of
conversion.
Reference: IRC~§~408A(d)(3); IRS Publication~590-B \cite{irs590b}.

Roth IRA accounts are not subject to Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs)
during the original owner's lifetime.
Roth~401(k) RMDs were eliminated by SECURE~2.0 Act~§~325 effective for tax
years beginning after December~31, 2023.
A 10\% early withdrawal penalty also applies to tax-deferred account
withdrawals before age~59\textonehalf{} (subject to exceptions not modeled here).

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

The five-year maturation rule is enforced as a set of minimum-balance
constraints that retain recent conversions at compounded value, plus
gains-only on recent contributions, as a floor on the Roth account balance.
Before age~59\textonehalf{}, a second set of constraints keeps cumulative Roth
withdrawals within the starting Roth balance, the contributions made since, and
the conversions past their five-year period, since by the ordering rules of
IRC~§~408A(d)(4)(B) anything beyond those comes from earnings or from
conversions still in their period.
The age-59\textonehalf{} threshold is computed per individual from their birth
month.
Conversion amounts are added to taxable ordinary income in the year of
conversion.
Conversion behavior can be further controlled via the options
\code{maxRothConversion}, \code{noRothConversions},
\code{startRothConversions}, \code{stopRothConversions}, and
\code{swapRothConverters}
\cite{irc408a,irs590b}.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

A single unified five-year lookback is applied conservatively: recent
conversions at compounded value plus gains-only on contributions are retained
as a minimum balance floor.
Contribution principal is not separately tracked, so some valid early
withdrawals of contribution principal are conservatively disallowed.
The pro-rata rule (IRS Form~8606) is not modeled: Owl treats the entire
tax-deferred balance as pre-tax, so every dollar converted or withdrawn is
fully taxable ordinary income.
Users with significant IRA after-tax (nondeductible) basis should be aware of
this limitation.
Roth conversions are also disallowed in the last two years of the plan horizon.

\section{Health Savings Accounts}
\label{Sec:HSA}
\label{sec:hsa}

\subsection*{Rules}

A Health Savings Account (HSA) is a tax-advantaged account available to
individuals enrolled in a High-Deductible Health Plan (HDHP).
HSAs offer a unique triple tax advantage: contributions are pre-tax (above the
line, reducing AGI), investment growth is tax-free, and withdrawals used for
qualified medical expenses are tax-free at any age.
After age~65, non-qualified withdrawals are taxed as ordinary income---making
the HSA functionally equivalent to a traditional IRA for general use, but
without Required Minimum Distributions.
Reference: IRC~§~223 \cite{irc223}; IRS Publication~969 \cite{irs969}.

Eligibility requires enrollment in a High-Deductible Health Plan (HDHP)
and no disqualifying coverage (e.g., Medicare, non-HDHP insurance).
Enrollment in any part of Medicare (Part~A, B, C, or D) immediately
disqualifies one from contributing; existing HSA balances may still be
withdrawn for qualified expenses.
Annual contribution limits for 2026 are \$4,400 for self-only coverage and
\$8,750 for family coverage, with an additional \$1,000 catch-up for
individuals age~55 and older (each spouse with family coverage may add
\$1,000 to their own account if both are 55+).
Contributions must cease once the account owner enrolls in Medicare, which
typically occurs at age~65.
Reference: IRC~§~223(b)(7) \cite{irc223}; IRS Publication~969 \cite{irs969}.

A surviving spouse who is the named beneficiary inherits the HSA intact and
may treat it as their own HSA, preserving all three tax advantages.
A non-spouse beneficiary must include the full account balance in taxable
income in the year of inheritance.
Reference: IRC~§~223(f)(8)(B) \cite{irc223}.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

Owl models the HSA as a fourth savings account type alongside taxable,
tax-deferred, and Roth accounts.
Because the dominant retiree use is funding qualified medical expenses, all
HSA withdrawals are treated as tax-free (simplified model); see Limitations below.
The key modeling choices are as follows.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textit{Contributions are pre-tax.}
    Made through payroll, HSA contributions are netted out of the anticipated
    wages, like the other pre-tax contributions (Section~\ref{sec:cfhfp}), which
    excludes the contributed dollars from ordinary taxable income, from
    provisional income used to compute Social Security taxability, and from the
    modified adjusted gross income (MAGI) used in the two-year Medicare IRMAA
    lookback. Made from savings without wages, they are entered as a negative
    \textit{other inc} as well, an above-the-line deduction (IRC~223(a)) that
    pays for them; in the year of Medicare enrollment, only for the months
    before it.
    Contributions are zeroed automatically at Medicare enrollment age; HFP entries past age~65 are ignored.
  \item \textit{Withdrawals are tax-free and fund any expense.}
    HSA withdrawals enter the annual cash-flow balance at full dollar value,
    identical to Roth account withdrawals, and are excluded from taxable income.
    Because Medicare Parts~B and~D premiums, IRMAA surcharges, Medigap
    premiums, and other out-of-pocket medical costs are all qualified
    expenses under IRS Publication~969, the user may apply HSA withdrawals
    to cover these costs tax-free.
    The optimizer does not track medical expenses explicitly; it treats HSA
    withdrawals as generic tax-free cash flow, and the user is responsible
    for ensuring actual withdrawals correspond to qualified expenses.
  \item \textit{No Required Minimum Distributions.}
    Unlike tax-deferred accounts, HSAs are never subject to mandatory annual
    distributions.
  \item \textit{Cannot exceed account balance.}
    As with taxable and Roth accounts, the optimizer may not withdraw more
    than the current HSA balance in any year.
  \item \textit{Bequest at heirs' tax rate.}
    The HSA balance remaining at the end of the plan is valued after the
    heirs' assumed marginal income tax rate, identical to the treatment of
    tax-deferred accounts.
    A surviving spouse inherits the HSA intact with no tax (the full balance
    transfers to their own HSA), but a non-spouse beneficiary must include
    the full amount in ordinary income.
    The reported estate value applies the same discount.
  \item \textit{Asset allocation.}
    In account-specific allocation mode, the HSA defaults to the same
    allocation as the Roth account, reflecting that both grow tax-free.
    Users may also specify a separate HSA allocation.
  \item \textit{Spousal transfer at death.}
    At the death of the first spouse, the full HSA balance transfers to the
    surviving spouse's HSA account, preserving its tax-advantaged status.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

\textbf{Withdrawals.}
All HSA withdrawals are treated as qualified medical expenses and therefore
entirely tax-free.
Non-medical withdrawals after age~65---which would be taxable as ordinary
income---are not separately tracked.
Before age~65, non-qualified withdrawals incur ordinary income tax plus a
20\% penalty; Owl does not model this penalty because all withdrawals are
assumed qualified.
This simplification is conservative for the dominant retiree use case:
funding Medicare premiums and out-of-pocket health costs.
Future work may introduce a more detailed model that distinguishes medical
from non-medical withdrawals.

\textbf{Contributions.}
The annual IRS contribution limit (\$4,400 self-only and \$8,750 family for
2026) is not enforced as an optimizer constraint; users are responsible for
not exceeding it in the \textit{HSA ctrb} column of the Household Financial
Profile.
The catch-up contribution (\$1,000 for age~55+) is not automatically applied;
users aged 55+ must add it manually if desired.
Self-only vs.\ family coverage is not distinguished; the applicable limit is
the user's responsibility.
The mandatory contribution stop at Medicare enrollment is enforced by zeroing
contributions from that year forward. \texttt{setHSA()} is invoked either
explicitly or automatically when loading from configuration (e.g., TOML).
HFP entries in the \textit{HSA ctrb} column for years when the individual
is age~65 or older are overwritten with zero.
If Medicare enrollment occurs mid-year, the IRS allows prorated contributions
for months before enrollment \cite{irs8889}; Owl zeroes the entire year, which is conservative.

\textbf{Eligibility.}
Owl assumes the user is HSA-eligible (enrolled in an HDHP with no
disqualifying coverage).
HDHP status, disqualifying coverage, and dependent status are not verified.

\textbf{Bequests.}
Non-spouse beneficiary inheritance is modeled by applying the heirs'
marginal income tax rate to the terminal HSA balance, identical to the
treatment of tax-deferred accounts.
The simplified qualified-withdrawal model (all HSA withdrawals tax-free
during the plan horizon) is not extended to non-qualified withdrawals after
age~65; those remain deferred to a future path.

\section{ACA Marketplace Premiums (Pre-65)}
\label{sec:aca}

\subsection*{Rules}

Before Medicare eligibility at age~65, individuals and couples may obtain health
coverage through the Health Insurance Marketplace (Exchange) established by the
Affordable Care Act.
The Premium Tax Credit (PTC) reduces the net premium cost for Marketplace plans
based on household modified adjusted gross income (MAGI) and the Federal Poverty
Level (FPL).
Reference: IRC~\S~36B \cite{irc36b}.

The applicable percentage---the share of income a household is expected to contribute
toward the benchmark plan---varies with the ratio of MAGI to FPL.
The IRS publishes the applicable percentage table annually.
For 2025, Rev.\ Proc.\ 2024-35 provides the table under the Inflation Reduction Act
(IRA), which suspends indexing and caps the contribution at 8.5\% for income above
400\% FPL.
Reference: Rev.\ Proc.\ 2024-35 \cite{revproc2024_35}.

For 2026 and beyond, Rev.\ Proc.\ 2025-25 provides the applicable percentage table
when the IRA enhanced subsidies have expired: indexing resumes, contribution
percentages are higher, and there is no subsidy for income above 400\% FPL (the
``subsidy cliff'' returns).
Reference: Rev.\ Proc.\ 2025-25 \cite{revproc2025_25}.

Federal Poverty Level guidelines are published annually by the Department of Health
and Human Services (HHS).
A coverage year uses the guideline \emph{published the year before}, since the statute
takes the most recently published poverty line as of the first day of the open-enrollment
period.  The guidelines published in 2025, which price coverage year 2026, are \$15,650
(one person) and \$21,150 (two) for the 48 contiguous states and DC; those published in
2026, pricing coverage year 2027, are \$15,960 and \$21,640.
Reference: HHS Poverty Guidelines \cite{hhspoverty}.

Household size for the poverty line is the \emph{tax household} --- two for a couple
filing jointly while both are alive, even when one spouse has reached Medicare
(IRC~36B(d)(1); 26~CFR~1.36B-1(d)(2)).  Who is \emph{enrolled} is a separate question,
settled month by month: an individual is covered in the months of a year before Medicare
begins, and where only one partner of a couple is enrolled the benchmark is the couple's
premium scaled by the CMS age-rating fraction for that partner.
Up to the state's Medicaid limit the household is covered by Medicaid at \emph{no} premium:
138\% of FPL in expansion states, 100\% in Wisconsin, and no Medicaid at all in the
remaining states.  Where that limit falls below 100\% of FPL, income below 100\% receives
neither Medicaid nor the credit, which starts at 100\% (IRC~36B(c)(1)(A)): the
\emph{coverage gap}, in which the household pays the full benchmark premium.
Income of exactly 400\% of FPL still qualifies, since the statute covers income that
``does not exceed 400 percent.''

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

Owl models ACA net premium costs for pre-65 years when the user supplies the
annual benchmark Silver plan (SLCSP) premium via \texttt{setACA()}.
Two computation modes are available.

In the default \code{withACA="loop"} mode, ACA costs are computed after each
solver pass using \texttt{acaCosts()}, which applies the IRS applicable-percentage
table to MAGI and FPL for each plan year.
Rules are year-aware, by taxable year: 2025 uses Rev.\ Proc.\ 2024-35, 2026 uses
Rev.\ Proc.\ 2025-25, 2027 uses Rev.\ Proc.\ 2026-26, and a year past the last published
table reuses it.
Within Medicaid the premium is zero; in a coverage gap the loop charges the lowest
applicable percentage as though from 100\% of FPL, because the exact cost jumps to the
full benchmark below 100\% and the loop would oscillate across that cliff.
Optimize mode prices the gap exactly, with a bracket of its own.

In \code{withACA="optimize"} mode, ACA bracket selection and cost are folded into
the optimization.
The formulation uses 8 MAGI brackets --- 7 intervals up to 400\% of FPL plus one above
--- with SOS1 binary selection, MAGI decomposition, and a piecewise cost.
Bracket~0, below the first threshold, is Medicaid at no premium or the coverage gap at the
full benchmark; brackets~1--6 follow the sliding scale, each priced from below by the
maximum of four tangent lines to the convex cost within the bracket; bracket~7, at 400\%
and above, imposes the full SLCSP.
The bracket thresholds are set by the year's Medicaid limit, so bracket~1 has zero width
wherever Medicaid reaches past the lowest band.
ACA uses current-year MAGI (no two-year lag like Medicare IRMAA). Unlike IRMAA and
the NIIT, the ACA MAGI is the full-Social-Security variant $\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$
(IRC~§36B adds the non-taxable portion of Social Security back to AGI).

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

The SLCSP benchmark premium is user-supplied and inflated by the plan's inflation
factor.
FPL and applicable-percentage tables are updated annually in the code; manual
updates are required when HHS or IRS publish new values.
If Congress extends IRA subsidies (e.g., the 8.5\% cap above 400\%), parameters
must be updated accordingly.
Cost-sharing reductions and per-year benchmark premiums by band are not modeled: the
benchmark is one user-supplied figure, inflated.

\section{Medicare and IRMAA Premiums}

\subsection*{Rules}

Medicare Part~B eligibility begins at age~65.
The standard 2026 monthly premium is \$202.90 (\$2,434.80/year).
Reference: Centers for Medicare \& Medicaid Services (CMS) 2026 Medicare
Part~B premium announcement \cite{cms2026}.

The Income-Related Monthly Adjustment Amount (IRMAA) adds a surcharge based
on MAGI from two years prior.
In 2026, the five incremental per-person per-month surcharges are \$81.20,
\$121.70, \$121.70, \$121.70, and \$40.70, applied at MAGI thresholds of
\$109k, \$137k, \$171k, \$205k, and \$500k (single); for married filing jointly
(MFJ) the first four are doubled, to \$218k, \$274k, \$342k, and \$410k, and the
top threshold is \$750k.
Reference: CMS 2026 IRMAA tables \cite{cms2026}.

MAGI for IRMAA purposes is the beneficiary's adjusted gross income (AGI, IRS
Form~1040 line~11) plus tax-exempt interest income (line~2a). It therefore
includes all ordinary taxable income, LTCG, and dividends, but only the
\emph{taxable} portion of Social Security benefits (the up-to-85\% already in
AGI); the non-taxable remainder is \emph{not} added back. This differs from the
Affordable Care Act definition (Section~\ref{sec:aca}), which does add back
non-taxable Social Security.
Reference: SSA POMS HI~01101.010 \cite{ssa-poms}.
For couples, premiums reflect both individuals when both are Medicare-eligible.
The table that applies is that of the filing status on the lookback return
(20~CFR 418.1115(c)): a couple files jointly in the year of the first death, so the
survivor's first two years after it still use the joint table, and the single
table starts in the third.  A survivor's request to use a more recent year
(418.1205(a)) is not modeled.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

Owl determines which IRMAA bracket applies to each year's MAGI and computes
the exact cumulative premium for that bracket.
Two computation modes are available.
The default, \code{withMedicare="loop"}, computes IRMAA costs after each
solver pass.
Setting \code{withMedicare="optimize"} folds bracket selection
directly into the optimization for an exact result.
The two-year MAGI lookback is fully implemented.
Because IRMAA in plan year $n$ depends on MAGI from year $n-2$, the first two
plan years require MAGI values from before the plan start.
These are supplied by the user via the \code{previousMAGIs} option
as a list of the two MAGI values from two and one years before the plan start.
When not supplied, both default to zero, which may underestimate IRMAA surcharges
in the first two plan years for higher-income households.
Reference: \cite{cms2026}.

\subsection*{Part~D and assumptions}

Medicare Part~D is modeled using the same MAGI brackets and two-year lookback as Part~B.
Part~D IRMAA surcharges (CMS 2026) are included by default; the optional Part~D base
premium is configurable (default zero).
Users can disable Part~D via \code{includeMedicarePartD=false} when they have other
drug coverage (e.g., employer or VA).
IRMAA thresholds in Owl are assumed to scale with the general inflation rate;
in practice, CMS adjusts brackets annually but not necessarily by the Consumer
Price Index (CPI).
The top tier is the exception: the Bipartisan Budget Act of~2018 froze it at
\$500,000 (\$750,000 joint) in nominal dollars, and Owl holds it there until
indexing resumes in~2028.

\subsection*{Medical price inflation}

Medical prices have historically outgrown general inflation, and a plan may say so.
The parameter \code{setMedicalInflation(excess)} takes a yearly growth above
inflation, in percent and bounded to the interval $[0, 10]$, and medical prices
then grow by the compounded factor
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:MedicalInflation}
    \gamma^{\mathrm{med}}_n \;=\; \gamma_n\,(1 + \epsilon^{\mathrm{med}})^{\,n},
\end{equation}
with $\epsilon^{\mathrm{med}}$ the excess as a decimal and $\gamma_n$ the general
inflation factor.
The default of zero keeps medical prices in step with inflation, recovering
$\gamma^{\mathrm{med}}_n = \gamma_n$.

What $\gamma^{\mathrm{med}}_n$ scales is a deliberate, asymmetric choice: the
Medicare Part~B and Part~D base premiums and their IRMAA surcharges, the ACA
benchmark premium, and the household's other qualified medical expenses.
What it does \emph{not} scale is anything statutory---the tax brackets, the
standard deduction, the IRMAA bracket thresholds, and the poverty line all keep
following $\gamma_n$ alone.
That asymmetry is the point of the parameter: a positive excess does not merely
raise a cost, it pushes medical costs \emph{through} thresholds that are not
moving with them, so IRMAA tiers and the ACA bands are crossed earlier than
general inflation alone would suggest.

\section{Long-Term Capital Gains Tax}
\label{sec:ltcg}

\subsection*{Rules}

Long-term capital gains (LTCG) and qualified dividends are taxed at
preferential federal rates of 0\%, 15\%, or 20\% based on total taxable
income (ordinary income plus LTCG combined).
Reference: IRS Topic~409 \cite{irs409}; IRS Publication~550.
LTCG ``stacks on top of'' ordinary income: the portion of gains that pushes
combined taxable income above each threshold is taxed at the corresponding
higher rate.
For 2026, the 0\%/15\% threshold is \$49,450 (single) or \$98,900 (MFJ);
the 15\%/20\% threshold is \$545,500 (single) or \$613,700 (MFJ).
These thresholds are indexed for inflation annually.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

LTCG bracket allocation is always active (no option required).
Total gains are split across the three rate brackets; because the rates
increase (0\%, 15\%, 20\%), the optimizer naturally uses the lowest-rate
brackets first.
The upper limit of each bracket depends on how much ordinary income already
occupies the corresponding threshold; these limits are refined at each solver
pass until they converge.
The total LTCG tax is derived from the bracket allocation after solving.
Reference: \cite{irs409}.

An optional MIP formulation is available via \code{withLTCG="optimize"}.
In this mode, the ordinary taxable income $G_n$ is embedded as a continuous
LP variable together with binary variables that select the applicable LTCG
bracket in each year, replacing the self-consistent loop refinement of
bracket thresholds with an exact mixed-integer constraint.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

Short-term capital gains (assets held one year or less) are taxed as ordinary
income.
Owl does not separately model short-term versus long-term gains within the
taxable account; all realized gains are treated as long-term.
The last line of the Qualified Dividends and Capital Gain Tax Worksheet takes
the lesser of the stacked tax and the regular tax on all taxable income
(IRC~1(h)(1)); Owl keeps the stacked tax. The two differ only because the 0\%
breakpoint is below the top of the 12\% bracket (\$49,450 and \$50,400 single,
\$98,900 and \$100,800 joint in 2026): gains in that gap pay 15\% where the
regular tax charges 12\%. The overstatement is at most 3\% of the gap, \$28.50
single or \$57 joint a year, and only in a year whose gains fall in it, while
taking the minimum exactly would need a binary variable per year.

\section{Net Investment Income Tax}

\subsection*{Rules}

The Net Investment Income Tax (NIIT) is a 3.8\% surtax imposed by the
Affordable Care Act on net investment income (NII) for taxpayers whose MAGI
exceeds \$200,000 (single) or \$250,000 (MFJ).
Reference: IRC~§~1411 \cite{irc1411}; IRS Form~8960.
The thresholds are \emph{not} indexed for inflation.
The tax equals 3.8\% of the lesser of net investment income and the amount by
which MAGI exceeds the applicable threshold.
NII includes dividends, interest, LTCG, rents, and royalties, but excludes
wages, Social Security benefits, tax-deferred retirement distributions, and
Roth withdrawals.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

The NIIT is computed annually from MAGI and net investment income (dividends,
interest, LTCG, and any rent or trust income entered in the \code{net inv}
column).
Rent and trust income from \code{net inv} is included in NII and also flows
into cash-flow and taxable-income as ordinary income.
The tax uses the IRS formula described above.
Because the NIIT depends on the solution, it is held fixed during each solver
pass and updated from the new income values until it converges.
The threshold is fixed in nominal dollars; its real impact over time is
correctly reflected in the model.
Reference: \cite{irc1411}.

An optional MIP formulation is available via \code{withNIIT="optimize"}.
In this mode, MAGI is embedded as a continuous LP variable together with a
binary variable $z^j_n$ that selects, each year, which term of the IRS
$\min(\cdot,\cdot)$ bounds the tax: the excess of MAGI over the threshold, or
net investment income. Because the optimizer minimizes taxes, it picks the
smaller, reproducing the IRS value over every income range.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

None.

\section{Ordinary Income Tax}
\label{sec:ordincome}

\subsection*{Rules}

The US federal income tax is a progressive tax applied to taxable ordinary
income (adjusted gross income, or AGI, minus deductions).
Reference: IRS Publication~17 \cite{irs17}.
Under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA, signed July~4, 2025), seven
marginal brackets apply at rates of 10\%, 12\%, 22\%, 24\%, 32\%, 35\%,
and 37\%.
Standard deductions for 2026 under the OBBBA are \$16,100 (single) and
\$32,200 (MFJ).
An additional deduction of \$2,050 (single) or \$1,650 (MFJ) per eligible
individual applies at age~65 or older.

OBBBA~§~1002 provides an extra \$6,000 per-person senior bonus deduction
for taxpayers aged~65 or older, phasing out at \$6 per \$100 of MAGI above
\$75,000 (single) or \$150,000 (MFJ) and fully phased out at \$175k/\$250k.
This bonus expires after tax year 2028.
Reference: \cite{obbba2025}.

Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs) are mandatory annual withdrawals from
tax-deferred accounts, beginning at age~70 for those born before 1949,
age~72 for those born 1949--1950, age~73 for those born 1951--1959, and
age~75 for those born in 1960 or later (effective 2033), per SECURE~2.0 Act~§~107
\cite{secure2}.
RMD fractions are taken from the IRS Uniform Lifetime Table~III (effective
2022), as published in IRS Publication~590-B \cite{irs590b}, except when the
sole designated beneficiary is a spouse more than ten years younger, in which
case IRS Table~II (Joint and Last Survivor) applies.
Roth accounts are exempt from RMDs.
Filing status transitions from MFJ to single in the calendar year of the
first spouse's death.

Owl can also model a future reversion to pre-OBBBA tax rates
(10\%, 15\%, 25\%, 28\%, 33\%, 35\%, 39.6\%) from a user-specified calendar
year (\code{yOBBBA} parameter), enabling scenario analysis of potential
future tax law changes.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}

Ordinary income is allocated across the seven tax brackets, with bracket
widths and rates updated each year for inflation and for the OBBBA sunset
schedule.
RMD fractions are computed per individual and year from the IRS Uniform
Lifetime Table.
The OBBBA~65+ bonus deduction phaseout is computed from the current MAGI
and refined at each solver pass.
Reference: \cite{irs17,secure2,obbba2025}.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

State income taxes are modeled separately; see the next section.
Itemized deductions are not modeled; only the standard deduction (plus the
65+ and OBBBA bonus deductions) is applied.
The OBBBA~65+ bonus deduction phaseout is computed outside the LP (as a
post-solution update) because it is a nonlinear function of MAGI.
The IRS Uniform Lifetime Table III is used for standard cases.
When the spouse is the sole designated beneficiary and more than ten years younger
than the account owner, IRS Table II (Joint and Last Survivor, Pub.\ 590-B) is applied instead.
Inherited IRA and beneficiary RMD rules are not modeled.

\section{State Income Tax}

\subsection*{Rules}
Most US states levy a personal income tax on top of the federal tax, each with
its own bracket structure, standard deduction, and filing statuses.
The treatment of retirement income varies widely: the majority of states exempt
Social Security benefits, several exempt all retirement income, and many others
offer capped exclusions for pension or retirement-account income.
A few states exempt Social Security only below an income threshold.

\subsection*{Implementation in Owl}
A plan may specify a two-letter state code.
Bracket rates and widths, standard deductions, Social Security treatment, and
retirement-income and pension exemption caps for all 50 states and the District
of Columbia are read from a data table (\code{data/taxes_state.toml}) with
separate entries for Single and Married Filing Jointly filers; the filing
status switches to Single at the passing of a spouse, and bracket widths and
deductions are indexed to inflation through $\gamma_n$.
State taxable income is derived from quantities already present in the model:
federal ordinary taxable income, plus capital gains (which most states tax as
ordinary income), minus the exempted portion of Social Security, pension
exemptions, the state standard deduction, and any capped retirement-income
exemption.
The resulting state tax joins the cash-flow constraint alongside its federal
counterpart.
The LP formulation is given in Sec.~(\ref{Sec:StateTax}).
States without an income tax simply generate no state-tax variables.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}
State AGI is approximated from federal quantities; state-specific additions and
subtractions beyond those listed above are not modeled.
Capital gains are taxed as ordinary income, which is accurate for most but not
all states.
States that exempt Social Security only below an AGI threshold (e.g., Kansas,
Missouri) are treated with a fixed exempt-or-taxed status rather than the
threshold rule.
Personal and senior credits are modeled where a state offers them, capped by the
year's state tax (Eq.~(\ref{Eq:StateCredit})); other state credits are not.
Local and county income taxes, and itemized deductions, are not modeled.

\chapter{Model Architecture and Solution Approach}
\label{Chap:Architecture}

\section{Overview}

Owl formulates retirement planning as a linear program (LP) or, when
binary variables are required, a mixed-integer program (MIP).
The plan is divided into annual time steps covering the entire household
planning horizon.
In each year, the optimizer simultaneously determines withdrawal amounts
from all accounts, Roth conversion amounts, and spending levels, subject to
tax laws, account balance dynamics, and user-specified constraints.
The resulting solution determines the optimal sequence of financial
decisions across the full horizon in a single solve---not year by year.

A complication arises because several tax quantities depend nonlinearly on
income, yet also influence the cash-flow balance that income must satisfy.
Owl resolves this circularity through a self-consistent (SC) iteration
loop: nonlinear quantities are fixed at their previous-iteration values
during each LP solve, then recomputed from the new solution, and the
process repeats until convergence.
Threshold quantities can instead be carried inside the optimization as binary
variables (MIP reformulations); the loop still iterates the quantities that remain
nonlinear in the solution.

\section{Planning Horizon and Time Steps}

The plan horizon runs from the current calendar year (year~0) through the
year following the last individual's projected death.
Each year index corresponds to one calendar year; all decision variables
are resolved at annual granularity.

For a single individual, the horizon spans the years from the plan start
through the assumed year of death, which is derived from a user-supplied
life expectancy or target age.
For married couples, the shorter-lived spouse's death marks the transition
from a joint plan to a survivor plan.
Both death years are inputs to Owl; they are treated as certain within a
given scenario.
Monte Carlo and historical stress-test simulations run the optimizer
repeatedly across many return scenarios.
When the longevity-risk option is enabled in stochastic spending
optimization (Chapter~\ref{chap:stochastic-spending}), ages at death are drawn for each scenario from a
selectable actuarial period life table (Section~\ref{sec:longevity}), so that
the planning horizon itself becomes a random variable.

\subsection*{The partial first year}

Every plan year runs from January~1, so year~0 is a full calendar year even
when the plan is built in the middle of one.
Balances, however, are known as of a date the user supplies---today by
default---and Owl reconciles the two by \emph{back-projecting} the balances to
January~1, dividing out the growth that the elapsed part of the year would have
produced at year-0 rates and allocations:
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:BackProject}
    b_{ij0} \;=\;
    \frac{\beta_{ij}}
         {1 + (1 - \varphi)\sum_k \alpha_{ijk0}\,\tau_{k0}},
    \qquad
    \varphi \;=\; 1 - \frac{d - 1}{365 + \ell},
\end{equation}
where $\beta_{ij}$ is the balance as entered, $d$ is the day of the year of the
balance date, $\ell$ is~1 in a leap year, and $\varphi$ is the fraction of the
year still ahead.
After a first-year loss the back-projected balance exceeds the balance entered,
as it should.

The back-projection removes \emph{growth} only.
It does not add back the money that has already moved through the accounts since
January~1, so year~0 counts a full year of spending, income and contributions
whatever the balance date.
The consequences run in opposite directions: spending already paid this year,
net of income already received, is charged again, which is conservative for a
retiree drawing down savings; contributions already made are deposited again,
which is generous for someone still accumulating.
On one retiree case the net effect of an October~1 date rather than January~1 is
a result about 2\% lower.

Two remedies are available, and neither requires a model change.
Entering January~1 balances---from a year-end statement---with a January~1 date
makes the first year exact.
Otherwise the first-year row of the Wages and Contributions table should carry
only the contributions, QCDs, and big-ticket items \emph{still to come}, since
those already made have already moved the balance that was entered; wages,
Social Security, and pensions stay at their full-year amounts, because
income received so far is what offsets the spending already paid.
Spending is the one flow that cannot be split this way, being an annual target
rather than a schedule.
To offset it, add to the taxable balance the spending and taxes paid since
January~1 \emph{minus} the wages, Social Security, and pensions received since
January~1: the net, not the spending alone, because the year-to-date income is
counted again too.

Because the balance date shifts every opening balance through $\varphi$,
re-stating balances must not silently re-date them: \code{setAccountBalances()}
leaves \code{startDate} unchanged unless it is given one explicitly.
Reports show the whole first year like any other, and the case summary adds the
row \textit{Net spending remaining in year}, $g_0\,\varphi$, which is what is
left to spend from the balance date on.

\section{Account Dynamics}

Each individual maintains up to four savings accounts: a taxable brokerage
account, a tax-deferred account, a Roth account, and a Health Savings Account (HSA).
A married couple therefore has up to eight accounts in total, tracked and
optimized jointly.

At the start of each plan year each account balance grows by the year's
investment return for that account's asset allocation.
During the year the following flows are applied:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item contributions from the Wages and Contributions table are deposited
    (modeled at mid-year so they earn half a year of return);
  \item withdrawals are made to fund spending, pay taxes, and cover other
    expenses including debt service and big-ticket items;
  \item Roth conversions transfer a chosen amount from the tax-deferred
    account to the Roth account, adding the converted amount to that
    year's ordinary taxable income;
  \item the taxable account generates a dividend and interest yield
    proportional to its balance, which is taxed as ordinary income or at
    preferential qualified-dividend rates;
  \item Required Minimum Distributions enforce a minimum withdrawal from
    each tax-deferred account once the owner reaches the applicable RMD
    starting age;
  \item any spending surplus---income exceeding expenses in a given
    year---is deposited back into the taxable account;
  \item HSA contributions are pre-tax (netted out of anticipated wages) and
    cease at Medicare enrollment;
    HSA withdrawals fund qualified medical expenses entirely tax-free and
    are not included in taxable income;
  \item at the death of the first spouse, a user-specified fraction
    of each account is transferred to the surviving spouse's
    corresponding account, reflecting beneficiary designations;
    the surviving spouse inherits the HSA intact at full tax-advantaged status.
\end{itemize}

The balance at the start of the following year is the result of all of
these flows.
All accounts are assumed to be rebalanced to their target asset allocation
at the end of each year.

\section{Asset Allocation and Glide Paths}

The target allocation of each account across asset classes (equities,
corporate bonds, Treasury notes, and cash) is specified by the user.
Allocation can be prescribed at three levels of granularity: per individual
per account, per individual across all accounts combined, or identically
for the entire household.

When the user specifies starting and ending allocation values, Owl
interpolates smoothly over the plan horizon using either a linear glide
path or an S-curve (hyperbolic tangent) glide path.
The S-curve produces a gradual, natural transition that accelerates through
the midpoint and tapers at both ends, which is often a more realistic model
of how investors de-risk their portfolios over time.
For couples, each individual's allocation glides independently over their
own planning horizon; the survivor's allocation therefore continues from
whatever value it had reached at the moment of the first spouse's death.

\section{Spending Profile and Objectives}
\label{sec:spending-profile}

\subsection*{Spending profile}

Net spending in each year follows a user-specified \emph{spending profile}
$\xi_n$ that scales a single level the optimizer chooses, the \emph{basis}~$g$:
real net spending in year $n$ is $g\,\xi_n$.
The profile encodes the desired shape of spending over time; the optimizer
determines the scale that is achievable given the household's resources and
constraints.

Two profiles are available.
A \emph{flat} profile holds real spending constant, $\xi_n = 1$.
A \emph{smile} profile spends more in early and late retirement and less in the
middle years, built as a cosine dip of amplitude $a$ (15\% by default) plus a
linear increase $b$ (12\% by default) over the horizon, optionally delayed by a
number of years:
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:SmileProfile}
    \xi_n \;\propto\; 1 + a\cos\!\left(\frac{2\pi (n - n_0)}{N_n - 1 - n_0}\right)
        + \frac{b\,(n - n_0)}{N_n - 1},
    \qquad n \ge n_0,
\end{equation}
held flat at its first value for $n < n_0$.
At the death of the first spouse, in year $n_d$, spending is multiplied by a
user-specified survivorship fraction $\phi$ reflecting the lower expenses of a
single person.

The smile profile is then \emph{normalized} so that
$\sum_n \xi_n = N_n - (1 - \phi)(N_n - n_d)$, the sum a flat profile with the
same survivorship would give.
This makes the basis profile-neutral: it is the \emph{average} of the yearly
amounts over the plan, not the first year's.
The distinction matters whenever the profile is not flat, since the two numbers
then differ, and the reporting surfaces do not all use the same one: the desired
net spending a user enters, and the spending axes of the trade-off and spending
optimization tools, are \emph{first-year} amounts, while the library keys and the
conversion-regret measure are in basis dollars.
The case summary reports both.
On a flat profile $\xi_0 = 1$ and the distinction disappears.

\subsection*{Primary objectives}

Two optimization objectives are supported:

\begin{description}[nosep, leftmargin=2em, style=nextline]
\item[Maximize net spending.]
  Subject to a desired bequest---which may be zero---the optimizer
  maximizes the sum of inflation-adjusted annual spending over the full
  plan horizon.
  This is the most common objective for retirees who want to sustain the
  highest possible standard of living.

\item[Maximize bequest.]
  Subject to a fixed annual spending level, the optimizer maximizes the
  after-tax estate value delivered to heirs at the end of the horizon.
  The heir's marginal income tax rate on inherited tax-deferred balances
  can be specified by the user.
\end{description}

\subsection*{Solver options for spending and bequest}
\label{sec:solver-options-objectives}

One option modifies how the primary objectives are formulated:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \code{timePreference} (\%/year, default~0) --- a subjective time-discount rate
    $\rho$ that front-loads spending.
    The objective weight for year $n$ is multiplied by
    $(1+\rho)^{-n}$, so near-term spending is valued more than equivalent
    spending in the distant future.
    At $\rho=0$ (the default) all years carry equal real weight.
\end{itemize}

A \code{fixedSpending} option, which pinned $g_0$ to a committed level under
\code{maxSpending}, has been withdrawn.
With the default zero profile slack, the spending-profile rows tie every $g_n$ to $g_0$,
so pinning $g_0$ fixes the whole spending vector.
The \code{maxSpending} objective is assembled solely from the $g_n$ coefficients and
therefore became \emph{constant} over the feasible set.
Since the bracket-fill variables $f_{t,n}$ are a relaxation of the progressive schedule,
tight only because the objective pushes ordinary income into the cheapest brackets, the
solver was then free to fill the top bracket while lower ones sat empty; the resulting
excess tax was absorbed by the estate at no cost to a constant objective.
Raising the slack does not repair this, because the upper edge of the slack band is itself
proportional to the pinned $g_0$, so the objective merely goes flat at saturation instead.

The well-posed replacement is to sweep the \code{bequest} floor under \code{maxSpending},
tracing the spending--bequest efficient frontier.
Every point of that sweep leaves $g_0$ free, so the objective retains a nonzero gradient and
prices the tax variables, and each point is a genuine optimum rather than an arbitrary
vertex of a degenerate program.

\subsection*{Lexicographic weight}

A small secondary weight can be added to the objective to break ties among
solutions with the same primary objective value.
By default, this weight mildly discourages unnecessary Roth conversions
(preferring fewer conversions when spending is otherwise unaffected) and
