mildly prefers drawing withdrawals from the first individual before the
second when both choices are equivalent.
The weight is small enough that it never overrides the primary objective.

\section{The Self-Consistent Iteration Loop}
\label{sec:selfconsistent}

Several quantities that enter the LP constraints are nonlinear functions of
the income variables being optimized.
The most important are:

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item the \emph{Social Security taxability fraction}---the share of SS
    benefits subject to ordinary income tax---which depends on provisional
    income, which in turn includes SS benefits themselves and all other
    income sources;
  \item \emph{Medicare IRMAA costs}, which depend on MAGI from two years
    prior and interact with the current year's spending and tax
    calculations;
  \item the \emph{Net Investment Income Tax (NIIT)}, which depends on MAGI
    and on investment income;
  \item the \emph{LTCG bracket upper bounds}, which depend on how much of
    each threshold is already occupied by ordinary income;
  \item the \emph{OBBBA~65+ senior bonus deduction phaseout}, which is a
    nonlinear function of MAGI;
  \item \emph{ACA marketplace premiums}, which depend on MAGI through the
    premium tax credit;
  \item the \emph{gain fractions} of the taxable accounts, when a cost basis is
    supplied, which depend on the withdrawals and deposits of earlier years.
\end{itemize}

Owl handles these nonlinearities through the SC loop.
At each iteration, all nonlinear quantities are held fixed at their values
from the previous iteration, making the problem an LP (or MIP).
The solver produces a new income solution; the nonlinear quantities are
recomputed from that solution and compared to their previous values.
The loop terminates when two conditions hold together: the objective has
settled within \code{absTol} or \code{relTol}, and the fixed-point residual,
the largest disagreement between a quantity the LP was built with and the value
the resulting plan implies (summed over the horizon in today's dollars, per tax
family), is at most \code{residualTol} per year.  An objective that has
settled is not enough on its own: an iterate whose own income implies different
premiums or a different taxable share of Social Security is not a fixed point.
Otherwise the updated values are substituted and the process repeats.

The loop also stops on three other conditions.  When the objectives fall into a
repeating cycle, the best iterate of the cycle is kept.  When branch-and-bound
solves keep timing out without improvement (three time-outs in eight
iterations), or when \code{maxIter} iterations have run, the iterate with the best
objective is kept, the first iteration excepted since its initial guesses
undercharge.  When an iterate cannot be solved at all, the loop falls back on the
\emph{most settled} earlier iterate, the one with the smallest residual, not the
richest: the fed-back costs start at nothing and catch up pass by pass, so the
best objective belongs to the iterate least charged for what its income brings.

To prevent oscillation near the SS taxability thresholds---where small
changes in income can flip the taxability fraction back and forth---the
updated SS taxability estimate is blended with the previous estimate using
a damped update, 30\% new and 70\% old.
The loop typically converges in fewer than fifteen iterations for
well-posed plans.

Six optional MIP formulations replace portions of the SC loop with binary
constraints, carrying the corresponding thresholds inside the optimization:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \code{withSSTaxability="optimize"} --- models SS taxability as an
    piecewise-linear MIP using binary variables $z^\sigma$ to select
    the correct taxability threshold (0\%, 50\%, or 85\%) each year.
  \item \code{withLTCG="optimize"} --- uses binary variables $z^l$ to select
    the correct 0\%, 15\%, or 20\% LTCG bracket each year, rather than
    approximating bracket thresholds from the previous SC iteration.
  \item \code{withNIIT="optimize"} --- uses a binary variable $z^j$ to
    select, each year, which term of the IRS minimum
    ($\min(\mathbb{G}-\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}},\,\mathbb{I}+Q)$) bounds the tax.
  \item \code{withACA="optimize"} --- folds ACA premium bracket selection and
    MAGI into the MIP, replacing the per-iteration SC approximation.
  \item \code{withMedicare="optimize"} --- models IRMAA bracket selection
    using binary variables $z^m$ to select each year's bracket.
  \item \code{withSeniorBonus="optimize"} --- carries the OBBBA senior-bonus
    phase-out, with one binary $z^{\mathrm{sb}}_n$ per bonus year marking it
    fully phased out (Section~\ref{Sec:SeniorBonus}).
\end{itemize}
The loop still runs in every mode: the investment income, the gain fractions,
and the senior deduction of a state that conforms to the federal one remain
nonlinear, so even a branch-and-bound solve of every family is not exact.
Each formulation removes the corresponding SC-loop approximation at the cost of
additional binary variables.
When multiple flags are active simultaneously, the resulting MILP can be large;
Section~\ref{sec:decomposition} describes the two strategies that solve it.

\section{Filing Status and the Death of a Spouse}

For married couples, the year of the first spouse's death is a known input
to the plan.
Beginning in that year, Owl automatically transitions all relevant
quantities from married-filing-jointly (MFJ) status to single status:

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item the standard deduction drops from the MFJ amount to the
    single-filer amount;
  \item tax bracket widths narrow to the single-filer schedule;
  \item Social Security taxability thresholds shift to the single-filer
    values;
  \item Medicare premiums reflect only the surviving spouse;
  \item the survivor begins receiving the survivor benefit computed at the
    year of the spouse's death;
  \item the spending profile is multiplied by the user-specified
    survivorship factor.
\end{itemize}

Because the death year is fixed at plan-construction time, this transition
requires no binary variables.
Separate parameter vectors are constructed for the MFJ period and the
single-survivor period; the LP selects the correct values automatically
for each year.

\section{Solvers}

Owl's optimization model is assembled in a solver-agnostic matrix form:
inequality constraints, equality constraints, variable bounds, and an
objective vector.
Any LP or MIP solver that accepts this standard input format can be used.

Two solvers are supported: \textbf{HiGHS}, a high-performance open-source LP
and MIP solver accessed directly via the \code{highspy} Python package, and
\textbf{MOSEK}, a commercial solver requiring a separate license, which
generally provides faster solution times for large MIP problems.
The default, \code{solver="default"}, selects MOSEK when it is installed and
licensed and HiGHS otherwise.

Two retries guard against verdicts HiGHS can get wrong.  A linear program that HiGHS
ends without a verdict (no status, an unknown status, a solve, presolve or postsolve
error, or ``unbounded or infeasible''), and without a feasible point, is solved once
more with the interior-point method and presolve off, which settles it either way;
whether HiGHS's default path stops there can turn on rounding in the last digits.
A mixed-integer program that HiGHS reports infeasible is retried, cheapest first,
with one presolve rule turned off and then with presolve off, before the verdict is
believed.

\section{MIP Strategies: Branch-and-Bound and Local Search}
\label{Sec:MIPDecomp}
\label{sec:decomposition}

When the \code{"optimize"} flags are active simultaneously---\code{withSSTaxability},
\code{withMedicare}, \code{withACA}, \code{withLTCG} and \code{withNIIT}---the MILP
carries on the order of 300 binary variables from the five bracket-selector families
($z^\sigma$, $z^m$, $z^a$, $z^l$, $z^j$), plus at most three senior-bonus binaries
$z^{\mathrm{sb}}$ under \code{withSeniorBonus}; with every tax mode in loop mode the
problem is a pure linear program. The option \code{mipStrategy} selects how those binaries are
solved within each iteration of the SC loop.

\paragraph*{Branch-and-bound} (\code{mipStrategy="branch-and-bound"}, the default) hands
the whole MILP to the solver's complete search. It stops when the relative gap between the
incumbent and the best bound falls below \code{gap}, or at \code{maxTime}, so its answer is
certified only to that gap. It is not exact in a wider sense either: it is solved inside the
SC loop, which still iterates the quantities that remain nonlinear in the solution
(investment income, gain fractions, the OBBBA phaseout unless \code{withSeniorBonus}
is optimized, and the senior deduction of a state that conforms to the federal one). Solve times grow quickly with the
binary count; on the bequest cases studied, a median of about six minutes per solve with
MOSEK, and HiGHS is far slower.

\paragraph*{Local search} (\code{mipStrategy="local-search"}) is a fix-and-optimize
heuristic. Within each SC iteration it solves a sequence of small restricted problems, each
with most binaries pinned:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item \emph{A feasible starting plan.} The previous iteration's binaries (an LP); failing
    that, a repair from the self-consistent loop's own plan, with its withdrawals and
    conversions held in a narrow band and the binaries free. The full MILP is never solved:
    if every attempt fails, the loop's plan is kept.
  \item \emph{Moves.} (a) $z^\sigma$ pinned, the other families freed one at a time;
    (b) the other families pinned, $z^\sigma$ free within a local-branching ball of at most
    $k$ flips from the current plan; (a$'$) when neither moved, the other families freed
    jointly; (c) every family's binaries freed in the years where the plan's own income
    implies a different cost than the one charged (the fixed-point residual). The senior-bonus
    binaries $z^{\mathrm{sb}}$, two or three in all, are never pinned: every restricted
    problem keeps them free.
  \item Repeat until no move improves the objective.
\end{enumerate}
Every restricted problem contains the current plan, so each is feasible and none returns a
worse plan: the search descends monotonically. Before searching, the plan is solved once in
loop mode; that plan seeds the search and is also its floor, so the result is never worse
than the loop's. Each restricted solve is capped by branch-and-bound nodes
(\code{localSearchStepNodes}; 3{,}000 with HiGHS, 20{,}000 with MOSEK, which count nodes
differently) rather than by time, so that the same case gives the same answer on any machine;
\code{localSearchStepTime} (60\,s) is only a backstop. The search has a budget
(\code{localSearchTime}, 300\,s), and $k$ is \code{localSearchRadius} (4). The preset
\code{breakpointMethod="local-search"} replaces a fixed Social Security taxable fraction with the
IRS formula: local search keeps the problem well behaved without it. Local search carries no certificate and can stop short of the optimum, chiefly on the
unrestricted problem, which has the most freedom. Claiming-age selectors
(\code{withSSAges="optimize"}) and withdrawal-ordering gates are not searched; such cases are
solved by branch-and-bound.

The preset \code{breakpointMethod = "local-search"} (or \code{"branch-and-bound"}) sets every
applicable family to \code{"optimize"} and selects the strategy in one step.

In tests of a prototype with HiGHS, on the shipped examples (with a benchmark ACA premium
added where someone is under 65), local search was never worse than the loop and improved
17 of 23 cases. Over 72 historical start years of a bequest-maximizing case swept over
first-year Roth conversions (1,261 cells), the cells whose regret came out negative---an
artifact of the solver, since a restricted plan cannot beat an unrestricted one---fell from
124 with the loop to 47 with local search, at a median of 28\,s per solve.

\chapter{Formulation with imposed asset allocation ratios}
\label{Chap:Formulation}
The indices, variables, and parameters this chapter uses are defined in
Appendix~\ref{Chap:Notation}, which is the notation key for everything that
follows; the reader meeting a symbol for the first time will find it there.

We first present the case where the sums of assets in each savings account $b_{ijn}$ are known
and for which we assume prescribed asset allocation ratios.
The amount in each asset class $k$ for $b_{ijkn}$ is simply obtained
from $\alpha_{ijkn} b_{ijn}$ in this case.
This formulation assumes that the accounts are always balanced. This is
a reasonable assumption given the auto-balancing feature offered by many financial service
providers and robo-advisers.

The benefit of this approach is that it has fewer variables and that only the sums of
all asset classes in each savings account need to be considered. The rate of return
of the account is then simply the product of the account balance with the sum of
the rates of return weighted according to the desired allocation ratio.
This approach allows us to eliminate $k$ by summing over it and rewrite
equations such as
\begin{eqnarray}
	\sum_k b_{ijk(n+1)} &=& \sum_k b_{ijkn} (1 + \tau_{kn}) + \ldots,
\end{eqnarray}
for the annual evolution of account balances from year $n$ to year $n+1$
as the simpler expression 
\begin{eqnarray}
	b_{ij(n+1)} &=& b_{ijn} \sum_k \alpha_{ijkn} (1 + \tau_{kn}) + \ldots ,\nonumber \\
		  &=& b_{ijn} (1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn}) + \ldots ,
\end{eqnarray}
where
\begin{eqnarray}
	\label{Eq:Tau1}
	\mathcal{T}_{ijn} &:=& \sum_k \alpha_{ijkn} \tau_{kn}.
\end{eqnarray}
It follows that the allocation ratios are normalized to unity, i.e.,
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:AlphaNorm}
	\sum_k \alpha_{ijkn} = 1.
\end{equation}
In this formulation where the $\alpha_{ijkn}$ are prescribed,
we will use $\mathcal{T}_{ijn}$ to
add the market returns to the savings balances.

\section{Constraints}
\subsection{Required minimum distributions (RMDs)}
	Withdrawals from the ($j=1$) tax-deferred savings accounts must be greater
	than or equal to the required minimum distributions, and therefore,
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:C1}
		w_{i1n} + \Xi_{in} -  \rho_{in}b_{i1n} \geq 0.
	\end{equation}
	The qualified charitable distribution $\Xi_{in}$ appears here because a QCD
	satisfies the RMD dollar for dollar, so it lowers the floor on the taxable
	withdrawal the plan is forced to take (Section~\ref{sec:qcd}).
	As $b_{ijn}$ are the balances at the beginning of year $n$, they are also the balances
	at December 31 of the previous year, which is the amount on which the IRS bases the RMDs.
	Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C1}) has to hold for each year $n$ and each individual $i$, and therefore, there
	are $N_i\times N_n$ such equations (although trivial when $\rho_{in} = 0$).
	These constraints avoid paying up to 25\% penalty
	on amounts not withdrawn when RMDs are required.
	Note that aggregate rules need to be considered separately as this approach only considers
	the sum of assets in a class with similar tax treatment (e.g., IRA and 401k).

\subsection{Qualified charitable distributions}
\label{sec:qcd}
	A qualified charitable distribution (QCD) is a transfer paid directly from a
	tax-deferred account to a qualified charity, authorized by IRC~\S408(d)(8).
	It is a prescribed parameter $\Xi_{in} \ge 0$ rather than a decision variable:
	how much to give is a preference of the household, not a quantity to optimize.
	It is worth modeling because it is not equivalent to withdrawing the money and
	then donating it, and it enters the formulation in four places.

	First, it is excluded from adjusted gross income. In this formulation that
	exclusion requires no term at all: $\Xi_{in}$ simply never appears in the
	taxable ordinary income of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:Tx2}), and it therefore propagates
	automatically to every quantity derived from it --- the state taxable income,
	the provisional income governing the taxability of Social Security, and the
	modified adjusted gross income driving IRMAA and the net investment income tax.
	This is a stronger benefit than a charitable deduction, which is unavailable to
	the majority of retirees who claim the standard exemption $\bar{\sigma}_n$.

	Second, it counts toward the required minimum distribution, which is why
	$\Xi_{in}$ appears in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C1}). A QCD large enough to cover the RMD
	releases the plan from taking any taxable distribution at all that year.

	Third, the money leaves the tax-deferred account, so $\Xi_{in}$ appears in the
	balance carryover Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C3b}) and in the withdrawal limit of
	Section~\ref{Sec:WithdrawalLimits}. A consequence worth noting is that a QCD
	shrinks the base on which later RMDs are computed.

	Fourth, it never becomes spendable cash, and so is absent from the net spending
	of Section~\ref{Sec:NetSpending}. This is what distinguishes $\Xi_{in}$ from a
	negative big-ticket item $\Lambda^-_{in}$, which is an after-tax outflow from
	the household budget.

	The freed capacity does not go to waste. Because the QCD removes ordinary income
	that the RMD would otherwise have forced, it opens room under the tax brackets,
	and an optimizing plan fills that room with a Roth conversion of the same size:
	taxable income is left essentially unchanged and the benefit accrues to the estate.

	Two statutory limits are enforced on the input rather than in the model.
	The donor must have reached age~70\textonehalf{} --- which is not the age at
	which RMDs begin --- and the amount is capped per person per year, indexed for
	inflation. Since the cap is published only a year in advance, later years carry
	the last published figure forward at a fixed assumed inflation rate. That rate is
	deliberately a constant rather than the scenario's own inflation: the cap
	validates user input, and a scenario-dependent bound would accept an entry on one
	Monte Carlo path and reject it on the next.

	Two eligibility rules are checked before the plan is built, and a violation is
	reported as an error rather than quietly adjusted. The owner must have reached
	age~70\textonehalf{} on or before the distribution, which is not the age at
	which required distributions begin; and the exclusion is capped per person per
	year, at \$115,000 for 2026, indexed for inflation. Because the IRS sets that
	figure only a year at a time, later years are projected forward at 3\% a year.

	Two simplifications are worth stating. A QCD must be paid from an IRA and not
	directly from a 401k, while this formulation aggregates both into a single
	tax-deferred account $j=1$ and so cannot distinguish them. This is less
	restrictive than it appears: rolling the employer plan over to an IRA makes the
	money eligible, and such a rollover is a non-event here, since both balances
	already occupy the same account. Ensuring it has been done is left to the user.
	The anti-abuse provision, under which deductible IRA contributions made after
	age~70\textonehalf{} cumulatively reduce the excludable amount, is not modeled.

\subsection{Income tax brackets}
	Taxable ordinary income is divided into tax brackets as defined in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:G_n}),
	and therefore
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:C2}
		0 \le f_{tn} \le \bar{\Delta}_{tn},
	\end{equation}
	where $\bar{\Delta}_{tn}$ is the inflation-adjusted income tax bracket width.

\subsection{Standard exemption}
	The standard exemption is constrained by
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:StdExemp}
		0 \le e_n \le \bar{\sigma}_n.
	\end{equation}
	Variable $e_n$ is required for accounting for years when the taxable ordinary
	income is smaller than the standard exemption.

\subsection{The OBBBA senior bonus and its phase-out}
\label{Sec:SeniorBonus}
	OBBBA~\S1002 adds \$6,000 to the deduction of each individual aged~65 or older,
	not indexed, shrinking by \$6 per \$100 of MAGI above a filing-status threshold
	and so vanishing \$100,000 above it (Section~\ref{sec:ordincome}).
	The phase-out makes the deduction a function of the solution, which the
	self-consistent loop can only handle by freezing it at the previous iterate's
	MAGI.
	A solve under that constant cannot trade income against the bonus, and will
	accept income the bonus would have sheltered.

	With \code{withSeniorBonus="optimize"} the phase-out is solved with the plan.
	Let $k_n$ be the number of individuals receiving the bonus in year $n$,
	$\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{sb}}_n$ the threshold of that year's filing status,
	$\Sigma = \$6{,}000$ the bonus, $r = 0.06$ the phase-out rate, and
	$\bar{\sigma}^{0}_n$ the standard exemption \emph{without} the bonus.
	A continuous variable $p_n$ carries the per-person reduction and a binary
	$z^{\mathrm{sb}}_n$ marks the year as fully phased out:
	\begin{eqnarray}
		e_n + k_n\,p_n &\le& \bar{\sigma}^{0}_n + k_n \Sigma, \label{Eq:SB1} \\
		p_n &\ge& r\,(\mathbb{G}_n - \mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{sb}}_n)
			- \mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{sb}}_n z^{\mathrm{sb}}_n, \label{Eq:SB2} \\
		p_n &\ge& \Sigma\, z^{\mathrm{sb}}_n, \label{Eq:SB3}
	\end{eqnarray}
	with $0 \le p_n \le \Sigma$ and
	$\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{sb}}_n = r\max(0, \bar{\mathcal{C}}_n -
	\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{sb}}_n) + \Sigma$.
	The reduction is a cost, so $p_n$ settles on its largest lower bound and the
	binary selects which bound that is: below the end of the phase-out,
	Eq.~(\ref{Eq:SB2}) with $z^{\mathrm{sb}}_n = 0$ is the cheaper branch; above it
	that row would demand more than $\Sigma$, which the column bound forbids, so
	$z^{\mathrm{sb}}_n = 1$ is the only feasible choice and Eq.~(\ref{Eq:SB3}) pins
	$p_n = \Sigma$.
	A small objective weight on $p_n$ settles ties in years whose cash is worth
	nothing to the objective.
	Because the deduction is now $\bar{\sigma}^{0}_n + k_n(\Sigma - p_n)$ rather than
	a constant, the term $k_n p_n$ joins $e_n$ wherever the deduction appears,
	including the capital-gains bracket rows of
	Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:LTCGded15})--(\ref{Eq:LTCGded20}).
	The MAGI here is the AGI basis of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MAGI}), shared with the NIIT.

\subsection{Withdrawal limits}
\label{Sec:WithdrawalLimits}
	We introduce another set of constraints that might look unnecessary, but can help
	convergence, and prevent overdrafts during the year of passing of spouse $i_d$.
	As withdrawals and conversions are at the beginning of the year 
	we impose that
	\begin{equation}
		w_{ijn} + \delta_{j, 1} \left( x_{in} + \Xi_{in} \right) \le b_{ijn},
	\end{equation}
	which just states that account balances need to be at least as large as withdrawals,
	possible Roth conversions, and any qualified charitable distribution, all of which
	are drawn from the same account.

\subsection{Withdrawal ordering}
\label{Sec:WithdrawalOrdering}
	By default, the optimizer is free to choose the order in which accounts are
	drawn down.
	When \texttt{withdrawalOrder="taxable\_first"} is selected, the conventional
	ordering --- taxable first, then tax-deferred, then Roth --- is enforced
	instead, modeling the naive strategy a hand-managed plan would follow; it
	serves as a baseline policy when quantifying the value added by the
	optimization.
	Two household-level binary gates per year, $z^o_{0n}$ and $z^o_{1n}$,
	indicate that the taxable and the tax-deferred balances, respectively, are
	exhausted at the end of year $n$.
	Withdrawals beyond what the gates allow are blocked through
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:WdOrderGates}
		w_{i1n} - \rho_{in}\,b_{i1n} &\le& \mathcal{M}^{d}_n\, z^o_{0n}, \nonumber \\
		w_{i2n} &\le& \mathcal{M}^{r}_n\, z^o_{1n},
	\end{eqnarray}
	so that, while the taxable gate is closed ($z^o_{0n}=0$), the tax-deferred
	withdrawal is pinned to exactly the required minimum distribution by
	Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C1}), and no Roth withdrawal can occur while the tax-deferred
	gate is closed.
	A gate may open only when the corresponding household balance is exhausted
	at the end of the year,
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:WdOrderExhaust}
		\sum_i b_{i0(n+1)} &\le& \mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{tx}}_{n+1}\,(1 - z^o_{0n}), \nonumber \\
		\sum_i b_{i1(n+1)} &\le& \mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{td}}_{n+1}\,(1 - z^o_{1n}),
	\end{eqnarray}
	and the full ordering follows from the monotonicity condition
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:WdOrderMono}
		z^o_{1n} \le z^o_{0n}.
	\end{equation}
	Each big-$\mathcal{M}$ bounds only what its row gates:
	$\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{tx}}_n$ and $\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{td}}_n$ bound the household taxable and
	tax-deferred balances at the start of year $n$, and
	$\mathcal{M}^{d}_n$ and $\mathcal{M}^{r}_n$ bound a year-$n$ tax-deferred and Roth withdrawal.
	They follow the one-way paths money can take between accounts
	(tax-deferred to Roth by conversion, any account to taxable as a surplus), compounding
	the starting balances, the contributions, and every fixed income that could reach taxable
	at the best return of the accounts on each path, and are then doubled so that they never bind.
	A bound built on the whole portfolio at the best return of any account runs to hundreds of
	times the real portfolio over a long plan, which HiGHS does not resolve reliably next to
	dollar coefficients (issue \#178).
	HSA withdrawals (qualified medical expenses) are not gated.
	Because surplus deposits land in the taxable account at year end, a year
	cannot both deposit a surplus and claim the taxable-exhausted gate, which
	closes the loophole of draining an account and immediately redepositing.

\subsection{Posthumous account activities}
	For cases with spouses, no withdrawal, Roth conversion, or deposit should
	occur in the accounts of the passed individual $i_d$:
	\begin{eqnarray}
		w_{i_djn} &=& 0,\nonumber\\
		d_{i_dn} &=& 0, \nonumber \\
		x_{i_dn} &=& 0, \nonumber \\
		&& \quad \forall j \in \{0, \ldots, N_j-1\} \nonumber \\
		&& \quad \forall n \in \{n_d, \ldots, N_n-1\}.
	\end{eqnarray}

\subsection{Roth conversions}
	Roth conversions $x_{in}$ are forced to zero in the last two years of
	each individual's horizon (see Section~\ref{sec:roth}).
	Otherwise, Roth conversions cannot be larger than the balance at the beginning of the year in the account:
	\begin{equation}
		x_{in} \le b_{i1n}.
	\end{equation}
	This constraint, however, is naturally satisfied when $b_{ijn} \ge 0$ non-negativity bounds are enforced.
	Additional maximum Roth conversion constraints $x_{max}$ can be imposed by the user.
	For a single individual, the previous equation becomes
	\begin{equation}
		x_{in} \le \min(b_{i1n}, x_{max}).
	\end{equation}
	For couples, the cap applies to each individual's conversions:
	\begin{equation}
		x_{in} \le x_{max}, \quad i \in \{0,1\}.
	\end{equation}
	As these equations involve a variable and a parameter in the $\min$ function,
	they are coded as separate constraints.

\subsection{Minimum taxable balance}
	To maintain liquidity throughout the plan, the user may impose a lower
	bound $\beta^{\min}_i$ (in today's dollars) on the taxable account balance
	of each individual.
	The inflation-adjusted floor is enforced from year~1 through the end
	of the individual's life horizon:
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:MinTaxBal}
		b_{i0n} \ge \beta^{\min}_i\,\gamma_n,
		\qquad n \in \{1, \ldots, \mathrm{horizon}_i - 1\}.
	\end{equation}
	This is controlled by the \code{minTaxableBalance} option.
	The constraint is omitted when \code{minTaxableBalance} is not specified.

\subsection{Initial balances}
	The initial balances $\beta_{ij}$ are one of the main inputs of the model.
	The initial savings account balances are imposed through the constraints
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:InitialBalance}
		b_{ij0} = \beta_{ij}.
	\end{eqnarray}
	At this point, we assume that all accounts are balanced according to the desired
	allocation ratios $\alpha_{ijk0}$.

\subsection{Roth 5-year maturation and conversion ladder}
\label{Sec:RothMaturation}
	Two IRS rules govern penalty-free access to Roth funds.
	(1) Roth \emph{contributions} can always be withdrawn tax- and penalty-free.
	(2) Roth \emph{conversions} can be withdrawn penalty-free only after a 5-year holding period,
	with each conversion carrying its own clock; \emph{earnings} additionally require age 59½.
	The two constraints below together allow only penalty-free Roth withdrawals, so no 10\%
	early-withdrawal penalty is assessed on $w_{i2n}$.

	The maturation constraint (row group $I_8$ in the matrix appendix, tag
	\code{roth_maturation}) enforces this rule:
	\begin{equation}
		b_{i2n} - w_{i2n} \;\geq\;
		\sum_{k=1}^{4} \Gamma_k\, x_{i,n-k}
		+ \sum_{k=1}^{5} \max(0,\,\Gamma_k - 1)\,\kappa_{i2,n-k},
		\quad \Gamma_k = \prod_{\ell=1}^{k}(1+\mathcal{T}_{i2,n-\ell}),
	\end{equation}
	forcing conversions still in their period and recent contribution gains to remain in the
	account.  A conversion's period is five taxable years, the year of the conversion and the
	four after it (IRC~§~408A(d)(3)(F)): a year-$m$ conversion enters the balance in $m+1$, is
	held through $m+4$, and is free in $m+5$.  Contribution gains are held five years.
	$\Gamma_k$ follows the account's real returns, losses included, since the carryover rows
	grow the balance at those returns and a conversion can never be worth more than that;
	flooring a loss at zero growth asked the account to retain more than its recent conversions
	were worth, which on a Roth holding only those left no conversion feasible at all.
	A contribution is retained for its gains only, and after a loss it has none.
	Historical amounts ($n-k < 0$) use a conservative 10\%/yr assumption.

	Before 59½ a second constraint (tag \code{roth_early_basis}) keeps earnings in the account.
	A Roth distribution comes from contributions first, then conversions, and earnings last
	(IRC~§~408A(d)(4)(B)), so for each individual and each year $n$ before the 59½ year,
	the first death, and the end of the horizon,
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:RothEarlyBasis}
		\sum_{m=0}^{n} w_{i2m} \;\le\; b_{i20} + \sum_{m=0}^{n} \kappa_{i2m}
		+ \left(1 - \frac{\textrm{oppCostX}}{100}\right) \sum_{m=0}^{n-5} x_{im}.
	\end{equation}
	Only conversions past their period count: one still within it is charged the 10\% when it
	comes out before 59½, and by the ordering rules it is what a withdrawal beyond the matured
	ones takes first.  Counting it would let a year convert $X$ and withdraw $X$ from a Roth
	holding only earnings, at no charge.  Early earnings are forbidden rather than taxed,
	conservatively, like the maturation rows.  The whole starting balance $b_{i20}$ counts as
	contributions and conversions (Owl has no input for its basis); the rows stop at the first
	death, since a surviving spouse can keep the deceased's Roth as an inherited Roth, exempt
	from the 10\%; and Roth~401(k) money is treated as a Roth IRA's.

	This constraint enables the \emph{Roth conversion ladder}, a strategy popular in the
	FIRE (Financial Independence, Retire Early) community.  An early retiree converts
	tax-deferred funds in years $n, n+1, \ldots$ and, 5 years later, withdraws the
	corresponding Roth amounts penalty-free.  The optimizer discovers this strategy
	automatically; the post-solve restoration of the conversion/withdrawal exclusion leaves the
	pre-59½ years untouched so as not to dismantle it (see the AMO discussion below).

\subsection{Cash-flow surplus}
	When both spouses are alive, surplus $s_n$ gets deposited in the taxable accounts
	according to variable $\eta$ as described in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:eta}),
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:eta2}
		d_{in} = [\delta_{i, 0} (1 - \eta) + \delta_{i, 1}\eta] s_n .
	\end{equation}
	Otherwise, for $n \ge n_d$, variable $\eta$ gets redefined as $\eta = \delta_{1, i_s}$.
	With \code{spousal_surplus_deposit_fraction = "optimize"}, the split is left to the
	optimizer while both spouses live: Eq.~(\ref{Eq:eta2}) is replaced for $n < n_d$ by the
	single row
	\begin{equation}
		d_{0n} + d_{1n} = s_n,
	\end{equation}
	so that each year's surplus can go to either taxable account, for instance to the one
	carrying fewer unrealized gains.  A tie-break (Section~\ref{sec:lexicographic}) deposits
	with the first individual where the split does not matter.  With beneficiary fractions
	below~1 this also decides how much of the first spouse's estate reaches the other heirs:
	the optimizer then deposits with the survivor, and a warning recommends a fixed fraction
	depositing all surplus with the first spouse to pass.
	Surplus can be caused by large influx of money coming from big-ticket items, compulsory RMDs,
	or the disposition of fixed assets.
	The \code{noLateSurplus} option pins surplus to zero in the final two plan years.
	This is useful because during market downturns, large sums can otherwise be recycled
	through the taxable account in those years with little tax or growth consequence,
	since the long-term compounding benefit of such deposits vanishes near the end of the plan.

\subsection{Account balances}
	Contributions are assumed to be made at half-year to better represent periodic contributions
	made throughout the year. As we already mentioned,
	the account balance at the end of a year is the same as the balance
	at the beginning of the following year.
	Changes include contributions $\kappa$, distributions and withdrawals $w$,
	conversions $x$, surplus deposits $d$, and growth $\tau$ on the account through the year.
	For each spouse $i$, we track each savings account $j$ separately, and tax-deferred accounts
	are coupled with the corresponding tax-free account through Roth conversions.

	The timing of Roth conversions, withdrawals, and deposits brings
	additional coupling between these variables, and is worth a detailed discussion.
	First, the financial aspects, and then the algorithmic ones.
	For the former, some financial advisors would recommend making Roth
	conversions at the beginning of the year, while making withdrawals
	at the end. Obviously, financial simulators would always yield higher numbers
	when using this scenario, as the money needed to pay the regular bills 
	stayed in the bank until the end of the year. More realistically,
	however, it would be more accurate to assume withdrawals at mid-year,
	to better represent evenly distributed withdrawals. So, financially,
	conversions at the beginning of the year, and withdrawals at mid-year
	make good sense. Conversions are also typically best when
	timed with market downturns, which are obviously not always at the
	beginning of the year.

	Now, let's look at the optimization side of these transactions.
	During years of positive returns,
	a direct withdrawal from the tax-deferred account at mid-year will always
	be unfavorable when compared to a Roth conversion
	at the beginning of the year, followed
	by a tax-free withdrawal later in the same year.
	This is because the second
	scenario involves gains which are tax-free over the half-year, while
	the first one does not. Moving account withdrawals at the beginning
	of the year, and the conversions in mid-year can only solve part of this artificial bias
	under specific conditions.

\paragraph*{Mutually exclusive transactions.}
\label{Sec:AMO}
	Moving all transactions at the beginning of the year can also cause undesired transactions. 
	To solve these spurious scenarios, it would be desirable to make the following at-most-one
	(AMO) exclusions between surplus $s_n$, withdrawals $w_{ijn}$, and conversions $x_{in}$:
	\begin{eqnarray*}
		\left(\sum_i w_{i0n} + \sum_i w_{i2n}\right) &\texttt{AMO} & s_n, \\
		\sum_i x_{in} &\texttt{AMO} & \sum_i w_{i2n},
	\end{eqnarray*}
	for $j \neq 1$, i.e., for all withdrawals except those from tax-deferred accounts.
	The AMO relation can also be represented by a logical NAND operator.
	The first exclusion prevents surplus deposits when withdrawals from taxable or tax-free accounts occur
	from either spouse, while the second exclusion prevents simultaneous Roth conversions and tax-free withdrawals
	from either spouse.
	The natural way to impose these exclusions is with binary variables and big-$\mathcal{M}$
	constraints, one pair per exclusion per year. Owl did exactly that, and no longer does.
	The reason is worth setting out, because it says something about the objective rather
	than about the constraints.

	\paragraph*{Why the binaries were removed}
	Measured across every shipped example case under both solvers, the constrained optimum
	equals that of the relaxation: the exclusions never change what the plan is worth. They
	are symmetry breakers, selecting the legible member of a family of equally valued
	solutions. They are not free, however. Owl's objective distinguishes the three account
	types \emph{only} through their tax treatment, so a household with a zero marginal rate
	on both ordinary income and capital gains gains nothing from where a dollar sits. The
	objective then goes flat across a combinatorial set of plans, and branch-and-bound loses
	the ability to prune: it holds the optimal value at the root node and spends the rest of
	its time proving no tie beats it. The effect is severe. A single retiree with about
	\$119{,}000 of savings, no pension, and Social Security of \$200 per month solves in
	0.05 seconds without the binaries and took 577 seconds with them.

	Two details of that pathology are worth recording. First, a loose $\mathcal{M}$ is not
	the cause: tightening it by two orders of magnitude changes the solve time by a quarter,
	because the bound was already exact and the failure is discrimination rather than
	tightness. Second, zero federal tax is necessary but not sufficient. The same household
	in a state with no income tax solves in 0.06 seconds; it is the state deduction and
	exemption variables, free to move when there is no tax to pay, that widen the flat face
	far enough to matter. These exclusions were also the only binary family a default plan
	carried, so removing them turns the default problem into a pure linear program.

	\paragraph*{Restoring the exclusions after the solve}
	Both properties are re-established on the solved vector instead. For the Roth exclusion,
	a year $n \ge n_{i,59\frac{1}{2}}$ carrying both a conversion and a tax-free withdrawal
	is rewritten with $m = \min(x_{in},\, w_{i2n})$ as
	\begin{alignat}{2}
		\label{Eq:Binary2}
		x_{in} &\;\rightarrow\; x_{in} - m, \qquad
		w_{i2n} &\;\rightarrow\; w_{i2n} - m, \qquad
		w_{i1n} &\;\rightarrow\; w_{i1n} + m.
	\end{alignat}
	Every affected row is invariant rather than merely slack. The two balance carryovers
	cancel, the second of them provided $\bar{x} = 1$; taxable income is unchanged because a
	conversion and a tax-deferred withdrawal are both fully taxable; net cash is unchanged
	once the ten-percent early-withdrawal penalty is absent, which is what confines the
	substitution to $n \ge n_{i,59\frac{1}{2}}$; and the tax-deferred withdrawal limit
	$b_{i1n} - w_{i1n} - x_{in} \ge 0$ is invariant term by term. The RMD floor, the five-year
	Roth retainer and the remaining withdrawal limits only loosen. The substitution is
	therefore exact and needs no second solve.

	Confining it to $n \ge n_{i,59\frac{1}{2}}$ is not merely a technical requirement: before
	an individual reaches 59½ it is legitimate to withdraw mature five-year-old conversions
	while initiating new ladder rungs, so removing that overlap would dismantle the Roth
	conversion ladder. The pre-59½ years are left exactly as solved.

	For the surplus exclusion, note first that the surplus is deposited into the same taxable
	account a withdrawal may have just drawn from, so the gross figures describe a movement
	that does not occur. Reporting the two net of one another is an identity for the cash flow
	balance, since the surplus appears on its left-hand side and the deposit within the
	withdrawals on its right, and cancelling them removes the same amount from both. Balances,
	taxes, spending and the bequest are untouched. Only a sale that realizes no gain is
	netted. A sale at a gain is a real transaction, whatever the federal bracket: the gain
	sits in $Q_n$, in MAGI and in the state base, and the rebuy resets the cost basis from
	which every later year's gain fraction is computed. Selling and rebuying at no gain moves
	neither, so there the gross and the net describe the same plan. Whatever overlap survives is re-examined by a
	churn-minimizing re-solve of the same program with spending, the terminal balances and the
	conversion schedule pinned, which cannot change any reported objective and for which the
	incumbent is itself a feasible point.

	\paragraph*{What is not recovered}
	Three situations admit no exact substitution and are reported rather than removed:
	a non-zero opportunity cost on conversions, for which $\bar{x} \neq 1$ breaks the balance
	invariance; the \code{taxable_first} withdrawal ordering, whose gate pins tax-deferred
	withdrawals to the RMD and so forbids the substitution's larger withdrawal; and a couple
	in which one spouse converts while the other withdraws, since the original exclusion was
	a household-level row while the substitution is per individual. In each case the overlap
	is left in the reported plan, which costs nothing, rather than paid for out of the
	objective. Two vanishingly small preferences in the objective---for leaving a dollar in an
	account that is never taxed again, and for not withdrawing money only to bank it---keep
	the solvers from selecting arbitrarily among the remaining ties.

	Another approach could be to perform Roth conversions at mid-year, while withdrawals
	could be made at the beginning of the year, and surplus deposits,
	if needed due to RMDs or receiving large sums of money,
	could be made at the end of the year. Let's formulate this approach
	in more detail and investigate for potential problems.
	Timing controls which terms get multiplied by the rate of return $(1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn})$.
	Therefore, our current choice would yield
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:C3a}
		b_{ij(n+1)} &=& [b_{ijn} - w_{ijn} + 0.5\kappa_{ijn}](1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn})
		+ [\delta_{j, 2} - \delta_{j, 1} ]x_{in} (1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn}/2)
		\nonumber \\
		&& 
		+\ \delta_{j, 0} d_{in} + 0.5 \kappa_{ijn},
	\end{eqnarray}
	where we use discrete Kronecker $\delta$ functions for selecting the specific accounts involved
	in Roth conversions. These conversions are made such that asset allocation
	ratios in the sending and receiving accounts are unchanged.

	Bringing all variables
	to the left-hand side, this gets rewritten as
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:C3}
		b_{ij(n+1)} - (b_{ijn} - w_{ijn}) (1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn})
		&& \nonumber \\
		-\ [\delta_{j, 2} - \delta_{j, 1}]x_{in}(1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn}/2)
		-\ \delta_{j, 0} d_{in}
		&=& \kappa_{ijn} (1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn}/2).
	\end{eqnarray}

	When $j=0$, this equation introduces
	a path to shelter negative returns by performing an over-withdrawal from the taxable
	account at the beginning of the year followed by a deposit in the
	same account at the end of the year. This can 
	be removed by making these events exclusive. Owl handles the same round-trip after the
	solve instead, by reporting the withdrawal net of the deposit that returns to the account
	(Eq.~(\ref{Eq:Binary2}) and the discussion around it), which avoids the binary variable
	and the branch-and-bound cost that comes with it.

	A much simpler approach, while not so natural,
	is to move all transactions to be synchronous at the beginning or at the end of the year
	thus avoiding undesirable movements of funds.
	If we select the beginning of the year, except for contributions, this leads to
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:C3b}
		b_{ij(n+1)}
		% && \nonumber \\
		- \ [b_{ijn} + \delta_{j, 0} d_{in} - w_{ijn} + (\delta_{j, 2} - \delta_{j, 1})x_{in}
		- \delta_{j, 1}\Xi_{in}]
		(1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn})
		&=& \kappa_{ijn} (1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn}/2).
		\nonumber
	\end{eqnarray}
The term in $\Xi_{in}$ removes any qualified charitable distribution from the
tax-deferred account, on the same timing as a withdrawal (Section~\ref{sec:qcd}).
Two further terms are left out of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C3b}) for clarity: with an opportunity cost
on conversions (option \code{oppCostX}), only the fraction $1 - \textrm{oppCostX}/100$ of $x_{in}$
reaches the Roth account, and a SPIA premium is taken from the tax-deferred balance in its
purchase year (Section~\ref{sec:spia}).
This is the current approach used in Owl.  Simultaneous deposits and withdrawals, and
	simultaneous Roth conversions and tax-free withdrawals, are not excluded by constraints;
	they are restored after the solve (Section~\ref{Sec:AMO}).

\subsection{Net spending}
\label{Sec:NetSpending}
	For calculating the net spending $g_n$, we consider the cash flow of all withdrawals,
	wages, other ordinary income, Social Security and pension benefits, proceeds from liquidation of fixed assets, and big-ticket items.
	Then we subtract potential surplus $s_{n}$ and all taxes (federal, state, and payroll), penalties, debts,
	Medicare premiums, and ACA premiums paid:
	\begin{eqnarray}
		g_n &=& \sum_i [\omega_{in} + \upsilon_{in} + \nu_{in} + \bar{\zeta}_{in} + \bar{\pi}_{in} + \bar{a}_{in} ]
		+ \sum_{i,j} w_{ijn} + \sum_i \Lambda^\pm_{in} + \sum_{*=x,c,f} \mathcal{A}_n^*  \nonumber \\
		&& - s_{n} - P_n - T_n - U_n - J_n - T^{s}_n - m_n - m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n - \mathcal{D}_n - \mathcal{F}_n,
	\end{eqnarray}
	where $T^{s}_n = \sum_t \theta^{s}_{tn} f^{s}_{tn} - c^{s}_n$ is the state income tax net of
	any personal credit (Section~\ref{Sec:StateTax}), and $\mathcal{F}_n$ the employee payroll tax.
	Here $m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ is the ACA net premium cost.
	It is a decision variable when \code{withACA="optimize"},
	or a fixed SC-loop parameter when \code{withACA="loop"}.
	Notice how big-ticket items $\Lambda^\pm$ contribute directly to the cash flow.
	Qualified charitable distributions $\Xi_{in}$ are deliberately absent: the money moves
	from the tax-deferred account to the charity without ever reaching the household,
	so it funds no spending (Section~\ref{sec:qcd}).
	Replacing intermediate variables and bringing all variables to the left-hand side, we get
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:C4}
		g_n - \sum_{i,j} w_{ijn} + 0.1 \sum_i (1-\mathcal{H}(n - n_{i,59\frac{1}{2}})) w_{i1n}\nonumber\\
                + m_n + m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n + s_n + \sum_{t=0}^{N_t-1} f_{tn} \theta^x_{tn} + U_n
                + \sum_t \theta^{s}_{tn} f^{s}_{tn} - c^{s}_n
		&=& \sum_{*=x,c,f} \mathcal{A}^*_n + \sum_i \Lambda^\pm_{in} \nonumber\\
                && + \sum_i [\omega_{in} + \upsilon_{in} + \nu_{in} + \bar{\zeta}_{in} + \bar{\pi}_{in} + \bar{a}_{in} ] \nonumber \\
                && - \mathcal{D}_n - \mathcal{F}_n - J_n.
	\end{eqnarray}
	When \code{withACA="loop"}, $m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ is not an LP variable; the ACA cost is
	computed in the SC loop and subtracted on the RHS (omitted from the equation above for clarity).
	Notice that we do not consider market losses or tax-loss harvesting,
	and we do not track individual stock purchases over the years.
	We also treat $J_n$ as a parameter as it is computed through a self-consistent loop,
	unless \code{withNIIT="optimize"} makes it a variable (Section~\ref{Sec:NIIT}).

	We want the net spending to be predictable and smooth. For that purpose, we use
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:C5a}
	g_{n}/\bar{\xi}_{n} = g_0/\bar{\xi}_0,
\end{equation}
where the net spending is adjusted for inflation and where we use the time series of parameter $\xi_n$,
allowing for additional adjustments to the overall desired spending.
Note that $\bar{\xi}_0 = \xi_0$ as $\gamma_0=1$.
This profile is used to lower the desired net spending amount by a reduction factor $\chi$
after the passing of one spouse and/or to allow for more realistic spending profiles, such as
the {\em smile} profile described above.
Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C5a}) can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:C5}
	g_n \xi_0 - g_0 \bar{\xi}_n = 0,
\end{equation}
for the constraints to be enforced. Once $g_0$ is determined, the whole time series of net spending
is determined.
When using slack variable $\lambda$, the spending profile is then implemented as inequality constraints
\begin{eqnarray}
	\label{Eq:lambda}
	g_n \bar{\xi}_0 - g_0 (1 - \lambda) \bar{\xi}_n &\geq&  0, \nonumber\\
	-g_n \bar{\xi}_0 + g_0 (1 + \lambda) \bar{\xi}_n &\geq&  0.
\end{eqnarray}
In this case, the objective function will need to consider the sum over all years when optimizing net spending.

\subsection{LTCG bracket constraints}
\label{Sec:LTCG}
	The LTCG tax $U_n = 0.15\,q_{1n} + 0.20\,q_{2n}$ is determined by three LP bracket
	allocation variables $q_{pn}$ ($p \in \{0,1,2\}$).
	The partition constraint
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:LTCG_partition}
		q_{0n} + q_{1n} + q_{2n} \ge Q_n
	\end{equation}
	ensures the allocations cover the total LTCG $Q_n$.
	The bracket upper-bound constraints prevent overflow into higher-rate brackets.
	Because LTCG is stacked on top of ordinary taxable income $G_n = \sum_{t=0}^{N_t-1} f_{tn}$,
	the room available in each bracket depends on how much of the threshold is already
	occupied by ordinary income. The constraints can be expressed as
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:LTCG2}
		q_{0n} &\le& \max(0,\; \bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n - G_n), \\
		q_{0n} + q_{1n} &\le& \max(0,\; \bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n - G_n), \label{Eq:LTCG3}
	\end{eqnarray}
	where $\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n$ and $\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n$ are the inflation-adjusted total taxable income
	thresholds above which the 15\% and 20\% LTCG rates apply.
	In these constraints, $G_n$ is taken from the solution of the previous SC loop
	iteration (initialized to zero on the first pass).
	These constraints converge together with the SC loop.
	Since the cost is convex ($0\% < 15\% < 20\%$), no binary variables are needed:
	the LP naturally allocates gains to the cheapest bracket first.

	When ordinary income does not use up the standard deduction, the unused part reduces
	taxable gains, which widens the bracket room by that amount.  Writing
	$u_n = \max(0,\,\bar{\sigma}_n - G_n - e_n)$ for the unused deduction of the previous
	iterate, Owl adds, in years where $u_n > 0$,
	\begin{eqnarray}
		q_{0n} + e_n &\le& \max(0,\;\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n - G_n) + \bar{\sigma}_n, \label{Eq:LTCGded15} \\
		q_{0n} + q_{1n} + e_n &\le& \max(0,\;\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n - G_n) + \bar{\sigma}_n, \label{Eq:LTCGded20}
	\end{eqnarray}
	alongside the plain bounds of Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:LTCG2})--(\ref{Eq:LTCG3}), whose right-hand
	sides are raised by $u_n$.
	Carrying $e_n$ on the left rather than $u_n$ on the right makes the extra room shrink by a
	dollar for every dollar of the deduction that ordinary income claims, so the widening
	cannot be double-counted.
	Where the senior bonus deduction is itself a decision variable, its term joins $e_n$ in
	these rows.

	When the option \code{withLTCG="optimize"} is selected, the bracket thresholds
	are no longer fixed from a previous iteration: $G_n$ itself becomes a continuous
	LP variable, and two binary variables $z^l_{0n},\,z^l_{1n} \in \{0,1\}$
	(per year $n$) indicate whether room exists below the 15\% and 20\% thresholds,
	respectively ($z^l = 1$ means room exists, $G_n$ is below the threshold).
	The big-$\mathcal{M}$ link constraints are
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n &\le& G_n + z^l_{0n}\,\mathcal{M}_n, \label{Eq:LTCGm1} \\
		G_n + z^l_{0n}\,\mathcal{M}_n &\le& \bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n + \mathcal{M}_n, \label{Eq:LTCGm2}
	\end{eqnarray}
	and analogously for $z^l_{1n}$ at threshold $\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n$,
	with $\mathcal{M}_n = \max(\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n,\,\bar{\mathcal{C}}_n - \bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n)$,
	which reaches from a threshold to anything ordinary income can be, in either direction.
	A monotonicity constraint
	\begin{equation}
		z^l_{0n} \le z^l_{1n}
	\end{equation}
	ensures consistency (if $G_n$ is below the 15\% threshold it is also below the 20\% threshold).
	The bracket room upper bounds then become linear:
	\begin{eqnarray}
		q_{0n} &\le& z^l_{0n}\,\mathcal{M}'_n, \label{Eq:LTCGmq1} \\
		q_{0n} + q_{1n} &\le& z^l_{1n}\,\mathcal{M}'_n, \label{Eq:LTCGmq2}
	\end{eqnarray}
	where $\mathcal{M}'_n = \max(\mathcal{M}_n,\,\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n + \bar{\sigma}_n)$ reaches
	past the 20\% threshold by the deduction, whose unused part widens the brackets.
	The rooms themselves are bounded, as in Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:LTCGded15})--(\ref{Eq:LTCGded20}) but
	with $G_n$ now a variable, by
	$q_{0n} + G_n + e_n \le \bar{\mathcal{B}}^{15}_n + \bar{\sigma}_n + \mathcal{M}_n(1 - z^l_{0n})$
	and
	$q_{0n} + q_{1n} + G_n + e_n \le \bar{\mathcal{B}}^{20}_n + \bar{\sigma}_n + \mathcal{M}_n(1 - z^l_{1n})$.
	Because the cost is still convex, the optimizer fills the cheapest bracket first,
	so no additional binary variables are needed for bracket ordering;
	only the bracket room bounds require binaries.

\subsection{Net investment income tax}
\label{Sec:NIIT}
	In the default SC-loop mode, the NIIT $J_n$ is derived from MAGI and
	investment income after each solver pass and held fixed for the next LP solve.
	When the option \code{withNIIT="optimize"} is selected, MAGI $\mathbb{G}_n$
	becomes a continuous LP variable \code{magi}$_n$, a binary variable
	$z^j_n \in \{0,1\}$ determines whether MAGI exceeds the statutory NIIT
	threshold $\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}}$, and $J_n$ becomes a continuous LP
	variable.

	\subsubsection*{One binary for the minimum}
	The IRS formula is
	$J_n = 0.038\,\max\!\bigl(0,\,\min(\mathbb{G}_n - \mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}},\;\mathbb{I}_n + Q_n)\bigr)$,
	where $\mathbb{I}_n + Q_n$ is net investment income (NII).
	High ordinary income (e.g.\ large RMDs or Roth conversions) can push
	$\mathbb{G}_n - \mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}}$ above NII, in which case the NIIT
	base is capped at NII; with large investment income, MAGI can instead exceed the
	threshold by less than NII, and the base is the excess.
	Since $J_n$ is a cost the optimizer minimizes, it settles on the largest of its
	lower bounds, and the binary $z^j_n$ selects which term of the minimum bounds it:
	\begin{eqnarray}
		J_n &\ge& 0.038\,(\mathbb{G}_n - \mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}}) - z^j_n\,\mathcal{M}^j_n, \label{Eq:NIIT_excess} \\
		J_n &\ge& 0.038\,(\mathbb{I}_n + \mathbb{G}_n - G_n - e_n) - (1-z^j_n)\,\mathcal{M}^j_n, \label{Eq:NIIT_nii}
	\end{eqnarray}
	with $J_n \ge 0$ and $\mathcal{M}^j_n = 0.038\,\max(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_n,\,\mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}})$,
	where $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_n$ bounds the year's income.
	The optimizer picks the branch with the smaller bound, so
	$J_n = 0.038\,\max\!\bigl(0,\,\min(\mathbb{G}_n - \mathbb{G}^{\mathrm{NIIT}},\,\mathrm{NII}_n)\bigr)$
	over every income range.
	Those two rows bound $J_n$ only from below, and rely on its being minimized.  Where a
	year's money is worth nothing to the objective the solver is free to overpay, so a fourth
	row caps the tax at 3.8\% of investment income whichever branch applies, with the same
	terms as Eq.~(\ref{Eq:NIIT_nii}) and no switch:
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:NIIT_cap}
		J_n \;\le\; 0.038\,(\mathbb{I}_n + \mathbb{G}_n - G_n - e_n).
	\end{equation}

	The $Q_n$ term of NII is written through MAGI because, on the AGI basis,
	$\mathbb{G}_n = G_n + e_n + Q_n$, so $\mathrm{NII}_n = \mathbb{I}_n + \mathbb{G}_n - G_n - e_n$,
	with $\mathbb{I}_n$ the SC-loop parameter.

	\subsubsection*{MAGI equality}
	An equality constraint links the NIIT MAGI (AGI basis) to ordinary income,
	dividends, and LTCG. Because $G_n + e_n$ already carries the taxable Social
	Security portion $R_n\sum_i\bar\zeta_{in}$, no additional Social Security term
	is added:
	\begin{equation}
		\mathbb{G}_n = G_n + Q_n + e_n.
		\label{Eq:MAGI}
	\end{equation}
	In the LP, $Q_n$ is represented directly by the LTCG bracket allocation
	variables $q^{(0)}_n + q^{(1)}_n + q^{(2)}_n$, which equal $Q_n$ when the
	partition lower-bound constraint (\ref{Eq:LTCG_partition}) is tight.
	The raw portfolio capital-gain LP expression must \emph{not} be substituted
	for $Q_n$ here: it evaluates to zero at the partition minimum and would
	remove $Q_n$ from MAGI, causing the NIIT to be computed as
	$0.038\,\mathbb{I}_n$ rather than $0.038\,(\mathbb{I}_n + Q_n)$.

\subsection{Taxable ordinary income}
	We connect the two definitions for $G_n$ stated above in Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:G_n}) and (\ref{Eq:Tx2}),
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\sum_{t=0}^{N_t-1} f_{tn} &=& \mathcal{A}^x_n +
		\sum_i [\omega_{in} + \upsilon_{in} + \nu_{in} + R_n\bar\zeta_{in} + \bar{\pi}_{in} + \bar{a}_{in}]  \nonumber \\
		&& + \sum_i [w_{i1n} + x_{in} ]
		\nonumber\\
		&& + \sum_{i,k\neq 0} [(b_{i0n} - w_{i0n} + d_{in}
		     + 0.5\kappa_{i0n})\alpha_{i0kn}\tau_{kn}] - e_n,
	\end{eqnarray}
	and rearrange to move variables to the LHS as follows
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:C6}
		e_n + \sum_{t=0}^{N_t-1} f_{tn} - l^{f}_n
		- \sum_i [ w_{i1n} + x_{in}] &&
		\nonumber \\
		- \sum_{i,k\neq 0} [(b_{i0n} - w_{i0n} + d_{in})\alpha_{i0kn}\tau_{kn}] &=&
		\mathcal{A}^x_n + \sum_i [\omega_{in} + \upsilon_{in} + \nu_{in} + R_n\bar\zeta_{in} + \bar{\pi}_{in} + \bar{a}_{in} ]
		\nonumber \\
		&& 		+ 0.5\sum_{i,k\neq 0} \alpha_{i0kn}\tau_{kn}\kappa_{i0n}.
	\end{eqnarray}
	A negative \textit{other inc} entry $\upsilon_{in}$ is an above-the-line deduction, and taxable
	income cannot go below zero, so the part of it the year's income cannot absorb is lost, not
	carried forward.  Variable $l^{f}_n$, with $0 \le l^{f}_n \le \sum_i \max(0, -\upsilon_{in})$,
	carries that loss; it exists only in cases with a negative entry, and a tie-break
	(Section~\ref{sec:lexicographic}) keeps it no larger than the year's income makes necessary.

\subsection{State income tax}
\label{Sec:StateTax}
	When the configured state levies an income tax, state ordinary income is
	allocated across the $N_s$ state brackets through variables $f^{s}_{tn}$,
	in direct analogy with Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C2}),
	\begin{equation}
		0 \le f^{s}_{tn} \le \bar{\Delta}^{s}_{tn},
	\end{equation}
	where $\bar{\Delta}^{s}_{tn}$ are the inflation-adjusted state bracket widths.
	Variable $e^{s}_n$ absorbs the state standard deduction through
	$0 \le e^{s}_n \le \bar{\sigma}^{s}_n$ and, when the state offers a
	retirement-income exemption, variable $r^{s}_n$, bounded by the
	inflation-adjusted per-person cap $\bar{c}^{\,r}_n$ as
	$0 \le r^{s}_n \le \bar{c}^{\,r}_n$, exempts eligible retirement income.
	State taxable income is tied to the federal quantities through the equality
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:StateTI}
		\sum_{t=0}^{N_s-1} f^{s}_{tn} + e^{s}_n + \sum_i r^{s}_{in}
		- \sum_{t=0}^{N_t-1} f_{tn} - e_n - \sum_{p=0}^{2} q_{pn}
		+ \mathbb{1}[\text{state excludes conversions}] \sum_i x_{in} - l^{s}_n
		= -E^{\sigma}_n - E^{\pi}_n,
	\end{equation}
	where the conversion term removes Roth conversions from state income in the states
	that leave them out at any age (PA, MS), and $l^{s}_n$, bounded like $l^{f}_n$, is the
	part of a negative \textit{other inc} the state base cannot absorb: a deduction can bring
	the state base below zero where the federal one is not, as when PA leaves out a
	conversion that federal income carries.
	The federal standard exemption $e_n$ is \emph{added back}: $\sum_t f_{tn}$ is federal
	taxable income, already net of the federal deduction, and a state that grants its own
	deduction $e^{s}_n$ must not also inherit the federal one.  Omitting this term subtracted
	the deduction twice and understated the state tax.
	The retirement-income exemption is per person: each $r^{s}_{in}$ is capped by the state
	amount and by that individual's own eligible income, and an unused amount does not
	transfer to a spouse.
	Here $E^{\sigma}_n = R_n\sum_i \bar\zeta_{in}$ (the variable $t^{\sigma}_n$ under
	\code{withSSTaxability="optimize"}) when the state does not tax
	Social Security --- removing the federally taxable portion embedded in
	Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C6}), with $R_n$ the self-consistent taxability ratio --- and
	$E^{\sigma}_n = 0$ otherwise, and where
	$E^{\pi}_n = \min\left(\sum_i \bar{\pi}_{in},\; N_i\,\bar{c}^{\,\pi}_n\right)$
	applies the state pension exemption with per-person cap $\bar{c}^{\,\pi}_n$.
	The retirement-income exemption cannot exceed the eligible income that
	individual actually received,
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:StateREcap}
		r^{s}_{in} \le w_{i1n}
			+ \mathbb{1}[\text{state exempts conversions}]\; x_{in}
			+ \mathbb{1}[\text{no separate pension exemption}]\; \bar{\pi}_{in}.
	\end{equation}
	Roth conversion income qualifies only where the state's rule admits it, and a pension
	shares the retirement exemption only in years whose state has no separate pension
	exemption of its own.
	A state that offers a personal or senior credit carries it as a variable $c^{s}_n$,
	which can only offset tax and so is capped by the year's state tax,
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:StateCredit}
		c^{s}_n \le \sum_{t} \theta^{s}_{tn} f^{s}_{tn}.
	\end{equation}
	Finally, the state tax net of the credit, $T^{s}_n = \sum_t \theta^{s}_{tn} f^{s}_{tn} - c^{s}_n$, with
	$\theta^{s}_{tn}$ the state marginal rates, is added to the tax terms of the
	cash-flow constraint Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C4}).

\subsection{Social Security taxability}
	Under IRS Publication~915, the fraction of Social Security benefits subject to income
	tax is determined by the \emph{provisional income} (PI), defined as
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:PI}
		\Pi_n = \mathbb{G}_n - \tfrac{1}{2}\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in}.
	\end{equation}
	The IRS piecewise formula gives the taxable SS amount as
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:TaxableSS}
		\mathcal{S}_n = \begin{cases}
			0 & \Pi_n < \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}, \\[4pt]
			0.5\,(\Pi_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}) & \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}} \le \Pi_n < \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}}, \\[4pt]
			\min\Bigl(0.85\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},\;
			      0.5\,(\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}}-\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}})
			      + 0.85\,(\Pi_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}})\Bigr) & \Pi_n \ge \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}},
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation}
	so that $R_n = \mathcal{S}_n\,/\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in} \in [0,\,0.85]$.

	Introducing the excess provisional income above each threshold,
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:plo_phi}
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n = \max(0,\,\Pi_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}_n),
		\qquad
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n = \max(0,\,\Pi_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}}_n),
	\end{equation}
	the three cases of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:TaxableSS}) collapse into the single expression
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:TaxableSSClosed}
		\mathcal{S}_n =
		\min\Bigl(0.85\!\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},\;
		      0.50\,\min\Bigl(\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},\,
		            \min(\Delta\mathcal{P},\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n)\Bigr)
		      + 0.85\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n\Bigr),
	\end{equation}
	where $\Delta\mathcal{P} = \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}} - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}$
	is the bracket width (\$12{,}000 for MFJ; \$9{,}000 for single filers).
	The inner $\min(\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},\,\cdot\,)$ guards against the unusual case
	$\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in} < \Delta\mathcal{P}$, in which SS benefits are too small to
	fill the 50\%-bracket entirely.
	Because the clipped excess $\min(\Delta\mathcal{P},\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n) \le \Delta\mathcal{P}$,
	whenever $\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in} \ge \Delta\mathcal{P}$ the inner $\min$ is
	automatically satisfied and Eq.~(\ref{Eq:TaxableSSClosed}) simplifies to
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:TaxableSSSimpl}
		\mathcal{S}_n =
		\min\Bigl(0.85\!\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},\;
		      0.5\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n + 0.85\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n\Bigr),
		\qquad
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n = \min(\Delta\mathcal{P},\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n).
	\end{equation}
	This condition holds for virtually all retirees, since combined annual SS benefits
	nearly always exceed \$12{,}000 (MFJ) or \$9{,}000 (single).

	Because $R_n$ enters the LP only as a constant multiplier of the fixed series
	$\bar{\zeta}_{in}$ on the right-hand side of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C6}), it is treated as a
	\emph{parameter} rather than a decision variable.
	It is updated between LP solves in the self-consistent (SC) loop:
	after each solve, $\mathbb{G}_n$ is computed from the current solution,
	$\Pi_n$ is evaluated, and $R_n$ is refreshed via Eq.~(\ref{Eq:TaxableSS}).
	A 30\% damping blend
	$R_n \leftarrow 0.3\,R_n^{\mathrm{new}} + 0.7\,R_n$
	prevents oscillation near the bracket boundaries.
	To avoid unnecessary LP rebuilds, $R_n$ is only updated when
	$\max_n|\Delta R_n| > 10^{-3}$.
	The SC loop terminates when the change in the objective function (net spending
	or bequest) falls below a prescribed tolerance.

	Alternatively, when the solver option \code{withSSTaxability="optimize"} is set,
	all max/min operations in
	Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:plo_phi})--(\ref{Eq:TaxableSSSimpl}) are encoded exactly within the
	LP as a mixed-integer program (MIP) using the continuous variables
	$p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n$, $p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n$, $p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n$, and
	$t^{\sigma}_n$, together with two binary variables $z^{\sigma}_{0n}$ and
	$z^{\sigma}_{1n}$ per year $n$ for which $\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in}>0$;
	in years with no Social Security income all four continuous variables and
	both binaries are trivially fixed to zero and excluded from the MIP enumeration.
	The non-negativity of $p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n$ and $p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n$
	combined with the lower-bound constraints
	$p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n \ge \Pi_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}_n$ and
	$p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n \ge \Pi_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}}_n$
	enforce the $\max(0,\cdot)$ operations; because maximizing spending minimizes taxes,
	the optimizer pushes these variables to their lower bounds at the optimum.
	The two $\min(\cdot,\cdot)$ operations in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:TaxableSSSimpl}) are
	formulated via big-$\mathcal{M}$ lower-bound constraints.
	For $p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n = \min(\Delta\mathcal{P}_n,\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n)$,
	the upper bounds
	\begin{equation}
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n \le \bar{p}_n,
		\qquad
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n \le p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n,
	\end{equation}
	with $\bar{p}_n = \min(\Delta\mathcal{P}_n,\,\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in})$, are complemented by
	binary-controlled lower bounds that force the optimizer to the tighter constraint:
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:BinQ}
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n \;\ge\; \bar{p}_n\,(1 - z^{\sigma}_{0n}),
		\qquad
		p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n - p^{\sigma,\mathrm{lo}}_n
		+ (\bar{\mathcal{C}}_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}_n)^+\,z^{\sigma}_{0n} \;\ge\; 0.
	\end{equation}
	Capping $\bar{p}_n$ at the benefit matters when the benefit is smaller than the tier's
	width: a lower bound of $\Delta\mathcal{P}_n$ would then exceed $p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n$'s
	reach and make the problem infeasible.
	Analogously, for
	$t^{\sigma}_n = \min\bigl(0.85\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},\;
	                            0.5\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n+0.85\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n\bigr)$:
	\begin{equation}
		t^{\sigma}_n \le 0.85\!\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in},
		\qquad
		t^{\sigma}_n \le 0.5\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n + 0.85\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n,
	\end{equation}
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:BinT}
		t^{\sigma}_n \;\ge\; 0.85\!\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in}\,(1 - z^{\sigma}_{1n}),
		\qquad
		t^{\sigma}_n - 0.5\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{min}}_n - 0.85\,p^{\sigma,\mathrm{hi}}_n
		  + \mathcal{M}^{\sigma}_n\,z^{\sigma}_{1n} \;\ge\; 0,
	\end{equation}
	with $\mathcal{M}^{\sigma}_n = 0.5\,\bar{p}_n + 0.85\,(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_n - \mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{hi}}_n)^+$.
	Each row is relaxed by exactly what it gates; a flat constant was thousands of times
	larger than any of these.
	In this formulation $t^{\sigma}_n$ is an LP variable rather than the fixed parameter
	$R_n\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in}$ used in SC-loop mode.
	Moving $t^{\sigma}_n$ to the left-hand side, Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C6}) becomes
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:C6prime}
		e_n + \sum_{t=0}^{N_t-1} f_{tn} - l^{f}_n
		- t^{\sigma}_n
		- \sum_i [ w_{i1n} + x_{in}] &&
		\nonumber \\
		- \sum_{i,k\neq 0} [(b_{i0n} - w_{i0n} + d_{in})\alpha_{i0kn}\tau_{kn}] &=&
		\mathcal{A}^x_n + \sum_i [\omega_{in} + \upsilon_{in} + \nu_{in} + \bar{\pi}_{in} + \bar{a}_{in} ]
		\nonumber \\
		&& + 0.5\sum_{i,k\neq 0} \alpha_{i0kn}\tau_{kn}\kappa_{i0n},
	\end{eqnarray}
	and $R_n = t^{\sigma}_n / \sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in}$ is recovered from the LP
	solution for reporting.
	There is no circular dependency: provisional income 	$\Pi_n$ is constructed from
	non-SS ordinary income, Roth conversions, $Q_n$ dividends, and the fixed
	half of SS ($0.5\sum_i\bar{\zeta}_{in}$), so $t^{\sigma}_n$ does not appear in
	$\Pi_n$.

\subsection{Medicare brackets and costs}
	Annual Medicare costs $m_n$ include the income-related monthly adjusted amount
	commonly known as IRMAA.
	As this additional adjustment
	is a piecewise constant function,
        it can be computed using binary variables and mixed-integer linear
	programming. In the current tax code, this adjustment
	depends on the modified adjusted gross income (MAGI) $\mathbb{G}$ from 2 years earlier. For the
	MAGI, we use the AGI basis $\mathbb{G}_{(n-2)} = G_{(n-2)} + Q_{(n-2)} + e_{(n-2)}$,
	i.e., gross taxable ordinary income plus dividends and capital gains plus the
	standard exemption.
	Only the \emph{taxable} portion of Social Security enters IRMAA MAGI (it is already
	contained in $G_{(n-2)} + e_{(n-2)}$); the non-taxable portion is not added back.
	If the plan
        has its oldest individual currently either 64 or 65 years old, MAGI values for previous years
	must be provided by the user to complete the calculations.
	\begin{figure}[t]
	    \center\includegraphics[width=10cm]{piecewiseConstant.png}
	    \caption{\small Modeling a piecewise constant monotonically increasing function.
	    The $x$-axis represents the MAGI from two years ago, while $m_n$ is the Medicare adjusted premiums.
	    A binary variable $z_q^m$ is associated with each segment $q$.
	    \label{Fig:piecewise}}
	\end{figure}

	Including regular premiums, there are $N_q=6$ brackets
	of MAGI indexed for inflation defined with annual cumulative Medicare costs of
	$\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{qn} = \gamma_n\mathcal{C}_q$ also adjusted for inflation.
        These segments are separated by $N_q - 1$ thresholds
	$\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{qn} = \gamma_n\mathcal{L}_q$.
        Points $(\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{qn}, \bar{\mathcal{C}}_{qn})$ form a piecewise
	constant function that could be modeled using a special ordered set of type 1 (SOS1).
	Points $(\mathcal{L}_q, \mathcal{C}_q)$ are shown in Fig.~\ref{Fig:piecewise}.
        When $\mathbb{G}_{(n-2)} \leq \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0n}$,
	the value $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0n}$
	represents the (inflation-adjusted) base premium for Medicare Part B in year $n$, while
	higher $q$ values include the IRMAA additional costs.

        To model the IRMAA brackets we introduce
	binary variables $z_{qn}^m \in \{0, 1\}$ (one per bracket) and continuous
	variables $h_{qn} \ge 0$ for the MAGI assigned to the selected bracket $q$.
	A big-$\mathcal{M}$ bound is used only for the upper bound of the top bracket (see below).
	We impose an SOS1 selection on binary $z_{qn}^m$ as
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:SOS1}
		\sum_q z_{qn}^m = 1,
	\end{equation}
	and the disaggregation constraints
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\sum_{q=0}^{N_q-1} h_{qn} &=& \mathbb{G}_{(n-2)}, \label{Eq:MedDecomp} \\
		0 \le h_{0n} &\le& \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0n} z_{0n}^m, \nonumber \\
		\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(q-1)n} z_{qn}^m \le h_{qn} &\le& \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{qn} z_{qn}^m,
		\quad q \in \{1, \ldots, N_q-2\}, \label{Eq:MedDecomph}\\
		\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(N_q-2)n} z_{(N_q-1)n}^m \le h_{(N_q-1)n} &\le&
		\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{(n-2)} z_{(N_q-1)n}^m. \nonumber
	\end{eqnarray}
	for all $n \in \{n_m, \ldots, N_n-1\}$, where $n_{m}$ is the index year when
	Medicare starts for any individual $i$ in the plan.
	The upper bound on the last bracket is necessary: when $z_{(N_q-1)n}^m = 0$
	(the top bracket is not selected), the lower bound alone only gives
	$h_{(N_q-1)n} \ge 0$, so the solver could assign positive MAGI to an inactive
	bracket. The constraint $h_{(N_q-1)n} \le \bar{\mathcal{C}}_{(n-2)} z_{(N_q-1)n}^m$
	forces $h_{(N_q-1)n} \le 0$ when $z_{(N_q-1)n}^m = 0$; with $h_{qn} \ge 0$ we
	obtain $h_{(N_q-1)n} = 0$ for the inactive bracket. When $z_{(N_q-1)n}^m = 1$,
	the income ceiling $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{(n-2)}$ of the lookback year cannot bind.
	For the first two years, whose MAGI $\mathbb{G}_{(-1)}$ and $\mathbb{G}_{(-2)}$ is
	user-provided rather than planned, the bound is raised to that MAGI when it is larger.
        As this applies to each individual eligible for Medicare, values
	$\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{qn}$ and  $\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{qn}$
	can include the accounting details for when both spouses are eligible and/or alive.
        For each year $n \geq n_m$, we can then express the Medicare costs $m_n$ as
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:MediCosts}
		m_n = \sum_{q=0}^{N_q - 1} z_{qn}^m \bar{\mathcal{C}}_{qn},
	\end{equation}
	as $z_{qn}^m = \delta_{q,q'}$ can only select a unique bracket $q'$.
	For $n \ge 2$, Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MedDecomp}) can be expanded using
	Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:Tx2}) and (\ref{Eq:Qx2}) as
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\label{Eq:MedDecomp2}
		\sum_{q=0}^{N_q-1} h_{qn} &=&
		\mathcal{A}^x_{(n-2)} + \mathcal{A}^c_{(n-2)}
		+ \sum_i [\omega_{i(n-2)} + \upsilon_{i(n-2)} + \nu_{i(n-2)} \nonumber \\
		&& \quad + R_{(n-2)}\bar{\zeta}_{i(n-2)} + \bar{\pi}_{i(n-2)}] \nonumber \\
		&& - e_{(n-2)} - \sum_i [w_{i1(n-2)} + x_{i(n-2)}] \nonumber \\
		&& - \sum_i \left[(b_{i0(n-2)} - w_{i0(n-2)} + d_{i(n-2)})\right. \nonumber \\
		&&\quad\left.\cdot\left(\sum_{k\neq 0}[\alpha_{i0k(n-2)}\tau_{k(n-2)}] + \mu\alpha_{i00(n-2)}\right)\right] \nonumber \\
		&& + \sum_i \bigl[w_{i0(n-2)}\alpha_{i00(n-2)} \nonumber \\
		&&\quad \cdot \max(0, \tau_{0\max(0, n-3)} - \mu)\bigr] \nonumber \\
		&& + 0.5\sum_{i} \left[\kappa_{i0(n-2)} \left(\sum_{k\neq 0}[\alpha_{i0k(n-2)}\tau_{k(n-2)}]
		+ \mu\alpha_{i00(n-2)}\right)\right]. \nonumber
	\end{eqnarray}
	Collecting variables on the left-hand side yields
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:MedDecomp3}
		\begin{aligned}
		\sum_{q=0}^{N_q-1} h_{qn}
		&+ e_{(n-2)} + \sum_i [w_{i1(n-2)} + x_{i(n-2)}] \\
		&+ \sum_i \Bigl[(b_{i0(n-2)} - w_{i0(n-2)} + d_{i(n-2)}) \\
		&\qquad\cdot \left(\sum_{k\neq 0}[\alpha_{i0k(n-2)}\tau_{k(n-2)}]
		+ \mu\alpha_{i00(n-2)}\right)\Bigr] \\
		&- \sum_i [w_{i0(n-2)}\alpha_{i00(n-2)}\max(0, \tau_{0\max(0, n-3)} - \mu)] \\
		&= \mathcal{A}^x_{(n-2)} + \mathcal{A}^c_{(n-2)}
		+ \sum_i [\omega_{i(n-2)} + \upsilon_{i(n-2)} + \nu_{i(n-2)} + R_{(n-2)}\bar{\zeta}_{i(n-2)} + \bar{\pi}_{i(n-2)}] \\
		&\quad + 0.5\sum_{i} \Bigl[\kappa_{i0(n-2)} \left(\sum_{k\neq 0}[\alpha_{i0k(n-2)}\tau_{k(n-2)}]
		+ \mu\alpha_{i00(n-2)}\right)\Bigr].
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation}
	Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MedDecomp}) is expanded using Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:Tx2}) and (\ref{Eq:Qx2})
	to yield a linear equality in the decision variables; it is implemented directly
	in the constraint matrix. For indices at the beginning of the time sequence,
	we use $\tau_{0, \max(0, n-x)}$, when $x>n$.
	IRMAA MAGI is on the AGI basis, so only the \emph{taxable} portion
	$R_{(n-2)}\bar{\zeta}_{i(n-2)}$ of Social Security enters; the non-taxable remainder
	is not added back. When Social Security taxability is itself optimized
	(\code{withSSTaxability="optimize"}), the taxable amount is the LP variable
	$t^\sigma_{(n-2)}$ rather than the parameter $R_{(n-2)}\bar{\zeta}_{i(n-2)}$.

	For plans where the year index when Medicare first starts $n_m<2$, we will request
	and use user-provided values for the first years' MAGIs as $\mathbb{G}_{(-1)}$ and possibly
	$\mathbb{G}_{(-2)}$ and set
	\begin{equation}
		\label{Eq:MedDecomp4}
		\sum_{q=0}^{N_q-1} h_{qn} = \mathbb{G}_{(n-2)},
		\qquad n \in \{n_m,\ldots, 1\},
	\end{equation}
	where $n_m \ge 0$.
	Using Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MedDecomph}), we can also check for bounds and add
	constraints directly on $z^m_{q0}$ and possibly $z^m_{q1}$.

\subsection{ACA marketplace premium constraints}
\label{Sec:ACA}
	When \code{withACA="optimize"} and ACA is enabled (SLCSP $> 0$, $n_{\mathrm{aca}} > 0$),
	ACA bracket selection and net premium cost are modeled within the optimization.
	ACA uses \emph{current-year} MAGI $\mathbb{G}_n$ (no two-year lag like Medicare).
	Let $N_r = 8$: seven thresholds, multiples of the poverty line of the tax household
	(published the year before), separate eight brackets.
	Bracket 0 lies below the year's first threshold, the state's Medicaid limit (138\% of the
	poverty line in expansion states): it is covered by Medicaid at no premium, or, in a state
	whose Medicaid limit is below 100\% of the poverty line, it is the coverage gap and pays the
	full benchmark premium.
	Brackets 1 to 6 follow the sliding scale up to 400\% of the poverty line, with bracket 1
	(100\%--133\%, the flat lowest percentage) of zero width where Medicaid reaches past 133\%.
	Bracket 7 (400\% and up) receives no credit under the 2026 rules.
	Each year uses its own applicable-percentage table.

	\textbf{SOS1 selection:}
	\begin{equation}
		\sum_{r=0}^{N_r-1} z_{rn}^{\mathrm{aca}} = 1,
		\qquad n \in \{0, \ldots, n_{\mathrm{aca}}-1\}.
	\end{equation}

	\textbf{MAGI decomposition:}
	\begin{equation}
		\sum_{r=0}^{N_r-1} h^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rn} = \mathbb{G}_n,
	\end{equation}
	where $\mathbb{G}_n$ is the current-year MAGI (same structure as in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MedDecomp2})
	but with $n$ instead of $n-2$, and the full Social Security benefit).

	\textbf{Bracket bounds} (analogous to Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MedDecomph}), with the income ceiling
	for the last bracket):
	\begin{eqnarray}
		0 \le h^{\mathrm{aca}}_{0n} &\le& \bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{0n} z_{0n}^{\mathrm{aca}}, \nonumber \\
		\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{(r-1)n} z_{rn}^{\mathrm{aca}}
		\le h^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rn} &\le& \bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rn} z_{rn}^{\mathrm{aca}},
		\quad r \in \{1,\ldots,N_r-2\}, \nonumber \\
		\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{(N_r-2)n}
		z_{(N_r-1)n}^{\mathrm{aca}}
		\le h^{\mathrm{aca}}_{(N_r-1)n}
		&\le& \bar{\mathcal{C}}_n\, z_{(N_r-1)n}^{\mathrm{aca}}.
	\end{eqnarray}
	Each lower bound is raised by a small margin so that a MAGI on a threshold has one bracket.
	A bracket whose lower threshold lies above the MAGI at which the required contribution reaches
	the benchmark premium is pinned off: such incomes belong to the full-premium bracket.
	Where a negative ``other income'' (a deduction) can bring MAGI below zero, the bottom bracket
	reaches down by that deduction.

	\textbf{Cost constraint:}
	Within brackets 1 to 6 the net premium, the applicable percentage of MAGI times MAGI, is convex
	in MAGI. It is represented by $K = 4$ tangent lines per bracket, at evenly spaced points, with
	slopes $\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rkn}$ and intercepts $\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rkn}$:
	\begin{equation}
		m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n \ge \sum_{r=0}^{N_r-2}
		\bigl(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rkn}\, h^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rn}
		+ \bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rkn}\, z^{\mathrm{aca}}_{rn}\bigr)
		+ \bar{S}^{\mathrm{aca}}_n\, z_{(N_r-1)n}^{\mathrm{aca}},
		\qquad k \in \{0,\ldots,K-1\},\ n \in \{0, \ldots, n_{\mathrm{aca}}-1\},
	\end{equation}
	with $0 \le m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n \le \bar{S}^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$, where $\bar{S}^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ is the
	year's benchmark (SLCSP) premium.
	Only the selected bracket's terms are nonzero, so $m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ is bounded below by the
	largest tangent of that bracket: the sliding-scale cost from below, exact at the tangent points
	and short by a few dollars at most between them.
	Bracket 0 carries no cost under Medicaid, and the full benchmark (zero slope) in a coverage gap.
	$m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ carries a small objective weight (see Section~\ref{sec:lexicographic}) so that it
	does not rise above that bound where cash has no value.
	For $n \ge n_{\mathrm{aca}}$, $m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n = 0$ (Medicare-eligible; no ACA cost).
	The variable $m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ is added to the cash-flow constraint Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C4})
	alongside $m_n$ when \code{withACA="optimize"} is active.
	A year in which one partner moves to Medicare is priced on the months-weighted benchmark of the
	two coverage families, keeping a single premium cap per year; this charges slightly more than the
