	loop's pricing, and only for MAGI between the two caps.
	A plan starting in 2025 is priced on the 2026 rules in this formulation.

\chapter{Objective Functions and Their Formulation}
\label{Chap:Objectives}
The objective function is a simple scalar defined as $c\cdot y$ that will be minimized.

\section{Maximizing net spending}

There are a few ways by which a retirement plan can be optimized. For maximizing the net spending under
the constraint of a desired bequest, we introduce the following relation
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:Bequest}
	E_n = \sum_{i,j} (1 - \nu(\delta_{j, 1} + \delta_{j, 3})) b_{ijn},
\end{equation}
which is the value of the estate in nominal dollars at year $n$,
taking into consideration the heir's marginal income tax rate $\nu$ on the ($j=1$) tax-deferred
and ($j=3$) HSA accounts, both of which heirs other than a spouse receive as ordinary income.

For a desired bequest $\epsilon_{N_n}$, expressed in today's
dollars, the final amount in year $N_n$ will need to be
\begin{equation}
	E_{N_n} = \bar\epsilon_{N_n} = \epsilon_{N_n} \gamma_{N_n}.
\end{equation}
Requiring a bequest amounts to adding the floor
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:BequestFloor}
	\sum_{i,j} b_{ijN_n} (1 - \nu(\delta_{j, 1} + \delta_{j, 3})) \ge \epsilon_{N_n} \gamma_{N_n} + \mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}}$ is the balance of debts still outstanding at the end of the
plan, which the savings must also cover; fixed assets still held at the end are added to the
estate separately and do not count toward the floor.
The bequest is a floor rather than an equality: a plan may leave more when doing so costs no
spending.
Without a \code{bequest} option the floor is \$1.
This adds one more row to $\ell \le A_uy$ as
\begin{eqnarray}
	A_u[I(0), q_b(i, j, N_n)] &=& (1 - \nu(\delta_{j, 1} + \delta_{j, 3})) \nonumber \\
	\ell[I(0)] &=& \epsilon_{N_n}\gamma_{N_n} + \mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}} \\
	&&\qquad\forall i \in \{0,\ldots, N_i-1\},\nonumber\\
	&&\qquad\forall j \in \{0,\ldots, N_j-1\},\nonumber\\
\end{eqnarray}
where $I(0)$ is used only to provide the proper row offset $C_l$. See Eq.~(\ref{Eq:Offsets}).
Its row carries the tag \code{bequest_floor}, whose dual prices the bequest
(Chapter~\ref{Chap:SpendingBequest}).

For maximizing the net spending under the constraint of a fixed bequest, one may either
maximize the first-year net spending basis $g_0$ (with the profile fixed by Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C5})),
or maximize the sum of net spending over all years in today's dollars.
In the first case, minimize the inner product $c\cdot y$ with
\begin{eqnarray}
	c[q_g(0)] &=& -1,
\end{eqnarray}
and 0 otherwise; the whole time series $g_n$ then follows from Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C5}).
In the second case, when the slack variable $\lambda$ is used over net spending
(as in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C15}) below), the objective is the sum
$\sum_{n=0}^{N_n-1} g_n/\gamma_n$, maximized by setting
\begin{eqnarray}
	c[q_g(n)] &=& -1/\gamma_n,
\end{eqnarray}
$\forall n \in \{0, \ldots, N_n-1\}$, and 0 otherwise.
This sum-of-net-spending formulation is the one implemented in Owl.

\subsection*{Variable profile with slack}
When the slack variable $\lambda$ is used (Eq.~(\ref{Eq:lambda}) or Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C15}) below),
the objective function is the sum of net spending over the full duration of the plan in today's dollars,
$\sum_{n=0}^{N_n-1} g_n/\gamma_n$. The coefficient vector is
\begin{eqnarray}
	c[q_g(n)] &=& -1/\gamma_n,
\end{eqnarray}
$\forall n \in \{0, \ldots, N_n-1\}$ and 0 otherwise.
In that case, constraint equality Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C5}) is replaced by an inequality.
Instead of obeying 
\begin{equation}
	g_n \xi_0 - g_0 \bar{\xi}_n = 0,
\end{equation}
we impose the inequality constraint
\begin{equation}
        \label{Eq:C15}
	(1 - \lambda) g_0 \bar{\xi}_n/\xi_0 \le g_n \le (1 + \lambda) g_0 \bar{\xi}_n/\xi_0 ,
\end{equation}
where $\lambda$ is the fractional tolerance by which annual net spending
is allowed to deviate from the desired profile.
It is set via the \code{spendingSlack} option (an integer percentage,
e.g.\ \code{spendingSlack=10} gives $\lambda=0.10$); the default is
$\lambda=0$ (strict profile).
It should be noticed that when $\lambda = 0$ the two last equations are
equivalent.

\section{Maximizing bequest}
\label{sec:bequest-objective}
If, on the other hand, one would like to maximize the bequest under the constraint of a desired
net spending $g_0$, specified for the first year,
one would add the following row to $A_ey = v$
\begin{eqnarray}
	\label{Eq:FixedIncome}
	A_e[I(0), q_g(0)] &=& 1, \nonumber \\
	v[I(0)] &=& g_0,
\end{eqnarray}
subject to the net spending $g_n$ obeying Eq.~(\ref{Eq:C5}) over time.

The objective function would then be derived from Eq.~(\ref{Eq:Bequest}) as
minimizing the inner product $c\cdot y$, where $c$ is
\begin{eqnarray}
	\label{Eq:MaxBequest}
	c[q_b(i, j, N_n)] &=& -(1 - \nu(\delta_{j, 1} + \delta_{j, 3})),\\
	&&\qquad\forall i \in \{0,\ldots, N_i-1\},\nonumber\\
	&&\qquad\forall j \in \{0,\ldots, N_j-1\},\nonumber\\
\end{eqnarray}
and 0 otherwise.

\section{Lexicographic tie-breaking}
\label{sec:lexicographic}
The objective leaves many directions flat: plans that differ only in which spouse draws a
dollar, or in a round trip that costs nothing, are worth exactly the same.  Small weights
added to the coefficient vector $c$ select one member of each such family without changing
the primary objective in practice.  Three sizes are used.

\paragraph*{Simplex tie-breaks, $\varepsilon$ (option \code{epsilon}, default $5\times10^{-7}$).}
In today's-dollar units of the objective, with $\varepsilon = 0$ turning all of them off:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item Roth conversions $x_{in}$ are charged $\varepsilon\,(1+n)$, rising with the year, which
	reduces churn and front-loads the conversions that remain.
\item Tax-free withdrawals $w_{i2n}$ are charged $\varepsilon$, so that a household with no tax
	to pay does not empty the tax-free account into the taxable one for nothing.
\item The surplus $s_n$ is charged $\varepsilon$, against withdrawing money only to bank it
	again: the round trip resets cost basis, which feeds back through the self-consistent loop.
\item For a couple, every withdrawal $w_{1jn}$ of the second individual is charged
	$\varepsilon$, preferring the first individual's when otherwise equivalent; and when the
	surplus split is optimized, so is the second individual's deposit $d_{1n}$ before the first
	death, so that the reported deposits do not move between solves.
\item The state bracket amounts $f^{s}_{tn}$ are charged $\varepsilon$, so that the full state
	deduction is taken even where a personal credit already cancels the year's state tax.
\end{itemize}
Larger values start to consume the economics they are meant only to break ties within: at
$10^{-3}$ the measured Roth conversion regret falls by about a quarter.

\paragraph*{Mixed-integer tie-breaks, $10^{-4}$.}
Inside a branch-and-bound gap, $\varepsilon$ is invisible, so variables that are only bounded
below and would otherwise drift where they cost nothing are charged $10^{-4}$ per today's
dollar: the ACA cost $m^{\mathrm{aca}}_n$ under \code{withACA="optimize"}, the senior-bonus
reduction $p_n$ under \code{withSeniorBonus="optimize"}, the lost deductions $l^{f}_n$ and
$l^{s}_n$, and the taxable share of Social Security $t^{\sigma}_n$ under
\code{withSSTaxability="optimize"}, which would otherwise be free to report half the benefit
as taxable in a year whose provisional income is below $\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{lo}}_n$.

\paragraph*{Tax pricing, $10^{-4}$, switched on when needed.}
The bracket variables are tight only while the year's cash has a price.  Where it has none,
as in a late surplus that can only swell a bequest above its floor, any split of income
across brackets is optimal, and the solver may fill the top bracket first and report tax the
plan does not owe.  From the first self-consistent iterate that fills a year out of order,
every bracket variable (federal ordinary, capital gains, and state) is charged $10^{-4}$ times
the tax it levies per today's dollar; an accepted plan that first goes out of order in the
final iterate is re-solved the same way.  It stays off otherwise, because always on it moved
the fixed point the loop settles on in cases that were never out of order.


\chapter{Rate models for return scenarios}
\label{Chap:RateModels}

The optimization model described in the preceding chapters requires a sequence
$\{\tau_{kn}\}_{n=0}^{N_n-1}$ of annual returns for each asset class $k$ over the plan
horizon.
Owl provides several models for generating this sequence, ranging from simple
fixed-rate assumptions to fully stochastic simulation.
This chapter describes the models available and the statistical algorithms
underlying the stochastic ones.

\section{Historical data}
\label{sec:historical-data}

All built-in models draw on an annual dataset covering the period from
$\mathcal{Y}_{\min} = 1928$ through the most recently completed calendar year
$\mathcal{Y}_{\max}$, containing $N_y = \mathcal{Y}_{\max} - \mathcal{Y}_{\min} + 1$
annual observations~\cite{shiller2000}.
Four asset-class return time series are included, corresponding to the index $k$:
\begin{description}[leftmargin=4em,style=multiline]
\item [$k=0$]
    S\&P 500 total return, including dividends.
\item [$k=1$]
    Moody's Baa corporate bond total return (investment-grade
    bonds with a moderate default premium).
\item [$k=2$]
    10-year U.S.\ Treasury note total return.
\item [$k=3$]
    Consumer Price Index (CPI) inflation.
    This series serves a dual role: it drives the cumulative inflation
    factor $\gamma_n$ (see Chapter~\ref{Chap:TaxRules}), and, as the {\em cash asset},
    it proxies a Treasury Inflation-Protected Security (TIPS) with a real
    yield of zero so that holding cash preserves but does not grow purchasing
    power.
\end{description}
All values are stored in percent (e.g.\ $7.0$ for a 7\% annual return).
A user-selected sub-range $[\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}},\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}]
\subseteq [\mathcal{Y}_{\min}, \mathcal{Y}_{\max}]$ is used
by the \code{historical}, \code{historical_average}, \code{historical_gaussian},
\code{historical_lognormal}, \code{historical_bootstrap}, \code{historical_copula},
\code{vector_ar}, \code{garch_dcc}, \code{gmm}, and \code{hmm} models.
We denote the window width by $N_w = \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}} -
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}} + 1$, and the observed return of asset class $k$ in
calendar year $y$ by $r_{ky}$.

\paragraph*{Sequence transformations: reverse and roll.}
Two optional transforms apply to the sequence $\tau'_{kn}$, $n = 0,\ldots,N_n-1$, that a
model has produced for the plan, rather than to the historical window itself.
The {\em roll} option shifts the sequence by $s$ positions, wrapping around the plan horizon,
$\tau_{kn} \leftarrow \tau'_{k,\,(n-s) \bmod N_n}$,
so that a positive $s$ delays every return by $s$ years and brings the last $s$ years to the
start.
The {\em reverse} option then reflects the sequence in time,
$\tau_{kn} \leftarrow \tau_{k,\,N_n-1-n}$,
so that the first plan year receives the return the model had placed in the last.
For the \code{historical} method over a window at least as long as the plan, reversing alone
maps calendar year $\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}+N_n-1$ to plan year~$0$.
Both transforms preserve the set of returns in the plan while altering their
order; they are useful for exploring sequence-of-returns sensitivity without
changing the underlying data.
They apply to every method that produces returns varying by year, including the
stochastic models and \code{dataframe}.
Constant-rate methods ignore them, with a warning.

\section{Constant rate methods}

The simplest approach assigns a constant return to every year of the plan,
$\tau_{kn} = \tau_k$ for all $n$.
Four variants are supported:
\begin{description}[leftmargin=4em,style=nextline]
\item [\texttt{trailing\_30}]
    Trailing 30-year geometric mean of annual returns, updated annually.
\item [\texttt{conservative} and \texttt{optimistic}]
    Preset rates based on long-term expert forecasts.
\item [\texttt{historical\_average}]
    The geometric mean over the selected historical window,
    \begin{equation}
        \tau_k = \exp\biggl(\frac{1}{N_w}\sum_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}}
                 \ln(1 + r_{ky})\biggr) - 1.
    \end{equation}
\item [\texttt{user}]
    Freely specified by the user for each asset class.
\end{description}

\section{Deterministic varying rates: \texttt{historical}}

The \code{historical} method assigns recorded returns directly to each year
of the plan:
\begin{equation}
    \tau_{kn} = r_{k,\,\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}+n},
    \qquad n = 0, \ldots, N_n - 1.
\end{equation}
If the plan horizon $N_n$ exceeds the window width $N_w$, the sequence is
repeated cyclically.
The reverse and roll transforms (see \S\,\ref{sec:historical-data}) are applied
before the plan runs.

\section{Stochastic models}

Stochastic methods draw $N_n$ annual return vectors
$\bm{\tau}_n = (\tau_{0n}, \tau_{1n}, \tau_{2n}, \tau_{3n})^\top \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k}$
from a probability distribution calibrated on historical data.
Because random number generation is involved, a seed can be fixed to obtain
reproducible results across runs.
Each fresh draw of $N_n$ vectors constitutes one scenario; running many
independent scenarios yields a Monte Carlo distribution of outcomes.

\subsection{Inflation skewness correction}
\label{Sec:InflationSkew}
\label{sec:inflation-pwl}

Historical US inflation rates are right-skewed: the distribution
has a long right tail from high-inflation episodes (e.g.\ the 1970s) while
low-inflation and deflation years cluster near the median.
This asymmetry violates the Gaussian residual assumption shared by four of
Owl's parametric stochastic models: \code{historical_gaussian},
\code{historical_lognormal}, \code{vector_ar}, and \code{garch_dcc}.

To correct for this, Owl applies a piecewise-linear (PWL) normalization
transform $\varphi$ to the inflation dimension ($k = 3$) before fitting
each of these models, and its inverse $\varphi^{-1}$ to the generated
inflation samples after drawing:
\begin{equation}
    \varphi(z;\,\kappa,s^-,s^+) = \begin{cases}
        (z - \kappa)\,s^- + \kappa & z \le \kappa, \\[2pt]
        (z - \kappa)\,s^+ + \kappa & z > \kappa,
    \end{cases}
    \label{Eq:pwl}
\end{equation}
where $\kappa$ is the empirical median of the inflation values in the selected
historical window and $s^- > 0$, $s^+ > 0$ are the slopes below and above the
median respectively.
With $s^- > 1$ and $s^+ < 1$, the transform stretches the short left tail and
compresses the long right tail, producing a distribution closer to Gaussian.
The inverse is
\begin{equation}
    \varphi^{-1}(w;\,\kappa,s^-,s^+) = \begin{cases}
        (w - \kappa)/s^- + \kappa & w \le \kappa, \\[2pt]
        (w - \kappa)/s^+ + \kappa & w > \kappa.
    \end{cases}
    \label{Eq:pwlinv}
\end{equation}

\paragraph*{Automatic calibration}
The slopes $s^-$ and $s^+$ are fitted automatically from the same historical
window used to calibrate the rate model, so they adapt when the user selects a
different date range.
They minimize the squared skewness of $\varphi(z)$ subject to a small
regularization toward the identity ($s^- = s^+ = 1$) that ensures a unique,
low-distortion solution:
\begin{equation}
    (s^-,\,s^+) \;=\;
    \arg\min_{\substack{s^->0,\;s^+>0}}
    \Bigl[
        \operatorname{skew}\!\bigl(\varphi(z)\bigr)^2
        +\, 0.1\bigl((s^{-}-1)^2 + (s^{+}-1)^2\bigr)
    \Bigr].
    \label{Eq:pwlfit}
\end{equation}
For US inflation over the full 1928--2025 window (sample skewness $0.31$),
the fit gives $\kappa \approx 2.75\%$, $s^- \approx 1.06$, and $s^+ \approx 0.94$ in return
space; in log-return space, used by \code{historical_lognormal}, the skewness is already
near zero ($-0.02$) and the slopes stay within half a percent of one.
The slopes are searched within $[0.1, 10]$.
The fitted values of $\kappa$, $s^-$, and $s^+$ are recorded in the debug
log at model initialization.

\paragraph*{Scope and domain}
The transform is applied to the inflation dimension only; equities, bonds,
and T-notes are unchanged.
User-specified models (\code{gaussian}, \code{lognormal}) and
data-reuse models (\code{historical}, \code{historical_bootstrap},
\code{historical_average}) are unaffected.
For \code{historical_gaussian}, \code{vector_ar}, and \code{garch_dcc},
$\varphi$ is applied in \emph{return space}: historical inflation returns
$r_{3,y}$ are mapped to $\varphi(r_{3,y})$ before parameter estimation,
and $\varphi^{-1}$ is applied to generated inflation draws before returning them.
For \code{historical_lognormal}, $\varphi$ is applied in
\emph{log-return space}: the log-returns $\ell_{3,y} = \ln(1+r_{3,y})$ are
mapped to $\varphi(\ell_{3,y})$ before Gaussian fitting, and $\varphi^{-1}$
is applied to the generated log-return samples before exponentiation.
Working in log-space eliminates the constraint $\varphi(r_{3,y})>-1$ that
would otherwise be required for the logarithm to remain well-defined.

\subsection{Mean anchoring (\texttt{constrain\_mean})}
\label{sec:constrain-mean}

An optional post-generation correction is available for
\code{historical_gaussian}, \code{historical_lognormal},
\code{historical_copula}, \code{vector_ar}, \code{garch_dcc}, \code{gmm}, and \code{hmm}.
When activated, the arithmetic mean of the generated scenario is shifted
to match the historical window mean exactly.

Let $\bm{\tau}^{(1)}, \ldots, \bm{\tau}^{(N_n)} \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k}$
be the $N_n$ annual return vectors produced by any of the stochastic models
above.
Define the sample mean over the scenario,
\begin{equation}
    \hat{\bm{\mu}} = \frac{1}{N_n}\sum_{n=1}^{N_n} \bm{\tau}^{(n)},
\end{equation}
and the historical arithmetic mean over the selected window,
\begin{equation}
    \mu_k^{\mathrm{hist}} = \frac{1}{N_w}
        \sum_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}} r_{ky}.
\end{equation}
The correction shifts every year's return vector by the same additive offset:
\begin{equation}
    \bm{\tau}^{(n)} \;\leftarrow\; \bm{\tau}^{(n)}
        + \bigl(\bm{\mu}^{\mathrm{hist}} - \hat{\bm{\mu}}\bigr),
    \qquad n = 1, \ldots, N_n.
\end{equation}
This preserves variances, correlations, skewness, volatility clustering, and
all other distributional properties, while ensuring that the scenario's mean
return matches the historical reference by construction.

The practical motivation is sampling bias in short scenarios: for small $N_n$,
the sample mean $\hat{\bm{\mu}}$ can deviate substantially from
$\bm{\mu}^{\mathrm{hist}}$, introducing a systematic optimistic or pessimistic
tilt that is purely a finite-sample artefact.
Mean anchoring removes this tilt without distorting the shape of the
distribution.
Return floors ($\tau_{kn} \ge -1$ for $k \in \{0,1,2\}$;
$\tau_{3n} \ge \tau_{\min}^{\mathrm{infl}}$) are applied \emph{after} the
correction.

\subsection{Multivariate normal: \texttt{historical\_gaussian} and \texttt{gaussian}}

Both methods model annual returns as draws from a multivariate normal
distribution,
\begin{equation}
    \bm{\tau}_n \overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
    \mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\,\bm{\Sigma}),
    \qquad n = 0, \ldots, N_n - 1.
\end{equation}
For \code{historical_gaussian}, the mean vector $\bm{\mu}$ and the
$N_k \times N_k$ covariance matrix $\bm{\Sigma}$ are estimated from the
selected historical window:
\begin{eqnarray}
    \mu_k &=& \frac{1}{N_w}
              \sum_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}} r_{ky},
    \\
    \Sigma_{kk'} &=& \frac{1}{N_w-1}
              \sum_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}}
              (r_{ky} - \mu_k)(r_{k',y} - \mu_{k'}).
\end{eqnarray}
For \code{gaussian}, the user supplies $\bm{\mu}$ directly, together
with the standard deviations $\sigma_k = \sqrt{\Sigma_{kk}}$ and the Pearson
correlation coefficients $\rho_{kk'} \in [-1,1]$, from which the covariance
matrix is assembled as $\Sigma_{kk'} = \rho_{kk'}\sigma_k\sigma_{k'}$.
In both cases, samples are generated as $\bm{\tau}_n = \bm{\mu} + L\bm{z}_n$,
where $L$ is the lower-triangular Cholesky factor of $\bm{\Sigma}$ and
$\bm{z}_n \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, I)$.
By construction, the multivariate normal model treats each year's returns as
independent of all other years; it captures cross-sectional correlation
among asset classes but no serial (year-to-year) autocorrelation.

\subsection{Log-normal models: \texttt{lognormal} and \texttt{historical\_lognormal}}

Geometric Brownian Motion (GBM)~\cite{hull2017} --- the foundation of most
option-pricing theory --- implies that the price relative $1 + \tau_{k,n}$
is log-normally distributed.
The log-normal model enforces $\tau_{k,n} > -1$ by construction (no
total-loss artifacts are possible), produces a right-skewed marginal
distribution (large gains are more likely than a symmetric normal would
suggest), and is consistent with continuous-time finance theory.

\paragraph*{Parameter conversion}
Given the arithmetic mean $m_k$ and arithmetic standard deviation $s_k$
of asset-class $k$ (both in decimal), the corresponding log-space parameters
are~\cite{hull2017}
\begin{eqnarray}
    \sigma_{Z,k}^2 &=& \ln\!\left(1 + \left(\frac{s_k}{1+m_k}\right)^{\!2}\right),
    \label{Eq:sigmaZ}\\
    \mu_{Z,k}      &=& \ln(1 + m_k) - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{Z,k}^2.
    \label{Eq:muZ}
\end{eqnarray}
One can verify that $\mathbb{E}[\exp(Z_k)] - 1 = m_k$ and
$\mathrm{Var}[\exp(Z_k) - 1] = s_k^2$ when
$Z_k \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_{Z,k},\,\sigma_{Z,k}^2)$.

\paragraph*{Sampling}
A log-space covariance matrix is assembled from the log-space standard
deviations $\sigma_{Z,k} = \sqrt{\sigma_{Z,k}^2}$ and the Pearson
correlation matrix $\mathbf{C}$ with entries $\rho_{kk'}$,
\begin{equation}
    (\bm{\Sigma}_Z)_{kk'} = \rho_{kk'}\,\sigma_{Z,k}\,\sigma_{Z,k'}.
\end{equation}
Then $N_n$ vectors of log-returns are drawn jointly,
\begin{equation}
    \bm{Z}_n \overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
    \mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_Z,\,\bm{\Sigma}_Z),
    \qquad n = 0, \ldots, N_n - 1,
\end{equation}
and annual returns are recovered by exponentiation,
\begin{equation}
    \tau_{k,n} = \exp(Z_{k,n}) - 1.
\end{equation}
Applying the correlation matrix directly in log-space is a standard
approximation, exact for jointly log-normal variates and accurate
for moderate return magnitudes.

\paragraph*{\texttt{lognormal} --- user-supplied parameters}
The user provides arithmetic means $m_k$ and standard deviations $s_k$
(in percent) together with Pearson correlations $\rho_{kk'}$.
These are converted to log-space via
Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:sigmaZ})--(\ref{Eq:muZ}) before sampling.

\paragraph*{\texttt{historical\_lognormal} --- history-calibrated parameters}
Parameters are estimated directly in log-space from the selected
historical window without any intermediate conversion to arithmetic
parameters.
Let $\ell_{ky} = \ln(1 + r_{ky})$ be the log-return of asset class $k$
in calendar year $y$.  Then
\begin{eqnarray}
    \mu_{Z,k} &=& \frac{1}{N_w}
                  \sum_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}}
                  \ell_{ky},
    \\
    (\bm{\Sigma}_Z)_{kk'} &=& \frac{1}{N_w-1}
                  \sum_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}}
                  (\ell_{ky} - \mu_{Z,k})(\ell_{k',y} - \mu_{Z,k'}).
\end{eqnarray}
Arithmetic statistics displayed in the UI are recovered from the
log-space moments via the inverse of
Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:sigmaZ})--(\ref{Eq:muZ}):
\begin{eqnarray}
    m_k &=& \exp\!\left(\mu_{Z,k} + \tfrac{1}{2}(\bm{\Sigma}_Z)_{kk}\right) - 1,
    \\
    s_k &=& (1 + m_k)\sqrt{\exp\!\left((\bm{\Sigma}_Z)_{kk}\right) - 1}.
\end{eqnarray}

\subsection{Bootstrap sequence-of-returns: \texttt{historical\_bootstrap}}

The bootstrap sequence-of-returns model resamples $N_n$ annual return vectors
directly from the observed window
$\{\bm{r}_y\}_{y=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}}^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}}$, where
$\bm{r}_y = (r_{0,y},\ldots,r_{N_k-1,y})^\top$.
Resampling from actual data preserves the empirical marginal distributions
exactly, including skewness, fat tails, and any departure from normality.
Four resampling strategies are supported.

\paragraph*{IID bootstrap}
Each year is drawn independently and with replacement from the full
window~\cite{efron1979}:
\begin{equation}
    \bm{\tau}_n = \bm{r}_{y_n},
    \qquad y_n \overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
    \mathrm{Uniform}\{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}, \ldots, \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}\}.
\end{equation}
This is the simplest and default variant; it destroys all serial correlation
present in the original data.

\paragraph*{Block bootstrap}
To preserve short-run serial correlation, the block bootstrap of
Künsch~\cite{kunsch1989} draws $\lceil N_n/b \rceil$ starting positions
$s_\ell$ uniformly from
$\{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}, \ldots, \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}-b+1\}$
and concatenates the corresponding blocks of $b$ consecutive years,
truncating to $N_n$ years:
\begin{equation}
    (\bm{\tau}_0, \ldots, \bm{\tau}_{N_n-1})
    = (\bm{r}_{s_1}, \ldots, \bm{r}_{s_1+b-1},\;
       \bm{r}_{s_2}, \ldots, \bm{r}_{s_2+b-1},\; \ldots).
\end{equation}
The parameter $b$ is the block size, chosen by the user (option \code{block_size}, default 1).

\paragraph*{Circular block bootstrap}
A variant due to Politis and Romano~\cite{politisromano1992} treats the
historical window as circular, wrapping from $\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}$
back to $\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}$.
Starting positions are drawn uniformly from the full window
$\{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}, \ldots, \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}\}$,
and block membership wraps around modulo $N_w$.
This removes the boundary effect present in the block bootstrap, where
observations near the endpoints of the window are underrepresented.

\paragraph*{Stationary bootstrap}
In the stationary bootstrap of Politis and Romano~\cite{politisromano1994},
block lengths are random rather than fixed.
Each block length $L_\ell$ is drawn from a geometric distribution,
\begin{equation}
    P(L_\ell = \ell) = p_b\,(1-p_b)^{\ell-1},
    \qquad \ell = 1, 2, \ldots,
\end{equation}
with parameter $p_b = 1/b$, so that the expected block length equals $b$.
This choice produces a strictly stationary bootstrap distribution, resolving
the boundary discontinuities of the fixed-block method while still
approximately preserving serial correlation at lags up to order $b$.
Within a block, the window is traversed circularly, as in the circular block bootstrap.
The strategy is selected by option \code{bootstrap_type}
(\code{"iid"}, the default, \code{"block"}, \code{"circular"}, or \code{"stationary"}).

\paragraph*{Crisis overweighting}
Selected years can be drawn more often than others:
option \code{crisis_years} lists them and \code{crisis_weight} (default 1, no effect)
multiplies their sampling probability.
The weights apply to the years drawn in the IID bootstrap and to the block starting years in
the other three strategies, so that a block starting in a crisis year is more likely, while
the years that follow it in the block are taken as they come.

\subsection{Vector autoregression: \texttt{vector\_ar}}

The VAR(1) model~\cite{campbellviceira2002,lutkepohl2005} extends the
multivariate normal approach by explicitly capturing year-to-year serial
correlations in asset returns, such as momentum and mean-reversion.
The generating model is
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:VAR1}
    \bm{y}_t = \mathbf{c} + \mathbf{A}\bm{y}_{t-1} + \bm{\varepsilon}_t,
    \qquad \bm{\varepsilon}_t \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \bm{\Sigma}),
\end{equation}
where $\bm{y}_t \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k}$ is the vector of asset-class returns at
time $t$, $\mathbf{c} \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k}$ is a constant intercept vector,
$\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k \times N_k}$ is the transition matrix encoding
one-period predictability, and $\bm{\Sigma}$ is the contemporaneous residual
covariance matrix.
For $\mathbf{A} = 0$, Eq.~(\ref{Eq:VAR1}) reduces to the multivariate normal
model of the \code{historical_gaussian} method.

\paragraph*{Parameter estimation}
Given $N_w$ historical observations
$\bm{y}_1, \ldots, \bm{y}_{N_w}$,
parameters $\mathbf{c}$ and $\mathbf{A}$ are estimated jointly by ordinary
least squares (OLS).
Stacking observations into matrices
\begin{eqnarray}
    Y &=& [\bm{y}_2 \mid \cdots \mid \bm{y}_{N_w}]^\top
          \;\in\; \mathbb{R}^{(N_w-1)\times N_k}, \\
    X &=& [\,\mathbf{1} \;\mid\; \bm{y}_1 \;\mid \cdots \mid\; \bm{y}_{N_w-1}\,]^\top
          \;\in\; \mathbb{R}^{(N_w-1)\times (N_k+1)},
\end{eqnarray}
the OLS solution $B = (X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top Y$ yields the intercept
$\mathbf{c} = B[0,:]$ and transition matrix $\mathbf{A} = B[1:,:]^\top$.
The residual covariance is estimated as
\begin{equation}
    \bm{\Sigma} = \frac{(Y - XB)^\top(Y - XB)}{N_w - N_k - 2},
\end{equation}
dividing by the $N_w - 1$ regression rows less the $N_k + 1$ coefficients of each equation.
At least ten historical years are required.

\paragraph*{Stationarity and spectral shrinkage}
Stationarity of the VAR(1) process requires that all eigenvalues of
$\mathbf{A}$ have modulus strictly less than one.
Let $\rho(\mathbf{A}) = \max_i |\lambda_i(\mathbf{A})|$ denote the spectral
radius.
If $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \ge 0.95$ (near or above the unit circle), the
transition matrix is rescaled as
\begin{equation}
    \mathbf{A} \;\leftarrow\; \frac{0.95}{\rho(\mathbf{A})}\,\mathbf{A},
\end{equation}
ensuring $\rho(\mathbf{A}) = 0.95 < 1$ while preserving the direction of
every eigenmode.
This spectral shrinkage step can be disabled by the user (option \code{shrink}, default
true, available in the case file and the API but not in the user interface), at the cost
of potentially non-stationary sequences.

\paragraph*{Sequence generation}
Starting from $\bm{y}_0 = \bm{\mu}$ (the historical sample mean),
sequences are generated recursively from Eq.~(\ref{Eq:VAR1}) using
$\bm{\varepsilon}_t = L\bm{z}_t$, where $L$ is the Cholesky factor of
$\bm{\Sigma}$ and $\bm{z}_t \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, I)$.
The VAR(1) model produces synthetic sequences of arbitrary length while
capturing both the cross-sectional covariance structure and the
serial autocorrelation of asset returns.

\subsection{DCC-GARCH(1,1): \texttt{garch\_dcc}}

The DCC-GARCH(1,1) model~\cite{engle2002,bollerslev1986} extends the
multivariate normal approach by allowing both the per-asset conditional
variance and the cross-asset correlation matrix to vary over time.
It is fitted in two steps on the selected historical window.

\paragraph*{Step 1 --- per-asset GARCH(1,1)}
Let $\varepsilon_{kt} = y_{k,t} - \mu_k$ be the demeaned return of
asset class $k$ at year $t$, with the $N_w$ observations numbered
$t = 0, \ldots, N_w - 1$.  The conditional variance $h_{kt}$
evolves as
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:GARCH11}
    h_{kt} = \omega_k + \alpha_k\,\varepsilon_{k,t-1}^2
              + \beta_k\,h_{k,t-1},
\end{equation}
with $h_{k,0} = \mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon_k)$, so that $h_{kt}$ depends only on shocks
before year $t$.
The parameters $(\omega_k, \alpha_k, \beta_k)$ are estimated
independently for each asset by maximizing the Gaussian
log-likelihood
\begin{equation}
    \ell_k = -\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{N_w-1}
             \bigl[\ln h_{kt} + \varepsilon_{kt}^2/h_{kt}\bigr],
\end{equation}
subject to $\omega_k > 0$, $\alpha_k, \beta_k > 0$, and the
covariance-stationarity constraint $\alpha_k + \beta_k < 1$.
Optimization uses L-BFGS-B; if convergence fails, a fallback shaped like the
RiskMetrics exponentially weighted moving average~\cite{riskmetrics1996}
($\alpha_k = 0.06$, $\beta_k = 0.94$, $\omega_k = 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon_k)$)
is used instead, its persistence $\alpha_k + \beta_k$ scaled to $0.999$ to keep it stationary.
The decay factor $0.94$ is the one RiskMetrics calibrated for daily returns
(its monthly value is $0.97$); it is applied here to annual returns without recalibration,
and serves only as a stand-in when the likelihood cannot be maximized.

The standardized residuals $z_{kt} = \varepsilon_{kt}/\sqrt{h_{kt}}$,
each scaled by the variance forecast made before its own shock,
are passed to the second step.

\paragraph*{Step 2 --- DCC dynamics}
Let $\overline{\mathbf{Q}}$ be the unconditional correlation matrix of the standardized
residuals $\mathbf{Z} = [z_{kt}]$, as in~\cite{engle2002}: the second-moment matrix
$\mathbf{M} = \frac{1}{N_w}\mathbf{Z}^\top\mathbf{Z}$ normalized to a unit diagonal,
$\overline{\mathbf{Q}} = \mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{M})^{-1/2}\,\mathbf{M}\,\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{M})^{-1/2}$.
The quasi-correlation matrix $\mathbf{Q}_t$ evolves as
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:DCC}
    \mathbf{Q}_t = (1 - a - b)\,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
                  + a\,\bm{z}_{t-1}\bm{z}_{t-1}^\top
                  + b\,\mathbf{Q}_{t-1},
\end{equation}
starting from $\mathbf{Q}_0 = \overline{\mathbf{Q}}$,
with $a, b > 0$ and $a + b < 1$.  The time-varying correlation matrix
is obtained by normalizing:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbf{R}_t = \mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{Q}_t)^{-1/2}\,
                  \mathbf{Q}_t\,
                  \mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{Q}_t)^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
The DCC parameters $(a, b)$ are estimated by maximizing the
concentrated log-likelihood
\begin{equation}
    \ell_{\mathrm{DCC}} = -\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{N_w-1}
    \bigl[\ln|\mathbf{R}_t| + \bm{z}_t^\top\mathbf{R}_t^{-1}\bm{z}_t\bigr],
\end{equation}
also with L-BFGS-B, falling back on $(a, b) = (0.05, 0.90)$ if it fails.

\paragraph*{Sequence generation}
The simulation starts from the forecasts for the year after the window,
$\mathbf{Q}_{N_w}$ and $\bm{h}_{N_w} = (h_{0,N_w},\ldots,h_{N_k-1,N_w})$,
obtained by a forward pass through all $N_w$ historical residuals.
Each simulated year is then drawn as
\begin{equation}
    \bm{z}_t = L(\mathbf{R}_t)\,\bm{u}_t, \qquad
    \bm{y}_t = \bm{\mu} + \mathrm{diag}(\bm{h}_t)^{1/2}\,\bm{z}_t,
    \qquad \bm{u}_t \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, I),
\end{equation}
where $L(\mathbf{R}_t)$ is the lower Cholesky factor of $\mathbf{R}_t$, so that $\bm{z}_t$ is
the standardized residual of the simulated year.
The GARCH update of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:GARCH11}) with $\varepsilon_t = \bm{y}_t - \bm{\mu}$ and the
DCC update of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:DCC}) with $\bm{z}_t$ then give the next year's $\bm{h}$ and $\mathbf{Q}$.
This produces sequences that exhibit \emph{volatility clustering} ---
large shocks in one year increase conditional variance in the next ---
and \emph{time-varying cross-asset correlations} that spike during
simulated market-stress episodes, consistent with empirical evidence
in financial return data.

\subsection{Gaussian mixture model: \texttt{gmm}}

The Gaussian mixture model (GMM)~\cite{mclachlan2000} generalizes the
single multivariate normal of \code{historical_gaussian} by representing
the joint return distribution as a weighted sum of $K$ multivariate
Gaussian components:
\begin{equation}
    p(\bm{r}) = \sum_{k=1}^{K} \pi_k\,
                \mathcal{N}\!\left(\bm{r};\,\bm{\mu}_k,\,\bm{\Sigma}_k\right),
    \qquad \pi_k \ge 0,\quad \sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_k = 1.
\end{equation}
Each component $k$ has its own mean vector $\bm{\mu}_k \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k}$,
full covariance matrix $\bm{\Sigma}_k \in \mathbb{R}^{N_k \times N_k}$, and
mixture weight $\pi_k$.

\paragraph*{Motivation}
Historical return data are not well-described by a single Gaussian.
The empirical distribution exhibits fat tails, negative skewness for equities,
and distinct clustering consistent with alternating market regimes --- a
prolonged bull market, a bear market, and acute crisis periods.
A GMM with $K = 3$ components can capture these regimes jointly across all
asset classes, so that in a ``crisis'' component, both equities and investment-grade
bonds may fall together (correlation breakdown), while in a ``bull'' component the
usual negative stock--bond correlation is restored.
Each component's $\bm{\Sigma}_k$ encodes the cross-asset covariance
\emph{conditional on being in that regime}.

\paragraph*{Fitting}
The parameters $\{\pi_k, \bm{\mu}_k, \bm{\Sigma}_k\}_{k=1}^K$ are estimated
from the $N_w$ annual return vectors in the selected historical window
$[\mathcal{Y}_\mathrm{frm}, \mathcal{Y}_\mathrm{to}]$ by
Expectation-Maximization (EM)~\cite{dempster1977}.
The EM algorithm alternates between:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{E-step}: compute the posterior responsibility
          $\gamma_{nk} = \pi_k\,\mathcal{N}(\bm{r}_n;\bm{\mu}_k,\bm{\Sigma}_k)\;
          \big/\;\sum_{j}\pi_j\,\mathcal{N}(\bm{r}_n;\bm{\mu}_j,\bm{\Sigma}_j)$
          for each observation $n$ and component $k$;
    \item \textbf{M-step}: update
          $\pi_k = \frac{1}{N_w}\sum_n \gamma_{nk}$,\;
          $\bm{\mu}_k = \frac{\sum_n \gamma_{nk}\bm{r}_n}{\sum_n \gamma_{nk}}$,\;
          $\bm{\Sigma}_k = \frac{\sum_n \gamma_{nk}(\bm{r}_n - \bm{\mu}_k)(\bm{r}_n - \bm{\mu}_k)^\top}{\sum_n \gamma_{nk}}$.
\end{itemize}
A diagonal regularization of $10^{-6}$ is added to every $\bm{\Sigma}_k$.
The means start at $K$ distinct observations drawn at random (hence the seed also fixes the fit),
the weights start uniform, and the covariances start at the pooled covariance.
Convergence is declared when the log-likelihood gains less than $10^{-6}$, within at most 200
iterations.

\paragraph*{Sequence generation}
To draw one simulated year of returns:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Sample a regime index $k^* \sim \mathrm{Categorical}(\bm{\pi})$.
    \item Draw $\bm{\tau}_n \sim \mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{k^*},\bm{\Sigma}_{k^*})$.
\end{enumerate}
Each of the $N_n$ plan years is drawn independently, so the model captures
cross-sectional correlation within each regime but no serial autocorrelation
across years.
The number of components $K$ is a user parameter (option \code{n_components}, default $K=3$,
at least 2 and no more than the number of years in the window); every component has a full
covariance matrix.

\subsection{Hidden Markov model: \texttt{hmm}}

The hidden Markov model (HMM)~\cite{rabiner1989} extends the GMM by adding
temporal structure to the regime sequence.
An unobserved discrete state $s_n \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ evolves as a
first-order Markov chain with transition matrix
$\bm{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{K \times K}$,
$A_{kj} = P(s_{n+1} = j \mid s_n = k)$, with $\sum_j A_{kj} = 1$,
and each year's returns are drawn from the multivariate Gaussian of the
current state:
\begin{equation}
    s_{n+1} \mid s_n = k \;\sim\; \mathrm{Categorical}(\bm{A}_{k\cdot}),
    \qquad
    \bm{\tau}_n \mid s_n = k \;\sim\; \mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_k, \bm{\Sigma}_k).
    \label{Eq:HMM}
\end{equation}
The emission parameters $\{\bm{\mu}_k, \bm{\Sigma}_k\}$ have the same
interpretation as in the GMM; the key new quantity is $\bm{A}$.

\paragraph*{Motivation}
The GMM treats each simulated year as an independent regime draw.
Historical return data exhibit regime \emph{persistence}: bull and
bear markets tend to continue for several years before switching.
The HMM encodes this by coupling consecutive years through $\bm{A}$,
so a diagonal entry $A_{kk}$ close to 1 produces multi-year runs of
the same regime.
This is directly relevant to sequence-of-returns risk: a retiree entering a
bear-market regime faces an elevated probability of further down years before
the regime changes, amplifying the compounding impact of early losses in ways
the i.i.d. GMM cannot reproduce.

\paragraph*{Fitting}
Given $N_w$ historical return vectors $\bm{r}_0,\ldots,\bm{r}_{N_w-1}$, the
parameters $\{\bm{\pi}, \bm{A}, \bm{\mu}_k, \bm{\Sigma}_k\}$ are estimated
by the Baum-Welch algorithm~\cite{baum1970}, which applies
EM~\cite{dempster1977} to the HMM likelihood.
Each iteration runs a scaled forward-backward pass and a parameter update.

\textit{Forward pass}:
\begin{align}
    \hat{\alpha}_0(k) &= \pi_k\,b_k(\bm{r}_0), &
    c_0 &= {\textstyle\sum_k}\,\hat{\alpha}_0(k), &
    \hat{\alpha}_0 &\leftarrow \hat{\alpha}_0/c_0, \\
    \hat{\alpha}_n(j) &= b_j(\bm{r}_n)\,{\textstyle\sum_k}\hat{\alpha}_{n-1}(k)\,A_{kj}, &
    c_n &= {\textstyle\sum_j}\,\hat{\alpha}_n(j), &
    \hat{\alpha}_n &\leftarrow \hat{\alpha}_n/c_n,
\end{align}
where $b_k(\bm{r}) = \mathcal{N}(\bm{r};\bm{\mu}_k,\bm{\Sigma}_k)$.
The log-likelihood is $\ell = \sum_{n}\log c_n$.

\textit{Backward pass}:
\begin{equation}
    \hat{\beta}_{N_w-1}(k) = 1, \qquad
    \hat{\beta}_n(k) = \frac{1}{c_{n+1}}\sum_j A_{kj}\,b_j(\bm{r}_{n+1})\,\hat{\beta}_{n+1}(j).
\end{equation}

\textit{Posterior quantities}:
\begin{equation}
    \gamma_n(k) = \hat{\alpha}_n(k)\,\hat{\beta}_n(k)\;
                  \Big/\;{\textstyle\sum_j}\hat{\alpha}_n(j)\hat{\beta}_n(j),
    \qquad
    \xi_n(k,j) \;\propto\;
        \hat{\alpha}_n(k)\,A_{kj}\,b_j(\bm{r}_{n+1})\,\hat{\beta}_{n+1}(j).
\end{equation}

\textit{M-step}:
\begin{alignat}{2}
    \pi_k &= \gamma_0(k), \\
    A_{kj} &= \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N_w-2}\xi_n(k,j) + \delta}
                   {\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N_w-2}\gamma_n(k) + K\delta},
    \label{Eq:HMM_trans}\\
    \bm{\mu}_k &= \frac{\sum_n \gamma_n(k)\,\bm{r}_n}{\sum_n \gamma_n(k)},
    \quad
    \bm{\Sigma}_k = \frac{\sum_n \gamma_n(k)\,(\bm{r}_n-\bm{\mu}_k)(\bm{r}_n-\bm{\mu}_k)^\top}
                         {\sum_n \gamma_n(k)} + \varepsilon\,\bm{I},
\end{alignat}
where $\delta > 0$ (default $10^{-3}$) is a Laplace smoothing term that
prevents zero-probability transitions for regimes not visited in the
historical window, and $\varepsilon = 10^{-6}$ regularizes the covariances.
Convergence is declared when the improvement in $\ell$ falls below a
tolerance; the transition matrix is initialized to an 80\% self-transition
prior, $A_{kk} = 0.8$, to encourage persistence from the first iteration.

\paragraph*{Sequence generation}
Let $\bm{\pi}_\infty$ denote the stationary distribution satisfying
$\bm{\pi}_\infty = \bm{\pi}_\infty\bm{A}$, computed by power iteration.
A plan of $N_n$ years is simulated as:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Draw the initial state $s_0 \sim \bm{\pi}_\infty$
          (or from a user-specified $\texttt{init\_regime}$).
    \item For $n = 0,1,\ldots,N_n-1$:
          draw $\bm{\tau}_n \sim \mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{s_n},\bm{\Sigma}_{s_n})$,
          then transition $s_{n+1}\sim\mathrm{Categorical}(\bm{A}_{s_n,\cdot})$.
\end{enumerate}
The fit depends on the seed, which picks the starting means among the observations.
Fitted on 1928--2025 annual US data with $K=3$ and seeds 1, 2, 3, 7, and 42,
two regimes are persistent, with diagonal entries $A_{kk}$ between 0.47 and 0.98
(mean runs $1/(1-A_{kk})$ of two to fifty years), while the third is often
short-lived, with $A_{kk}$ between 0 and 0.80.
The HMM is strictly more expressive than the GMM: if $\bm{A}$ is set to
the outer product $\bm{1}\bm{\pi}^\top$ (all rows identical), the model
reduces to the i.i.d. GMM, so the maximum likelihood of the HMM is at least
that of the GMM.
The fitted values need not compare that way: Baum-Welch, like EM for the GMM, reaches a
local maximum from a random start, and the Laplace smoothing of $\bm{A}$ makes it a
slightly penalized fit.

\subsection{Gaussian copula: \texttt{historical\_copula}}

The Gaussian copula model~\cite{sklar1959} generates synthetic return sequences
whose marginal distributions match the historical data exactly, while their
joint dependence structure is captured by a parametric Gaussian copula.
Unlike \code{historical_gaussian} and \code{historical_lognormal}, which
impose a Gaussian (or log-normal) shape on every marginal,
this method is fully non-parametric in the marginals: it preserves
the exact empirical distribution of each asset class --- including
the left skew of equity returns, the right skew of T-Note returns,
and the right skew of inflation.

\paragraph*{Motivation}
Sklar's theorem~\cite{sklar1959} states that any joint distribution can be
decomposed into its marginal distributions and a copula that captures all
dependence.
In the Gaussian copula family, the dependence is parameterized by a
$N_k \times N_k$ correlation matrix $\bm{R}$, separating the fitting of
individual-asset behavior from the fitting of cross-asset correlations.
For retirement planning, this matters because inflation in particular
is neither Gaussian nor log-normal; preserving its empirical shape avoids
systematic bias in real spending projections.

\paragraph*{Fitting}
Let $\bm{r}_y = (r_{1y}, \ldots, r_{N_k,y})^\top$ denote the observed
return vector for calendar year $y$ in the selected window
$[\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}, \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}}]$,
with $N_w$ total observations.

\textit{Step 1 --- rank-based empirical CDF.}
For each asset class $k$, map the $N_w$ observations to the
uniform grid via rank statistics:
\begin{equation}
    U_{ky} = \frac{\mathrm{rank}(r_{ky}) - \tfrac{1}{2}}{N_w},
    \qquad y = \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}},\ldots,\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}},
    \label{Eq:CopulaUniform}
\end{equation}
where rank is computed within the window for each $k$ separately.
The $-\tfrac{1}{2}$ centering keeps all values strictly inside $(0,1)$
so that the next step is well-defined.

\textit{Step 2 --- normal scores.}
Apply the standard normal quantile function to obtain copula variates:
\begin{equation}
    Z_{ky} = \Phi^{-1}(U_{ky}),
    \label{Eq:CopulaNormalScores}
\end{equation}
where $\Phi$ denotes the standard normal CDF.

\textit{Step 3 --- copula correlation matrix.}
Estimate the $N_k \times N_k$ Gaussian copula correlation matrix
from the normal scores:
\begin{equation}
    \bm{R} = \mathrm{corrcoef}\!\left(\bm{Z}^\top\right),
    \label{Eq:CopulaR}
\end{equation}
where $\bm{Z} \in \mathbb{R}^{N_w \times N_k}$ collects all normal-score
rows.
$\bm{R}$ captures Pearson (linear) correlation in the normal-score space,
which corresponds to Spearman rank correlation in the original return space.

\paragraph*{Sequence generation}
To draw one plan year of synthetic returns:

\textit{Step 4 --- sample from the copula.}
Draw a standard multivariate normal vector:
\begin{equation}
    \bm{z}_n \sim \mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\, \bm{R}).
    \label{Eq:CopulaSample}
\end{equation}

\textit{Step 5 --- map back to the original scale.}
Convert to uniform scores and apply the empirical quantile function
for each asset class:
\begin{equation}
    u_{kn} = \Phi(z_{kn}), \qquad
    \tau_{kn} = \hat{Q}_k(u_{kn}),
    \label{Eq:CopulaBackTransform}
\end{equation}
where $\hat{Q}_k$ is the empirical quantile function of asset $k$, obtained
by linear interpolation over the $N_w$ sorted historical values
$r_{k,(1)} \le \cdots \le r_{k,(N_w)}$ at the grid
$\{(i - \tfrac{1}{2})/N_w\}_{i=1}^{N_w}$.
Values of $u_{kn}$ outside $[\,u_{k,1},\, u_{k,N_w}]$ are clamped to the
observed $[\min, \max]$, preventing extrapolation beyond the historical record.
The resolution of $\hat{Q}_k$ equals $N_w$, so a wider window provides finer
quantile resolution.

\textit{Step 6 --- inflation floor.}
To exclude the Great Depression artefact of deeply negative inflation,
the generated inflation series is floored at $-5\%$.

Each of the $N_n$ plan years is drawn independently; the model captures
cross-asset rank correlation but no serial autocorrelation across years.

\section{User-supplied rates: \texttt{dataframe}}

The \code{dataframe} method allows the user to bypass all built-in rate
models and supply the return matrix $\{\tau_{kn}\}$ directly as a
pandas \code{DataFrame} (one row per plan year, with columns named
\code{S&P 500}, \code{Bonds Baa}, \code{T-Notes}, and \code{Inflation}).
This is useful for custom scenarios, external forecasts, or deterministic
stress tests not expressible through the other methods.
The rows are read in order, after skipping the first \code{offset} rows (default 0);
the DataFrame must hold at least $N_n$ rows past the offset, and a shorter one is refused
rather than repeated.
Values are read in percent unless \code{in_percent} is false, in which case they are
taken as decimal fractions.
No statistical estimation or sampling is performed; the user bears full
responsibility for the consistency and units of the supplied data.
This method is accessed programmatically via the Python API
(\code{setRates("dataframe", ...)}); it is not available through the
TOML configuration file.

\section{Monte Carlo and stress testing}

In Monte Carlo mode, Owl calls one of the stochastic models
(\code{historical_gaussian}, \code{historical_lognormal}, \code{gaussian},
\code{lognormal}, \code{historical_bootstrap}, \code{historical_copula},
\code{vector_ar}, \code{garch_dcc}, \code{gmm}, or \code{hmm}) independently
$N_s$ times, each time solving the full optimization problem with a different
rate scenario.
The distribution of resulting optimal spending or bequest values across the
$N_s$ scenarios provides a probabilistic assessment of the retirement plan.

In stress-testing mode (Historical Range), the \code{historical} method
is applied repeatedly: the starting year $\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}$ is
advanced one year at a time over a specified calendar range, producing one
deterministic solve per starting year (by default).
This sweep is equivalent to asking: ``How would this plan have fared had the
retiree begun at every historical starting year?''
When the {\em augmented} option is enabled, the run is expanded: for each
starting year, every $(reverse, roll)$ pair in
$\{\mathrm{false}, \mathrm{true}\} \times \{0, \ldots, N_n-1\}$ is executed,
yielding $2 N_n$ sequences per year instead of one.
For a 30-year plan over 50 years of history, that produces $50 \times 60 =
3000$ outcomes rather than 50, providing a much denser sample for the
result histogram while still using only observed historical returns.
Reverse and roll (see \S\,\ref{sec:historical-data}) alter the ordering of
the same returns; augmented mode systematically explores all such orderings.

\paragraph*{Robustness of first-year decisions}
Only the first year's decisions of a plan are executed; later years are
re-optimized as returns realize.
Owl therefore also reports, across the same scenario set, the distribution of
the \emph{first-year} decisions: per-person Roth conversion percentiles with
the fraction of scenarios recommending a conversion and an agreement metric
(share of scenarios within 10\% of the median), withdrawals by account, net
spending, and the modal top ordinary-income bracket
(\code{summarize_year1} in \code{stresstests.py}, surfaced through the
\code{run_year1_robustness} assistant tool).
A statement such as ``convert \$45k--\$60k this year in 87\% of scenarios''
is more decision-relevant than any single-scenario schedule.
Each scenario is solved with perfect foresight of its own return path, so the
spread \emph{understates} true decision uncertainty; a two-stage stochastic
formulation committing the first-year decisions across scenarios would price
that uncertainty and is left as future work.

\chapter{Stochastic Spending Optimization and Longevity Risk}
\label{chap:stochastic-spending}

A natural measure of plan robustness is the \textit{Probability of Success} (PoS), defined as
the fraction of scenarios in which the chosen spending level is fully sustained throughout the
plan horizon.
While intuitive, PoS is a binary criterion: a plan that falls short by one percent and a plan
that collapses completely are counted identically.
Two plans with the same PoS may therefore carry very different risk profiles.

To address this limitation, Owl adopts a framework that treats the retirement spending decision
as a commitment made \emph{before} the sequence of returns is known.
The retiree commits to a single real spending level $g^*$, and for each scenario
$s$ the shortfall---the amount by which $g^*$ exceeds what the scenario can
sustain---is measured explicitly.
Tracing the trade-off between committed spending and expected shortfall as a two-dimensional
Pareto frontier, controlled by a risk-aversion parameter, yields a richer picture of plan
risk that is analogous to the mean-variance efficient frontier of Markowitz
portfolio theory~\cite{markowitz1952}.
The conservative extreme of the frontier recovers the classical safe withdrawal rate
of Bengen~\cite{bengen1994}: the spending level that succeeds in every scenario.

\section[Scenario bases]{Scenario bases and the spending commitment}

Let $s = 0, 1, \ldots, S-1$ denote the scenario index (0-based, following the
C-array convention used throughout this document), where $S$ is the total number
of scenarios considered.
For each scenario $s$, Owl solves one full optimization problem
(the LP, or MILP when options call for binary variables, described in the preceding chapters)
with the return sequence $\{\tau_{kn}^{(s)}\}$ of that scenario.
The result is the \textit{scenario basis}
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:ScenarioBasis}
    g_s \;=\; \max\bigl\{g : \text{the plan is feasible under scenario } s
                         \text{ at spending level } g\bigr\}.
\end{equation}
Because $g_s$ is computed by the full optimization, it incorporates all tax rules, RMDs,
Social Security, Medicare, and Roth conversion constraints as modeled there.
The basis is the spending at profile factor one; the first year's net spending of the
scenario is $\xi_0 g_s$, which equals $g_s$ on a flat profile.
The commitment LP below is positively homogeneous, so its frontier is the same in either unit,
up to that scale, and Owl reports it in both.
Social Security claiming ages are held at the plan's values in every scenario
(Chapter~\ref{Chap:SSAges}): choosing them again in each scenario would choose them knowing
that scenario's returns and lifespans.

The retiree must commit to a single first-year real spending level $g^*$
\textit{before} observing which scenario will unfold.
In scenario $s$ the \textit{shortfall} is
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:Shortfall}
    \varsigma_s \;=\; \max(0,\; g^* - g_s).
\end{equation}
Scenario $s$ is a \textit{success} when $\varsigma_s = 0$.
The \textit{Probability of Success} is the fraction of scenarios without shortfall:
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:PoS}
    \varrho \;=\; \frac{1}{S}\,\#\bigl\{s : \varsigma_s = 0\bigr\},
\end{equation}
and the \textit{mean shortfall} is
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:MeanShortfall}
    \bar\varsigma \;=\; \frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1} \varsigma_s.
\end{equation}

\section{The commitment LP and efficient frontier}

Given the scenario bases $\{g_s\}$ and a non-negative risk-aversion parameter
$\Lambda \geq 0$, the optimal committed spending is determined by the
\textit{commitment LP}:
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:CommitmentLP}
    \begin{aligned}
        \text{maximize}   &\quad g^* - \frac{\Lambda}{S}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1} \varsigma_s \\
        \text{subject to} &\quad \varsigma_s \geq g^* - g_s, \qquad s=0,\ldots,S-1, \\
                          &\quad \varsigma_s \geq 0, \qquad\qquad\quad s=0,\ldots,S-1, \\
                          &\quad 0 \leq g^* \leq \max_{s}\, g_s.
    \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The decision variables are $g^*$ and the $S$ shortfall variables $\{\varsigma_s\}$.
The parameter $\Lambda$ controls the spending--shortfall trade-off:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
    \item $\Lambda = 0$: maximize $g^*$ without any shortfall penalty.
          This is the most aggressive point on the frontier; it corresponds to
          committing to the best-case scenario basis, $g^* = \max_s g_s$.
    \item $\Lambda \to \infty$: the shortfall penalty dominates, forcing
          $\varsigma_s = 0$ for all $s$.
          This is the most conservative point; it recovers the safe withdrawal
          rate $g^* = \min_s g_s$.
\end{itemize}
The \textit{efficient frontier} is the curve traced in the $(g^*, \bar\varsigma)$
plane as $\Lambda$ is swept over 61 values: $\Lambda = 0$ and 60 values spaced
logarithmically from $10^{-1}$ to $10^3$.
Every frontier point is Pareto-optimal: no other committed spending level achieves
simultaneously higher $g^*$ and lower $\bar\varsigma$.
Table~\ref{Tab:Markowitz} draws the analogy between this framework and
mean-variance portfolio theory.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\hline
\textbf{Concept} & \textbf{Portfolio theory} & \textbf{Spending framework} \\
\hline
Decision variable   & Portfolio weights           & Committed spending $g^*$     \\
Objective           & Expected return             & Committed spending $g^*$     \\
Risk measure        & Variance $\sigma^2$         & Mean shortfall $\bar\varsigma$  \\
Risk-aversion param & $\Lambda$                   & $\Lambda$                    \\
Conservative extreme & Min-variance portfolio     & Safe withdrawal rate         \\
Aggressive extreme  & Max-return portfolio        & Best-case scenario           \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Analogy between Markowitz mean-variance portfolio theory and the
         spending/shortfall efficient frontier.}
\label{Tab:Markowitz}
\end{table}

\paragraph*{Connection to Conditional Value-at-Risk.}
The mean shortfall $\bar\varsigma$ is related to the
Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR)~\cite{artzner1999,rockafellar2000} at
confidence level $\varrho$ by
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:CVaR}
    \mathrm{CVaR}_\varrho \;=\; \frac{\bar\varsigma}{1 - \varrho}.
\end{equation}
CVaR is a coherent risk measure that quantifies the expected shortfall
conditional on being in the failure region.
Because the commitment LP~(\ref{Eq:CommitmentLP}) directly minimizes mean shortfall,
it is equivalent to minimizing CVaR at the corresponding success rate.

Owl reports a floor-capped version that bounds the heavy tail of Monte Carlo ensembles:
given a spending floor $F$, each scenario's shortfall is capped at $g^* - F$,
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:CVaRFloor}
    \mathrm{CVaR}^{F}_\varrho \;=\; \frac{1}{1 - \varrho}\,\frac{1}{S}
    \sum_{s=0}^{S-1} \max\bigl(0,\; g^* - \max(F, g_s)\bigr).
\end{equation}
With $F = \min_s g_s$, the historical safe floor, this is the CVaR of
Eq.~(\ref{Eq:CVaR}).
The \textit{Retirement Efficiency Score} at each frontier point is the spending above the
floor per unit of tail risk,
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:RES}
    \mathrm{RES} \;=\; \frac{g^* - F}{\mathrm{CVaR}^{F}_\varrho},
\end{equation}
defined where $g^* > F$ and the CVaR is positive.
Owl reports its maximum, the success rate $\varrho^*$ at which it occurs, and the CVaR there
and at the user's target success rate (functions \code{compute_cvar} and \code{compute_res}).

\section{Longevity risk}
\label{sec:longevity}

All scenario analyses described so far use a fixed planning horizon $N_n$
equal to the assumed life expectancy.
In practice, however, the time of death is uncertain, and the risk of
outliving one's assets---\textit{longevity risk}---is a central concern in
retirement planning.
Owl models longevity risk by randomizing the planning horizon within each
Monte Carlo scenario.

For each Monte Carlo scenario $s$ and each individual $i \in \{0,\ldots,N_i-1\}$,
an age-at-death $T_i^{(s)}$ is drawn independently from the SSA period life
table conditioned on having survived to the individual's current age:
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:LongevityDraw}
    T_i^{(s)} \;\sim\; F_i\!\bigl(\,\cdot \mid \text{current age}_i,\, \text{sex}_i\bigr),
    \qquad i = 0, \ldots, N_i - 1,
\end{equation}
where $F_i$ is the discrete conditional survival distribution.
For a couple, the plan must remain funded until the last survivor, so the
effective plan horizon for scenario $s$ is
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:LastSurvivor}
    T^{(s)} \;=\; \max_i\, T_i^{(s)}.
\end{equation}
Each scenario is solved with a fresh plan clone whose life expectancy is set
to the drawn last-survivor horizon $T^{(s)}$, so the optimization
horizon matches the sampled mortality outcome exactly.
Scenarios with $T^{(s)} \leq 1$ year are pre-assigned $g_s = 0$ to
handle the degenerate case of an imminent end of horizon.

\section{Scenario generation}

The scenario bases $\{g_s\}$ are generated by one of two methods.
The rate models underlying each method are described in Chapter~\ref{Chap:RateModels}.

\begin{description}[leftmargin=4em,style=multiline]
\item[Historical]
    Each calendar starting year
    $\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{frm}}, \ldots, \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{to}} - N_n + 1$
    yields one fixed real-return sequence and therefore one scenario.
    $S$ equals the number of valid starting years.
    Augmented mode expands this set by applying all $(reverse, roll)$
    combinations, yielding up to $2 N_n$ sequences per starting year.
\item[Monte Carlo]
    $S$ independent return paths are drawn from the plan's stochastic rate model
    (see Chapter~\ref{Chap:RateModels}); a deterministic method is refused.
    The resulting bases span the full tail of the return distribution,
    including sequences more extreme than any observed historically.
    Longevity risk sampling is available in this mode only (see below).
\end{description}

\section{Implementation in Owl}

The stress-test computation is implemented in \code{stresstests.py} and
exposed through the \code{runStochasticSpending} method of the \code{Plan}
class, which calls \code{run_stochastic_spending} in that module.
Each of the $S$ scenarios is solved independently and in parallel using a
\code{ThreadPoolExecutor} with up to \code{cpu_count} workers; each worker
receives an independent clone of the base plan and calls \code{plan.solve}
with the \code{"maxSpending"} objective.
Scenarios for which Owl finds no feasible solution are assigned $g_s = 0$,
contributing a full shortfall $\varsigma_s = g^*$ to the commitment LP---the
most conservative treatment consistent with the framework.
Once all $S$ bases are collected, the commitment LP~(\ref{Eq:CommitmentLP})
is solved for each of the 61 values of $\Lambda$; because the LP has only
$S + 1$ variables, this final sweep is fast relative to the scenario
solves.

The Streamlit interface (\code{ui/Spending_Optimization.py}) caches the
scenario data; adjusting the target success rate $\varrho$ via the slider
merely looks up the corresponding frontier point without re-solving any
scenario.
An integer seed may be supplied for reproducible Monte Carlo paths and
longevity draws.
The \code{run_stochastic_spending} call returns a dictionary whose keys include
\code{bases}, \code{frontier_g}, \code{frontier_prob},
\code{frontier_shortfall}, \code{lambdas}, and \code{drawn_lifespans},
the first-year versions \code{bases_year1}, \code{frontier_g_year1},
\code{frontier_prob_year1}, and \code{frontier_shortfall_year1},
the profile factor \code{xi_0}, the per-scenario \code{partial_bequests},
and the first-year decisions \code{year1_decisions}.

\subsection*{Limitations and assumptions}

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
    \item Longevity risk sampling is supported only with Monte Carlo scenarios.
          Historical scenarios have a bounded return series; a drawn lifespan
          could exceed the length of available data, making the combination
          infeasible.
    \item Scenario bases are computed independently: the return sequences of
          different scenarios are assumed to be statistically independent.
          This is exact for Monte Carlo but approximate for historical
          scenarios, which share overlapping return sub-sequences when the
          plan horizon exceeds one year.
    \item The historical scenario set is limited by the length of the
          available dataset.
          For a 30-year plan, only starting years up to
          $\mathcal{Y}_{\max} - N_n + 1$ are valid, reducing $S$
          as the horizon grows.
    \item The commitment LP assumes that the scenario bases $\{g_s\}$ are
          fixed parameters independent of $g^*$.
          This is valid because each scenario's solve maximizes spending given the
          return sequence, so $g_s$ does not depend on the committed level.
    \item Longevity draws use static life tables, conditioned on current age.
          Eight tables are selectable, here ordered by life expectancy at age~65
          (average of men and women): the SOA VBT~2015 Smoker table (82.3 years),
          the SSA~2025 period table (83.3, the general US population, and the default),
          the SOA Pub-2010 Public Safety (84.9) and General Employees (85.6) tables,
          the SOA RP-2014 Healthy Annuitant table (85.9), the VBT~2015 Non-Smoker
          table (86.7), the SOA IAM~2012 Individual Annuity table (86.7), and the
          SOA Pub-2010 Teachers table (87.1).  The spread matters: an annuitant or a non-smoker table carries a
          materially longer horizon than the population table, and occupation and
          smoking status are the two inputs that select among them.
          Cohort tables, which account for expected future mortality improvements,
          are not used.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{The Spending--Bequest Trade-off}
\label{Chap:SpendingBequest}

The two objectives of Chapter~\ref{Chap:Objectives} are the two ends of one question: how much
spending does each dollar left to heirs cost?
Maximizing spending under a bequest floor gives one point of the answer, and maximizing the
bequest under a spending level gives another.
Owl traces the whole curve between them by sweeping the bequest floor $\epsilon_{N_n}$ of
Eq.~(\ref{Eq:BequestFloor}) under the \code{maxSpending} objective.
The user-facing surfaces call the result the spending--bequest \emph{trade-off}, to keep it apart
from the spending--shortfall frontier of Chapter~\ref{chap:stochastic-spending}: both are Pareto
frontiers, but only that one is a risk--return curve in the sense of Markowitz.

\section{Why the bequest is swept and spending is not}
\label{sec:frontier-why-bequest}

The same curve could in principle be traced the other way, by pinning the spending level and
maximizing what is left.
Under \code{maxSpending}, pinning $g_0$ makes the objective constant over the feasible set,
and the bracket-fill variables $f_{tn}$, which are tight only because the objective pushes
income into the cheapest brackets, are then no longer priced: the solver may fill a top bracket
while lower ones sit empty (Section~\ref{sec:solver-options-objectives}; issue~\#140).
A bequest floor constrains the estate instead, so $g_0$ stays free, the objective keeps a nonzero
gradient, and every point of the sweep is a genuine optimum of an ordinary solve.
No new optimization structure is involved.

\section{Deterministic trade-off}
\label{sec:frontier-deterministic}

Given a grid of bequest levels $B_0 < B_1 < \cdots < B_{K-1}$ in today's dollars, sorted and
without duplicates, Owl solves the plan on its own rates at each level,
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:FrontierLevel}
	S(B_k) \;=\; \max\bigl\{\, g \;:\; \text{the plan is feasible with } \epsilon_{N_n} = B_k
	\text{ and spending basis } g \,\bigr\},
\end{equation}
where $g$ is the spending basis of Section~\ref{sec:spending-profile}, the average of the
yearly amounts; the first year's spending is $\xi_0 S(B_k)$.
A level that does not solve is recorded as unreachable.

\paragraph*{Exchange rate.}
The cost of the bequest is measured on the curve itself, one grid segment at a time, by the
secant
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:FrontierSecant}
	r_k \;=\; \frac{S(B_k) - S(B_{k-1})}{B_k - B_{k-1}},
	\qquad k = 1, \ldots, K-1,
\end{equation}
the change in spending basis per today's dollar of bequest (negative on a sloping curve),
and $\xi_0 r_k$ in first-year dollars.
One entry is reported for every segment, empty when either end did not solve, so that the
$k$-th entry always describes the segment ending at $B_k$.

\paragraph*{Shadow-price cross-check.}
On request, each level is also solved with duals, and the dual $\pi$ of the
\code{bequest_floor} row converts to the lifetime spending, in today's dollars, that one more
today's dollar of bequest costs, $\Pi_B = -\pi\,c_{\mathrm{obj}}\,\gamma_{N_n}$, where
$c_{\mathrm{obj}}$ converts the solver's objective into today's dollars of the reported objective.
Spread over the profile, it implies the basis slope
\begin{equation}
	\label{Eq:FrontierShadow}
	\hat{r}_k \;=\; -\Pi_B(B_k) \Big/ \sum_n \xi_n ,
\end{equation}
which is compared with the secant: the segment is flagged reliable when
$|\hat{r}_k - r_k| \le 0.2\,|r_k|$.
The dual belongs to the final LP with the self-consistent loop's parameters frozen, so it is the
partial derivative of a fixed point and runs shallow; a larger disagreement means the segment is
driven by the loop (taxable Social Security, IRMAA, gain fractions) rather than by the LP, and the
secant, not the dual, is the slope to read.
The dual is not computed in the stochastic modes below, where a single scenario's price says
nothing about the ensemble.

\paragraph*{Reach.}
A sweep can only bracket the largest estate a plan can leave: it lies at or above the highest
level that solved, and below the lowest level above it that did not.
Failures need not form a suffix of the grid, since a solve can time out at one level and succeed
above it, so the upper end is the lowest failure \emph{above} the highest success.
When every level solves, the bracket has no upper end and the highest level merely echoes the grid.

\paragraph*{What is not reported.}
A ``free bequest'', the largest floor that costs no spending, and a ``knee'' of the curve both read
as properties of the plan but are set by the grid: the first is zero for any curve that slopes at
all, and the second lands on the second grid point whenever the first two segments differ.
Resolving either needs a much finer sweep than a handful of levels, so neither is reported.

\section{Stochastic trade-off}
\label{sec:frontier-stochastic}

In the historical and Monte Carlo modes, every bequest level is solved across the whole scenario
ensemble of Chapter~\ref{chap:stochastic-spending}, giving the scenario bases $g_s(B_k)$, and the
commitment LP of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:CommitmentLP}) is solved over them for every $\Lambda$.
This turns the curve into a surface $S(B, p)$: spending as a function of the bequest target $B$ and
of the probability $p$ of not falling short.
For each success rate $p$ requested (by default 50\%, 75\%, and 90\%), the spending reported at
level $B_k$ is the committed spending $g^*$ at the least conservative $\Lambda$ whose shortfall
probability is at most $1 - p$.
Reading the surface down a column gives spending against bequest at fixed confidence, the fan
across $p$ being the sequence-of-returns risk; reading it across a row gives the spending--success
curve of Chapter~\ref{chap:stochastic-spending} at that bequest.
The exchange rate of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:FrontierSecant}) is then measured along the column of a chosen
success rate (90\% by default).

Three details keep the surface well behaved.
In Monte Carlo mode the rate generator is seeded once (seed 0 unless one is given), so every
bequest level meets the \emph{same} ensemble: without common random numbers, the surface wanders
non-monotonically in $B$ on sampling noise alone.
A scenario that cannot reach a level enters the commitment LP with basis zero, a full shortfall,
as in Chapter~\ref{chap:stochastic-spending}; only a level that fails outright is unreachable,
since individual scenarios failing at a high floor is exactly what the success rates express.
And Social Security claiming ages are held at the plan's values (Chapter~\ref{Chap:SSAges}),
as in every scenario analysis.

\section{What reaches heirs}
\label{sec:frontier-estate}

The bequest $B$ is the after-tax value of the savings accounts at the end of the plan, net of
remaining debt (Eq.~(\ref{Eq:BequestFloor})).
Two further amounts reach heirs and are reported beside it.
Fixed assets still held at the end of the plan are set by the asset table rather than by the floor,
so their value is the same at every level and is added on top of every bequest figure; Owl also
notes whether that today's-dollar value depends on the inflation path, which it does unless every
asset held to the end grows at a real rate quoted at the plan's start.
The partial bequest passing to heirs other than the spouse at the first death, net of their tax,
is solved rather than set, so it moves with the floor; it is reported per level, as the median and
range across scenarios in the stochastic modes.
The total estate at each level is the sum of the three.

\section{Implementation in Owl}

The sweep is \code{run_spending_bequest_frontier} in \code{stresstests.py}, exposed as
\code{Plan.runSpendingBequestFrontier}, and summarized by
\code{summarize_spending_bequest_frontier}, which computes the exchange rates, the reach bracket,
and the estate rows.
It is offered through the \emph{Spending vs Bequest} page of the user interface (default grid
\$0, \$500k, \$1M, \$2M, and \$4M), the \code{owlcli frontier} command, and the
\code{run_spending_bequest_frontier} tool of the assistant.
Each solve is limited to 120~seconds unless the options set \code{maxTime}, since a sweep multiplies
any pathological scenario by the number of levels.
Every spending figure is reported twice, as the basis the solver optimizes and as the first year's
amount, which is the quantity the \code{netSpending} option of \code{maxBequest} pins and
therefore the one to compare a \code{maxBequest} run against; the two differ by $\xi_0$ and
coincide on a flat profile.

\chapter{Optimization of Social Security Claiming Ages}
\label{Chap:SSAges}
\label{chap:ss-ages}

The age at which an individual claims Social Security retirement benefits is one of
the most consequential financial decisions in retirement planning.
Claiming early---as young as age~62---provides income sooner but at a permanently
reduced rate; deferring to age~70 maximizes the monthly benefit through delayed
retirement credits but foregoes years of payments.
The optimal age depends on individual longevity expectations, household income,
tax situation, and the presence of a spouse whose spousal and survivor benefits
are linked to both partners' claiming decisions.

In Owl's base operating mode, the user supplies a fixed claiming age for each
individual and the Social Security benefit stream $\bar{\zeta}_{in}$ is computed
as a deterministic parameter.
When the option \code{withSSAges} selects one or more individuals, Owl chooses
their claiming ages by \emph{solving the plan at each claiming month still open}
and keeping the ages that gave the best objective.
Each candidate is an ordinary fixed-age solve, in the case's own tax modes, so
the benefits of every candidate age are taxed exactly as that age would be taxed.
The claiming age is therefore not a variable of the optimization problem: it is
an outer search whose inner problem is the plan of
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Formulation}, unchanged.
Section~\ref{sec:ss-why-not-mip} records why the claiming age is not chosen
inside the MILP, which is how Owl once did it.

\section{The claiming-age grid}
\label{sec:ss-grid}

Social Security claiming ages are discretized to a monthly grid.
The ages open to individual $i$ run from eligibility, or from the current month
if that is later, through age~70 in whole months:
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:SSAopen}
    \mathcal{A}_i \;=\;
    \left\{ \frac{m}{12} \;:\;
        m = \max\!\left(m^\mathrm{elig}_i,\, m^\mathrm{today}_i\right), \ldots, 840
    \right\},
\end{equation}
where $m$ counts whole months of age, $840 = 70 \times 12$, and
$m^\mathrm{today}_i$ is the age in whole months that individual $i$ reaches in
the current calendar month.
A claiming month already behind us is excluded because SSA does not pay
retroactively for it, so the grid shrinks as a plan ages: it holds at most
97~points, for someone not yet~62, and fewer for anyone older.
Eligibility is $m^\mathrm{elig}_i = 744$ (62 years, 0 months) for an individual
born on the first or second day of a month, and $745$ (62 years, 1 month)
otherwise, following the SSA rule that a benefit cannot begin in the month of
attainment unless age~62 is attained on its first day.

An individual whose entered claiming age is \emph{already past} cannot be
searched, and Owl refuses the case rather than silently holding that age.
A past age reads as benefits already being collected, and a claim cannot be
redone: every month still open would pay more than the reduced benefit actually
received.
An individual with no PIA, or one not named by \code{withSSAges}, keeps the
entered age and contributes a fixed benefit stream as in the base mode.

\section{Claiming-age adjustment factors}
\label{sec:ss-factors}

The benefit multiplier $\Phi(\alpha, \mathrm{FRA})$ encodes the SSA rules for
early reduction and delayed credits.
It is applied not to the conventional claiming age $\alpha$ but to the
\emph{SSA age}
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:SSAage}
    \tilde{\alpha} \;=\; \min\!\left(\alpha + \tfrac{1}{12}\,
        \mathbb{1}[\text{born on the 1st}],\; 70\right),
\end{equation}
because an individual born on the first of a month attains each age on the last
day of the prior month and has therefore been that age one month longer
(POMS~RS~00615.015); being born on the second confers no such shift.
The cap at~70 is the end of delayed credits, which stop with the month before
age~70 is attained (42~U.S.C.~402(w)(2)(A); POMS~RS~00615.690), so an individual
born on the first earns no credit for a claim at a conventional age of~70.
With that substitution,
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:SelfFactor}
    \Phi(\alpha, \mathrm{FRA}) =
    \begin{cases}
        1 + 0.08\,(\tilde{\alpha} - \mathrm{FRA}), & \tilde{\alpha} \geq \mathrm{FRA}, \\[4pt]
        1 - \tfrac{1}{15}\,(\mathrm{FRA} - \tilde{\alpha}),
            & \mathrm{FRA} - 3 < \tilde{\alpha} < \mathrm{FRA}, \\[6pt]
        0.80 - \tfrac{1}{20}\,(\mathrm{FRA} - 3 - \tilde{\alpha}),
            & \tilde{\alpha} \leq \mathrm{FRA} - 3.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Delayed credits of 8\% per year apply above FRA, capping at
$f = 1.24$ at age~70 when FRA is~67.
For early claiming the reduction is $\tfrac{1}{15}$ per year (i.e., $5/9$ of~1\% per month)
for the first 3~years before FRA, and $\tfrac{1}{20}$ per year (i.e., $5/12$ of~1\% per month)
for any additional years, yielding a minimum of approximately 70\% of PIA at age~62.
The Full Retirement Age $\mathrm{FRA}_i$ is determined from the individual's
birth year as described in Chapter~\ref{Chap:TaxRules}.

A separate factor applies to the spousal benefit, which receives no delayed
credit beyond the spousal FRA and whose reduction before FRA is $25/36$ of~1\%
per month for the first 36~months.
Where benefits are withheld by the earnings test, the months withheld are
credited back at FRA through the adjusted reduction factor, which recomputes
$\Phi$ at an effective age advanced by the number of crediting months.

\section{The search over fixed-age solves}
\label{sec:ss-search}

Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of individuals whose ages are searched and
$V(\bm{\alpha})$ the objective value of the plan solved with claiming ages
$\bm{\alpha}$, in the case's own tax modes and with \code{withSSAges} set to
\code{"fixed"}.
A candidate that does not solve contributes no value and is skipped.

For a single searched individual the search is exhaustive: every age in
$\mathcal{A}_i$ is evaluated and the best is kept.
For two, the ages interact through the spousal and survivor benefits, and Owl
runs coordinate descent: each searched individual's age is swept over
$\mathcal{A}_i$ in turn while the other is held at its incumbent value, and the
sweeps repeat until a full round moves neither age, to a maximum of five rounds,
\begin{equation}
    \label{Eq:SSAcoord}
    \bm{\alpha}^{(k+1)}_i \;=\;
    \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}_i} \;
    V\!\left(a, \bm{\alpha}^{(k)}_{-i}\right),
    \qquad i \in \mathcal{F},
\end{equation}
starting from the ages entered in the case.
An age replaces the incumbent only on a strict improvement, with a relative
margin of $10^{-9}$, so ties keep the incumbent and the sweep terminates.

Because every candidate is a complete solve of the unmodified plan, the ages
