        A_e[J_0(i, j, n), q_b(i_d, j, n)] &=& - [\phi_j\delta_{n, n_d-1}\delta_{i, i_s}]
		(1 + \mathcal{T}_{i_djn}), \nonumber \\
        A_e[J_0(i, j, n), q_d(i_d, n)] &=&  -[\phi_j\delta_{n, n_d-1}\delta_{i, i_s}]
		\delta_{j, 0}(1 + \mathcal{T}_{i_djn}), \nonumber \\
        A_e[J_0(i, j, n), q_w(i_d, j, n)] &=& [\phi_j\delta_{n, n_d-1}\delta_{i, i_s}]
	(1 + \mathcal{T}_{i_djn}), \nonumber\\
        A_e[J_0(i, j, n), q_x(i_d, n)] &=& - [\phi_j\delta_{n, n_d-1}\delta_{i, i_s}]
                (\delta_{j, 2} - \delta_{j, 1})
		(1 + \mathcal{T}_{i_djn}), \nonumber \\
                \nonumber \\
        &&\qquad\forall i \in \{0,\ldots, N_i-1\},\nonumber\\
        &&\qquad\forall j \in \{0,\ldots, N_j-1\},\nonumber\\
        &&\qquad\forall n \in \{0,\ldots, N_n-1\}, \nonumber
\end{eqnarray}
where $v$ is
\begin{eqnarray}
	v[J_0(i, j, n)] 
	&=& [1 - \delta_{n, n_d-1}\delta_{i, i_d}] \kappa_{ijn}(1 + \mathcal{T}_{ijn}/2)
	\nonumber \\
	&& + [\phi_j\delta_{n, n_d-1}\delta_{i, i_s}]\kappa_{i_djn}(1 + \mathcal{T}_{i_djn}/2). 
\end{eqnarray}
While the last two equations may look cumbersome, their net effect is only to include
a few more terms when $n=n_d-1$. 
The QCD, opportunity-cost, and SPIA-premium terms of Eq.~(\ref{Eq:B1}), omitted here, carry
over with the same multipliers.

\paragraph*{Asset allocation ratios}
When asset allocation ratios $\alpha$ are imposed,
they should also be applied to how
contribution amounts $\kappa_{ijn}$ are invested, such that 
\begin{equation}
	\kappa_{ijkn} = \alpha_{ijkn} \kappa_{ijn}.
\end{equation}
For other allocation schemes, just substitute $\alpha_{ijkn} = \alpha_{ikn}$ or $\alpha_{kn}$
depending on the scheme selected.

Asset allocation has been handled easily by assuming that
the accounts are always rebalanced
and only using a single multiplier $\mathcal{T}$, defined as
\begin{equation}
	\mathcal{T}_{ijn} = \sum_k \alpha_{ijkn}\tau_{kn},
\end{equation}
to compute the return
on the total balance of each savings account.

\paragraph*{Spousal deposits and withdrawals}
While keeping the problem linear, a simple constraint can be imposed on
surplus deposits in taxable savings accounts: the spousal fraction $\eta$ splits each
surplus as in Eq.~(\ref{Eq:eta}), which for the two deposits reads
\begin{equation}
	\eta\, d_{0n} = (1 - \eta)\, d_{1n}.
\end{equation}
With \code{spousal_surplus_deposit_fraction = "optimize"}, the split is instead left to the
optimizer through $d_{0n} + d_{1n} = s_n$ while both spouses live (Eq.~(\ref{Eq:eta2})).
A similar spousal ratio could be imposed on withdrawals from tax-deferred accounts,
\begin{equation}
	\eta\, w_{01n} = (1 - \eta)\, w_{11n},
\end{equation}
but this can cause drawing from an empty account while the other spousal account is not.
Only the deposit scheme has been implemented in Owl.

\section{Constraint tags and the explanation engine}
\label{Sec:ConstraintTags}
Every row added to the constraint matrices carries a machine-readable
\emph{tag}: a tuple whose first element names the constraint \emph{family} and
whose remaining elements are the row's indices, e.g.\ \code{("rmd", i, n)} for
the RMD floor of individual $i$ in year $n$.
Tags are inert during optimization; they exist so that the solution can be
\emph{explained}.
When a plan is solved with \code{withDuals=True}, Owl re-solves the final
linear program with all binary variables fixed at their solved values and
extracts the row duals (shadow prices), row activities, and column reduced
costs.
The tags map each dual back to the constraint it prices, letting the
explanation engine (\code{owlplanner.assistant.explain}, surfaced through the
\code{explain_results} tool) answer questions such as ``what does the bequest
requirement cost in lifetime spending?''\ or ``which years do RMDs force
withdrawals?''\ directly from the mathematics of the optimization.

Each family is classified in one of three classes:
\begin{description}
	\item[Policy] User goals and legal rules (Table~\ref{Tab:TagsPolicy}).
	A binding row of this class is reported to the user together with the
	marginal value of relaxing it, expressed in today's dollars of the
	reported objective.
	\item[Structural] Accounting identities and definitions
	(Table~\ref{Tab:TagsStructural}).
	These rows are binding by construction; their duals are valuation
	information --- for instance, the duals of the cash-flow balances form the
	plan's endogenous discount curve --- and are used selectively by the
	explanation engine.
	\item[Artifact] Big-$\mathcal{M}$ linkings, at-most-one selectors,
	and convexification rows
	(Table~\ref{Tab:TagsArtifact}).
	These encode formulation technique rather than financial meaning and are
	excluded from user-facing output.
	Threshold (cliff) effects that the underlying binaries model --- IRMAA
	tiers, ACA brackets, Social Security taxability --- are instead reported as
	primal \emph{headroom} to the next threshold, since marginal prices cannot
	express discrete jumps.
\end{description}
The authoritative family-to-class table is
\code{CONSTRAINT_FAMILIES} in \code{owlplanner/assistant/explain.py}; it must
be kept in synchrony with the tags emitted in \code{plan.py} and with the
tables below.
An unregistered family is \emph{not} dropped: the lookup defaults to the policy
class, deliberately, so that a newly tagged constraint surfaces rather than
vanishes.
The cost of that default is that an artifact row left unregistered is reported as
though it priced a user goal; a test (\code{test_every_emitted_tag_is_registered})
therefore fails when \code{plan.py} emits a tag the registry lacks.
Year indices of the Medicare and ACA families are offsets from the first
Medicare- or ACA-eligible year, respectively.

Constraint rows are only half of the sensitivity picture: policy also enters
the model as \emph{bounds on decision variables}, priced by their reduced
costs rather than by row duals.
A companion registry (\code{COLUMN_FAMILIES}, same module) prices variables
sitting at a policy-meaningful bound: the \code{maxRothConversion} cap on
$x_{in}$, conversions pinned to zero by rule (\code{noRothConversions},
\code{startRothConversions}, the last-two-years rule), the
\code{minTaxableBalance} safety-net floor on
$b_{i0n}$, and the loop-mode LTCG bracket room bounds on $q_{0n}$ and
$q_{1n}$ of Eqs.~(\ref{Eq:LTCG2})--(\ref{Eq:LTCG3}).
At a binding upper bound, the value of one more dollar of allowance is the
reduced cost converted to reported-objective units; at a binding lower bound,
the same conversion prices each dollar of relaxation of the floor.
Structural bounds --- bracket widths $\bar{\Delta}_{tn}$, standard deductions,
big-$\mathcal{M}$ caps --- are deliberately not registered; bracket fill is
reported through the primal bracket analysis instead.
The resulting explanation payload is validated against a versioned Pydantic
schema (\code{PlanExplanation} in
\code{owlplanner/assistant/explain_schema.py}), which constitutes the
machine-readable contract consumed by AI assistants.

\code{niit_nii_cap} & $(n)$ & Eq.~(\ref{Eq:NIIT_cap}) \\
\code{senior_bonus_phaseout} & $(n)$ & Eq.~(\ref{Eq:SB2}) \\
\code{senior_bonus_cap} & $(n)$ & Eq.~(\ref{Eq:SB3}) \\
\code{irmaa_amo} & $(n)$ & Eq.~(\ref{Eq:SOS1}) \\
\code{irmaa_bracket_lb}, \code{irmaa_bracket_ub} & $(n,q)$ & Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MedDecomph}) \\
\code{aca_amo} & $(n)$ & Sec.~(\ref{Sec:ACA}) \\
\code{aca_bracket_lb}, \code{aca_bracket_ub} & $(n,r)$ & Sec.~(\ref{Sec:ACA}) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Artifact-class constraint tags: formulation machinery, excluded from
user-facing explanations. The withdrawal-ordering gates implement the
\texttt{withdrawalOrder="taxable\_first"} baseline policy of
Sec.~(\ref{Sec:WithdrawalOrdering}).}
\label{Tab:TagsArtifact}
\end{table}

\chapter{Solver Options}
\label{Chap:Options}

Every option below is a key of the dictionary passed to \code{plan.solve()} as
its second argument, or equivalently an entry of the \code{[solver_options]}
table of a case file.
An unrecognized key is reported and ignored rather than refused, so that a case
file written against a later version still loads.
Defaults are those of the engine; the user interface may present different
initial values.

\section{Objective and goals}

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[netSpending]
Required under \code{maxBequest}: the net annual spending to sustain, in the
units of \code{units}.  Ignored under \code{maxSpending}, where spending is
what is maximized.
\item[bequest]
Under \code{maxSpending}, the floor on the final bequest (default \$1).
Sweeping it traces the spending--bequest frontier of
Section~\ref{sec:bequest-objective}.  Ignored under \code{maxBequest}.
\item[partialBequestWeight]
The value of a dollar left to non-spouse heirs at the \emph{first} death, as a
fraction of a dollar of the objective, spending or final bequest (default
$\max(0.01, 2\,\textrm{gap})$).  Without it, that money is worth nothing to
either objective, and the solve is free to waste it.
\item[timePreference]
A subjective time-discount rate $\rho$, in \%/year (default~0), that front-loads
spending by weighting year $n$ with $(1+\rho)^{-n}$.
\item[spendingSlack]
Percent of slack allowed around the spending profile (default~0), which lets
yearly spending depart from $g\xi_n$ within a band.
\item[minTaxableBalance]
A per-person floor on the taxable balance, in \code{units}, from year~1 through
each horizon, inflation-adjusted (Eq.~(\ref{Eq:MinTaxBal})).  Absent, no floor
row is generated.
\item[oppCostX]
An opportunity cost charged on Roth conversions, in percent (default~0): the
fraction $1 - \textrm{oppCostX}/100$ of a converted dollar is what arrives.
\end{description}

\section{Roth conversion controls}

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[maxRothConversion]
The largest conversion allowed to each individual in any one year, in
\code{units}.  Absent, or negative, there is no cap.  Zero forbids conversions.
\item[noRothConversions]
The name of an individual whose conversions are pinned to zero.
\item[startRothConversions, stopRothConversions]
Calendar years before and after which conversions are pinned to zero.
\item[swapRothConverters]
For a couple, a signed calendar year before which only one spouse may convert:
positive lets the first individual convert first and the second from that year
on, negative the reverse.  It takes precedence over \code{noRothConversions},
which is dropped, with a warning, when both are given.
\end{description}

\section{Tax-breakpoint modes}

Each of the following selects how one nonlinear tax feature is handled:
\code{"loop"} prices it from the previous self-consistent iterate, while
\code{"optimize"} puts it in the mixed-integer program, as described in
Chapter~\ref{Chap:Formulation}.
A value that is none of the documented modes is refused rather than silently
solved as another.

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[withMedicare]
IRMAA bracket selection (default \code{"loop"}); \code{"none"} or \code{false}
drops Medicare entirely.
\item[withACA]
ACA marketplace premiums, default \code{"loop"}
(Section~\ref{Sec:ACA}).
\item[withLTCG]
Long-term capital-gains brackets, default \code{"loop"}
(Section~\ref{Sec:LTCG}).
\item[withNIIT]
Net investment income tax, default \code{"loop"}
(Section~\ref{Sec:NIIT}).
\item[withSSTaxability]
Taxable share of Social Security, default \code{"loop"}.  A number in $[0,1]$
pins the share instead of computing it.
\item[withSeniorBonus]
The OBBBA 65+ bonus phase-out, default \code{"loop"}
(Section~\ref{Sec:SeniorBonus}).
\item[breakpointMethod]
A preset, not a family: \code{"loop"} (default) changes nothing, while
\code{"branch-and-bound"} or \code{"local-search"} sets \emph{every} applicable
family above to \code{"optimize"} and \code{mipStrategy} to the same value.
Medicare is included unless it is off, and the ACA only when a benchmark premium
is set.  It overrides a pinned Social Security share, with a warning.
\item[withSSAges]
Social Security claiming ages: \code{"fixed"} (default) or \code{"none"} (no
search), \code{"optimize"} (every individual with a PIA), or a name or list of
names (Chapter~\ref{Chap:SSAges}).
\item[withdrawalOrder]
\code{"optimal"} (default) lets the optimizer choose the order, while
\code{"taxable_first"} imposes the conventional
taxable--tax-deferred--tax-free waterfall through gating binaries
(Section~\ref{Sec:WithdrawalOrdering}).
\end{description}

\section{Solver selection and effort}

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[solver]
\code{"default"} (the default), \code{"HiGHS"}, or \code{"MOSEK"}.  The
default selects MOSEK when it is installed and licensed, and HiGHS otherwise.
\item[gap]
Relative mixed-integer optimality gap (default $10^{-4}$).  When
\code{withMedicare="optimize"} and no gap is given, the default is loosened to
$3\times10^{-3}$, and ten times further when \code{maxRothConversion} is at most
\$15k, because those models are the hardest to close.
\item[maxTime]
Time limit per solve, in seconds (default~900).  Applied \emph{per
self-consistent iteration}, so the budget for one case is
\code{maxIter}~$\times$~\code{maxTime}.
\item[mipMaxNodes]
Branch-and-bound cap: nodes under HiGHS (default 1,000,000), branchings under
MOSEK (no cap by default).  The two engines count different things, which is
worth remembering when comparing them.
\item[numThreads]
Threads per MOSEK solve, 0 meaning all cores (default~0).  Setting it allows
matched parallelism, where several scenarios run at a few threads each instead of
one scenario at all of them.  It is a no-op under HiGHS, which solves serially
here.
\item[mipScaleOrder]
The money unit inside a mixed-integer solve, $10^{\textrm{order}}$ dollars
(default~2, i.e.\ hundreds of dollars; 0 is dollars).  Scaling the money keeps
the big-$\mathcal{M}$ coefficients and the balances within a range the solver
can condition.
\item[withDuals]
When true (default false), the final linear program is re-solved with every
binary fixed at its solved value, to extract the shadow prices the explanation
engine reads (Section~\ref{Sec:ConstraintTags}).
\end{description}

\section{Convergence of the self-consistent loop}

The loop stops when the objective has settled \emph{and} the quantities fed back
into the next iteration have stopped moving.  The first is judged by
\code{absTol} and \code{relTol} on the objective, the second by
\code{residualTol} on the fixed-point residual.

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[maxIter]
Maximum number of self-consistent iterations (default~29).
\item[absTol]
Absolute objective tolerance, in dollars (default~100).
\item[relTol]
Relative objective tolerance (default $\max(5\times10^{-5}, \textrm{gap}/300)$).
\item[residualTol]
The largest disagreement still allowed at convergence between a quantity the
linear program was built with and the value the resulting plan implies, in
today's dollars \emph{per year} (default~50).  Per year, not per plan: the
underlying measure sums over the horizon, so a flat figure would be three times
stricter on a 33-year plan than on an 11-year one.
\item[epsilon]
Lexicographic tie-breaking weight (default $5\times10^{-7}$), described in
Section~\ref{sec:lexicographic}.
\end{description}

\section{Local search}

These apply only when \code{mipStrategy="local-search"}
(Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[mipStrategy]
\code{"branch-and-bound"} (default) or \code{"local-search"}.
\item[localSearchTime]
Total time budget per solve, in seconds (default~300).
\item[localSearchStepTime]
Time cap per restricted solve, in seconds (default~60).
\item[localSearchStepNodes]
Node limit per restricted solve.  Unset, it takes the engine default, which is
3,000 nodes under HiGHS and 20,000 branchings under MOSEK --- a difference large
enough to confound a comparison of the two engines unless it is set explicitly.
\item[localSearchRadius]
The number of flips allowed on the Social-Security-taxability binaries inside the
local-branching ball (default~4).
\end{description}

\section{Inputs, reporting, and units}

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[previousMAGIs]
The two MAGIs preceding the plan, which the IRMAA two-year lookback needs for the
first two plan years.
\item[includeMedicarePartD]
False drops Part~D and its IRMAA surcharge from the Medicare costs
(default true).
\item[medicarePartDBasePremium]
The Part~D base premium, \$/month per person (default~0).  The Part~D IRMAA
surcharges apply regardless.
\item[noLateSurplus]
Pins the cash-flow surplus to zero in the final two plan years (default false).
\item[units]
The monetary units of the option values: \code{"k"} (the default), \code{"M"},
or \code{"1"}.
\item[verbose]
Enables progress logging for one solve (default false).
\end{description}

\section{Retired and removed options}

An option that no longer does anything is still \emph{accepted} and reported as
deprecated, because a case file saved earlier still carries it; one whose
behaviour was unsound is refused outright.
Both registries live in the source, \code{RETIRED_OPTIONS} in \code{plan.py}
and \code{REMOVED_OPTIONS} in \code{config/schema.py}, each entry paired with
the reason a reader would need.

\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=1.5cm, font=\ttfamily]
\item[amoConstraints, amoRoth, amoSurplus]
Retired.  The mutually exclusive transactions these selected are now restored
after the solve (Section~\ref{Sec:AMO}), so nothing needs constraining, and the
default plan is a pure linear program.
\item[bigMamo, bigMaca, bigMss, bigMltcg, bigMniit]
Retired.  Each big-$\mathcal{M}$ is now derived per year from what its own row
gates, so there is nothing left to tune; the flat constants these keys set were
hundreds to thousands of times too large.
\item[withDecomposition]
Retired.  Its only mode rounded all five bracket families at once from a single
relaxation, which is jointly infeasible even where each family is feasible
alone, so every solve fell back to the monolithic program it was meant to avoid.
\item[withSCLoop]
Retired.  No mode can solve without the self-consistent loop---the OBBBA
phase-out of the standard exemption and the cost-basis gain fractions are both
nonlinear in the solution---and turning it off left Medicare out of the budget,
overstating spending.  Use \code{maxIter} to shorten the loop instead.
\item[fixedSpending]
Removed, and refused.  Pinning $g_0$ under \code{maxSpending} made the objective
constant over the feasible set, after which the solver could fill the top tax
bracket while lower ones sat empty at no cost to the objective
(Section~\ref{sec:solver-options-objectives}).  Sweep the \code{bequest} floor
instead.
\end{description}

\chapter{Source File Reference}
\label{Chap:SourceRef}

The Owl Python package is located under \code{src/owlplanner/}.
The table below summarizes the primary source files and their roles.

\begin{sloppypar}
\begin{description}[style=nextline, leftmargin=2.5cm, font=\ttfamily]

\item[plan.py]
The core optimization engine.
Constructs and solves the LP/MIP using HiGHS (via \code{highspy}) or MOSEK directly,
MOSEK being selected by default when it is licensed.
Implements the self-consistent iteration loop, all constraint-building methods,
the objective functions, and post-solution analysis.
Delegates stress-testing and stochastic spending methods to \code{stresstests.py} via mixin.
This is the largest and most complex file.

\item[varmap.py]
Variable block indexing and extraction for the LP solver.
\code{VarBlock} records the start offset and shape of one decision-variable family
(e.g., $b$, $d$, $e$, $f$, $g$, $h$, $m$, $s$, $w$, $x$, $z^m$, \ldots);
\code{VarMap} accumulates them in declaration order and exposes a cursor tracking the
running offset.
Used by \code{plan.py} to build and query all LP/MIP variable indices without
hard-coded offset arithmetic.

\item[abcapi.py]
A solver-neutral constraint-building API used by \code{plan.py}.
Provides an abstract interface for adding rows to the constraint matrix,
keeping the model description independent of any specific LP solver.

\item[stresstests.py]
Stress-testing and stochastic spending optimization methods.
Provides \code{run_historical_range}, \code{run_mc}, and \code{run_stochastic_spending},
which take a \code{Plan} instance and run the optimizer across many return scenarios
using a thread pool.
Also implements the efficient-frontier LP, CVaR computation, and the Retirement
Efficiency Score (RES).
\code{Plan} exposes these as methods by delegation (mixin pattern).

\item[tax\_federal.py]
Federal tax calculations: ordinary income brackets and standard deductions,
LTCG tax thresholds, SS taxability formula, NIIT, ACA Premium Tax Credit
(with \texttt{acaCosts} and \texttt{acaVals}), and Medicare IRMAA premiums and bracket thresholds.
Also computes RMD fractions from the IRS Uniform Lifetime Table.
Implements the OBBBA (2025) tax schedule, the 65+ bonus deduction phaseout,
and the future-law reversion scenario (\code{yOBBBA} parameter).

\item[tax\_state.py]
State income tax parameters for all 50 states and the District of Columbia.
Loads per-state, per-filing-status bracket structures, standard deductions,
Social Security treatment, and retirement-income and pension exemption caps
from \code{data/taxes_state.toml}, and converts them into the
inflation-indexed LP parameter arrays used by the state-tax constraints
(Sec.~(\ref{Sec:StateTax})).

\item[socialsecurity.py]
Social Security benefit modeling: FRA schedules, own-benefit and spousal
adjustment factors, spousal and survivor benefit computation, and optional
benefit trim for policy scenarios.
Also implements the SSA earnings test --- exempt amounts, countable earnings,
grace year, months withheld, and the adjusted reduction factor that credits them
back at FRA --- and the survivor claiming window.
The claiming-age search of Chapter~\ref{Chap:SSAges} calls into this module once
per candidate age rather than precomputing a benefit table.

\item[hfp\_io.py]
Household Financial Profile (HFP) workbook I/O.
Reads and validates the Wages and Contributions sheet (one tab per individual)
and the Fixed Assets and Debts tables, owns the person-sheet column list and the
header validation, and returns the year-indexed tables consumed by
\code{plan.py}.

\item[localsearch.py]
The local search of Sec.~(\ref{sec:decomposition}), used when
\code{mipStrategy="local-search"}.
Seeds a solution, then alternates restricted mixed-integer solves --- one family
of binaries freed at a time, and a local-branching ball around the
Social-Security-taxability binaries --- under per-step time and node caps.

\item[amorepair.py]
Post-processing that repairs the mutually exclusive transactions once enforced by
at-most-one binaries (Sec.~(\ref{Sec:AMO})): a surplus deposit made in the same
year as a taxable or tax-free withdrawal, and a Roth conversion made in the same
year as a tax-free withdrawal.
Replacing those binaries with this pass made the default plan a pure LP.

\item[pension.py]
Pension income: per-person schedules, indexation, start ages, and the
joint-and-survivor reduction.

\item[spending.py]
The spending profile $\Lambda_n$ --- flat, smile, or a user-supplied series ---
and the subjective time-discount front-loading of \code{timePreference}.

\item[fixedassets.py]
Models fixed-income assets (real estate, annuities, collectibles, etc.),
computing year-by-year income, proceeds, and capital gains arising from
assets listed in the Fixed Assets table.

\item[debts.py]
Models debt obligations (loans, mortgages), computing annual payment
schedules from the Debts table.

\item[export.py]
Workbook and worksheet output: the year-indexed tables, the Summary, and the
real- or nominal-dollar views written by the UI and by \code{owlcli run}.

\item[utils.py]
Shared helpers, including the canonicalization of the mode-valued solver options
(\code{normalize_mode_options}), which refuses a value that is none of the
documented modes rather than silently solving another.

\item[progress.py]
Progress reporting for the long sweeps.

\item[mylogging.py]
The thread-aware logger, whose \code{tag} values map onto log levels.

\item[rates.py]
Loads and pre-processes the historical market-return data from
\code{data/rates.csv} (equity, bond, and inflation series from~1928
to present) and provides utilities for constructing rate scenarios.

\item[rate\_models/]
A pluggable system of rate model classes inheriting from \code{BaseRateModel}.
The constant and deterministic methods (\code{trailing_30}, \code{optimistic},
\code{conservative}, \code{user}, \code{historical},
\code{historical_average}) and the parametric ones (\code{gaussian},
\code{lognormal}, \code{historical_gaussian}, \code{historical_lognormal})
live in \code{builtin.py}; each remaining method has its own module:
\code{historical_bootstrap.py}, \code{vector_ar.py}, \code{garch_dcc.py},
\code{gmm.py}, \code{hmm.py}, \code{copula.py} (\code{historical_copula}),
and \code{dataframe.py}.
Shared machinery sits in \code{base.py} (the \code{BaseRateModel} interface),
\code{_sampling.py}, \code{inflation_transform.py} (the skewness correction of
Sec.~(\ref{Sec:InflationSkew})), \code{constants.py}, and \code{loader.py},
which discovers models at runtime.
Custom models are discoverable if they subclass \code{BaseRateModel} and are
importable.

\item[config/schema.py]
Pydantic~v2 models defining the full case configuration schema.
All plan parameters (account balances, claiming ages, rate method, options,
etc.) are validated here before being passed to \code{plan.py}.

\item[config/toml\_io.py]
TOML file loading and saving for \code{.toml} case files.

\item[config/legacy.py]
Backward compatibility for older case-file formats.

\item[config/plan\_bridge.py, config/ui\_bridge.py]
Bidirectional bridges between the validated configuration and, respectively, a
\code{Plan} object and the Streamlit session state.
\code{config/defaults.py} and \code{config/constants.py} hold the default values
and the shared key names.

\item[data/]
Tabulated data compiled into the package: the IRS Publication~590-B life
expectancy tables (\code{irs_590b.py} for the Uniform Lifetime and Joint tables,
\code{irs_590b_table_ii.py}), the CMS age-rating curve used to price a single
enrollee from a couple's benchmark (\code{aca_age_rating.py}), the eight
actuarial mortality tables of Sec.~(\ref{sec:longevity})
(\code{mortality_tables.py}), the per-state tax parameters
(\code{taxes_state.toml}), and the historical return series
(\code{rates.csv}).

\item[plotting/]
Visualization backends: \code{plotly_backend.py} for interactive plots
in the Streamlit UI, and \code{matplotlib_backend.py} for static plots
used in the CLI and Jupyter notebooks.

\item[cli/]
A Click-based command-line interface registered as the \code{owlcli}
entry point.
Supports \code{list} (enumerate available case files) and \code{run}
(solve a case from a TOML file) commands.

\item[assistant/]
AI-assistant integration layer shared by the MCP server and the chat page.
\code{tools.py} implements the MCP tool functions (case solving, comparison,
stochastic frontiers, \code{explain_results}, \ldots);
\code{explain.py} classifies the constraint tags
(Sec.~(\ref{Sec:ConstraintTags})) and turns a solved plan's shadow prices and
primal solution into a structured, JSON-ready explanation;
\code{explain_schema.py} holds the versioned Pydantic schema that validates the
explanation payload; and \code{intake.py} provides the guided intake prompt and
resources.

\end{description}
\end{sloppypar}
